# Sūrah Yūsuf (Chapter 12) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:1

> Alif-Lãm-Ra. These are the verses of the clear Book.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Letters Pronounced One at a Time**

Alif, lām, rāʾ are recited separately, never blended into a word, and they signify nothing in the ordinary lexicon of Arabic. That is the first fact about them, and it decides how everything after them should be heard. Twenty-nine chapters of the Qur'an open with such letter-groups, and across all of them only fourteen letters of the twenty-eight ever appear — exactly half the alphabet, arranged in combinations of one to five, producing no name, no verb and no sentence. A Meccan hearer caught those three sounds and knew at once that he was hearing the raw material of his own speech with its ordinary job removed. The letters belong to you; the arrangement does not. Before a single proposition is stated, the opening has made a claim about origin. This book is built from components every Arab possesses in equal measure, and no Arab has built anything resembling it.

**A Signature Shared by Six Neighbouring Chapters**

The identical group stands at the head of the chapters immediately around this one. Yūnus, Hūd, Yūsuf, Ibrāhīm and al-Ḥijr each begin *Alif-Lãm-Ra*, and between the third and fourth sits al-Raʿd with the lengthened form *Alif-Lãm-Mĩm-Ra* (10:1; 11:1; 13:1; 14:1; 15:1). Six consecutive chapters, one signature. The clustering is not ornamental. In all but three of the twenty-nine openings the letter-group is followed almost at once by a mention of revelation, and this block is the densest instance of that pattern in the book: letters, then the Book, then what the Book exists to do. Yūsuf contributes a third element its neighbours lack, namely a narrative running nearly a hundred verses without interruption. The signature announces a book; the chapter delivers a story; the third verse resolves the tension by declaring that the story is itself a mode of revelation.

**"Clear" Describes What the Book Does**

Al-kitāb al-mubīn is an active description rather than a passive one. Mubīn names a thing that makes plain, that sets two matters apart until each can be seen for what it is. Applied to scripture it says the Book does not merely contain clarity; it manufactures it — in law, in creed, in the difference between a prophet and an impostor, between a genuine dream and a fever. The same phrase opens two other chapters word for word, *“These are the verses of the clear Book”* (26:2; 28:2), and it is invoked as an oath in a third, *“By the clear Book!”* (44:2). Al-Ḥijr fuses the two descriptions this chapter keeps apart: *“These are the verses of the Book; the clear Quran”* (15:1). The insistence matters because what follows is a story saturated with concealment — a dream withheld, a plot hidden, an identity disguised, a providence operating unseen — and it is introduced by a sentence asserting that the book holding it is the instrument of disclosure. Clarity of this kind is a property of outcomes rather than of surfaces. Nothing in the plot of Joseph is transparent while it is happening; the shirt, the well, the prison and the famine all look like disasters at the time, and each is later shown to have been a step. The adjective promises that this reversal of appearance will be made intelligible, which is a larger claim than promising that the sentences will be easy.

**This Recitation, or the Tablet Behind It**

Two readings of the definite article have competed from the earliest commentaries onward. The first takes *the Book* to be the Qur'an now being recited. The second takes it to be the Preserved Tablet, the register from which revelation descends, elsewhere described as a glorious Qur'an *“˹recorded˺ in a Preserved Tablet”* (85:22). Neither reading is frivolous. The second accounts for the strange double status of a text spoken aloud in a Meccan marketplace and simultaneously stored beyond the world. The first is nevertheless the stronger in this place, for the very next verse speaks of sending down and of Arabic, and both are operations performed upon this Qur'an rather than upon the Tablet; the plural *āyāt*, verses, likewise fits a recited text with divisions and pauses. What both readings agree on is the decisive point: the clarity being asserted is not a human accomplishment, and it is not on offer for negotiation.

**A Meccan Chapter Arriving at Its Darkest Hour**

Yūsuf belongs to the Meccan period, and the reports place it toward the end of that period, after the deaths of Khadījah and Abū Ṭālib — the year the biographers call the Year of Sorrow — which falls around the tenth or eleventh year of the prophetic mission, some two or three years before the emigration to Medina. No report fixes the date with certainty, and the surviving accounts differ about the immediate occasion. The fit between setting and content is nonetheless too close to dismiss. A prophet rebuffed and abused by his own clan is given the account of a prophet thrown into a well by his own brothers. The opening line, which reads like a formal preamble, functions in that setting as reassurance: what is about to be recited is not a diversion but the verses of a book that makes things plain. Clarity is precisely what a besieged community most requires and least expects to receive.

**The Task the Two Clauses Assign**

Everything the chapter goes on to do is licensed by this verse. The letters establish that the material is common property while the product is not. The description establishes that the product discloses instead of obscuring. Taken together they set a rule of reading for the ninety-eight verses that follow: the story is evidence for a claim about the Book, not a tale told for its own sake. Every reversal in the plot — the favourite cast into the pit, the slave placed over the treasury, the prisoner summoned to interpret a king's sleep — is a demonstration of what *mubīn* means in practice. Things are not as they appear, and the Book is the instrument by which they become apparent. A reader who accepts the opening will therefore expect the narrative to end in exposure and clarification rather than in mere survival. That expectation is met at the close, when the dream is explained aloud in a throne room and the brothers finally recognise the man they threw away. The letters and the adjective are the contract; the plot is the performance of it.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:2

> Indeed, We have sent it down as an Arabic Quran so that you may understand.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Sent Down in a Chosen Tongue**

The verb is *anzalnā*, We have sent down, and it carries a direction: the movement is from above to here. What descends does not arrive as meaning alone, waiting to be clothed in whatever words are handy; it arrives already wearing Arabic. The phrase *Qurʾānan ʿarabiyyan* pins the language to the revelation as a property of the sending, not as an accident of the messenger's biography. Arabic is not the container into which a prior content was poured. It is the form the descent took. The distinction is not academic, because it determines what counts as the Qur'an afterwards: the wording is revelation, and a rendering into another language reports the meaning of that wording without becoming it. That is why the whole tradition of translation in Islam runs alongside an unbroken insistence that the recited text is the Arabic one. A translation is a report about the Qur'an, useful and often necessary, but the sentence *We have sent it down as an Arabic Quran* is the reason the report can never fully take the place of what it reports. Even the word *Qurʾān* is a verbal noun from *qaraʾa*, to read aloud, so the name of the book already describes an act performed in a specific language by a specific mouth.

**Understanding Named as the Purpose**

The clause *liʿallakum taʿqilūn* turns the sentence from a statement about God into a demand on the hearer. The verb derives from *ʿaql*, the faculty that ties things together, the same root that gives the word for a hobble binding a camel's knee — an intellect that restrains, gathers, holds fast. Comprehension is therefore not described as a passive reception but as an act of binding what has been given. Revelation descends; understanding must be performed. The Qur'an repeatedly refuses to treat hearing as sufficient, and here the stated aim of the whole enterprise is that the audience should grasp it. That aim is the reason the following verses launch into narrative at all. A story is the form of discourse that most readily engages the faculty of tying events together, and a chapter whose declared purpose is understanding will choose the form best suited to producing it.

**A Formula the Book Uses Again and Again**

This is not an isolated remark. *“And so We have sent it down as an Arabic Quran and varied the warnings in it”* (20:113); *“˹It is˺ a Quran ˹revealed˺ in Arabic without any crookedness”* (39:28); *“˹It is˺ a Book whose verses are perfectly explained—a Quran in Arabic for people who know”* (41:3); *“And so We have revealed to you a Quran in Arabic, so you may warn the Mother of Cities and everyone around it”* (42:7); *“Certainly, We have made it a Quran in Arabic so perhaps you will understand”* (43:3). Five other passages, three of them ending on the same purpose as this one. Al-Shuʿarāʾ adds the channel: the trustworthy spirit brought it down *“into your heart ˹O Prophet˺—so that you may be one of the warners—in a clear Arabic tongue”* (26:193–195). The recurrence shows a settled policy rather than a passing comment, and it shows that the policy is directed at comprehension rather than at ethnic pride.

**The Messenger Speaks the Language of His People**

The general rule sits behind the particular case: *“We have not sent a messenger except in the language of his people to clarify ˹the message˺ for them”* (14:4). A revelation delivered in a foreign tongue to a first audience would have failed its own purpose, since clarification requires shared vocabulary, shared idiom, shared expectations about what a sentence can do. Arabic here is an act of accommodation to hearers, not a claim of superiority for them. The Qur'an itself answers the suspicion that its eloquence was borrowed: *“And We surely know that they say, ‘No one is teaching him except a human.’ But the man they refer to speaks a foreign tongue, whereas this ˹Quran˺ is ˹in˺ eloquent Arabic”* (16:103). The alleged teacher could not have produced it, and the argument turns on the very Arabicness the verse affirms. Language is thus both the gift and the evidence.

**The Objection That Would Have Followed Otherwise**

The Book anticipates the counterfactual and answers it before it is raised. *“Had We revealed it as a non-Arabic Quran, they would have certainly argued, ‘If only its verses were made clear ˹in our language˺. What! A nonArabic revelation for an Arab audience!’”* (41:44). The same verse then supplies the verdict on those who reject it in either form: for the believer it is guidance and healing, while for others it remains sealed. The rhetorical trap is worth seeing plainly. Whatever the language, the objector finds a reason. When the tongue is unfamiliar the complaint is obscurity; when it is the hearer's own, the complaint is that there is nothing extraordinary about it. That is why the purpose clause in this verse locates the outcome in *taʿqilūn* — the work of the mind — rather than in the properties of the words. Clarity is supplied; the binding is left to the one who hears.

**Clarity Claimed Twice in Two Lines**

Verse 1 called the Book clear, and verse 2 explains the medium of that clarity. Placed side by side they make a single argument: the disclosure is real, and it is addressed in the hearer's own idiom. No excuse of unintelligibility survives the pairing. Yet the chapter that follows is full of people who fail to understand what is directly in front of them — brothers who misread a dream, a father whose sons lie to him in his own language, a woman who mistakes refusal for coyness, a prison full of men who cannot interpret their own nights. Clarity in the Book does not guarantee clarity in the reader. The Arabic is straight, as 39:28 insists, without crookedness; the crookedness, when it appears, belongs to the one listening. This is the pivot on which the whole sūrah turns, and it is set here, before a single character enters, so that the story is heard as a test of attention rather than as a chronicle of other people's fortunes. The word *laʿalla*, so that or perhaps, keeps the outcome open. Revelation is sent for understanding, and understanding remains something a person either does or declines to do. Nothing in the verse coerces the second step, which is exactly the arrangement the rest of the chapter will illustrate through a family that hears clearly and grasps nothing.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:3

> We relate to you ˹O Prophet˺ the best of stories through Our revelation of this Quran, though before this you were totally unaware ˹of them˺.

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Superlative Attached to One Narrative**

The words are *aḥsan al-qaṣaṣ*: the best of stories, not a good story, not an instructive one. The superlative is explicit and it is attached to a single narrative among all the narratives revelation could have chosen. Elsewhere the same construction is applied to the Book as a whole — *“˹It is˺ Allah ˹Who˺ has sent down the best message—a Book of perfect consistency and repeated lessons”* (39:23) — so the Qur'an is comfortable ranking its own discourse. The ranking here is not aesthetic in the modern sense of pleasant. *Ḥusn* in this usage means the story does its work completely: nothing in it is idle, nothing is missing, and every element returns later to complete another. A narrative earns the superlative by the tightness with which its parts interlock, and by the range of human situations it manages to hold inside one arc without breaking.

**One Unbroken Thread Where the Others Are Episodic**

The structural reason is visible on the page. Stories of Moses appear in many chapters, retold from different angles, each telling a fragment; Abraham, Noah, Lot and Jonah recur in pieces. Joseph's account is told once, from the dream in verse 4 to its fulfilment in verse 100, without repetition and without a rival version elsewhere in the Book. Nearly a hundred consecutive verses carry a single plot with a beginning, a middle and a resolved end, and no other chapter offers anything comparable. The consequence for the reader is unusual: suspense is possible. Because the story is not being restated, details introduced early — a shirt, a well, a measured supply of grain — can be stored and paid out much later, and the audience discovers their meaning only when the plot requires it.

**Why the Content Deserves the Word "Best"**

The range explains the rest of the claim. Inside one narrative sit the unity of God and the nature of prophethood; a father's partiality and a brother's envy; dream interpretation treated as a learned skill rather than a superstition; seduction, false accusation and imprisonment; court politics, granary administration and a seven-year famine; forgiveness granted by the injured party to the injurers; and the reunion of a family scattered across two countries. Creed and statecraft, the inner life and the market, all run through the same set of characters. No other chapter gathers so much of religious and worldly experience into a single continuous account, which is why the tradition has read it as a manual as much as a story: patience under trial in one half, gratitude under power in the other.

**The Ignorance Clause Is the Argument**

The second half of the verse does the evidential work. *Though before this you were totally unaware* is not a biographical aside; it is a proof offered to an audience that could check it. The Qur'an uses the move repeatedly and in the same terms. *“This is one of the stories of the unseen, which we reveal to you ˹O Prophet˺. Neither you nor your people knew it before this. So be patient! Surely the ultimate outcome belongs ˹only˺ to the righteous”* (11:49). *“This is news of the unseen that We reveal to you ˹O Prophet˺. You were not with them when they cast lots to decide who would be Mary’s guardian”* (3:44). *“You were not there ˹O Prophet˺ on the western side of the mountain when We entrusted the Commandments to Moses”* (28:44). Each time the claim is that the teller had no access. A man who never studied scripture, living in a city with no rabbinical academy, recites a Hebrew patriarch's biography with details unknown to his own people. The unawareness is the ground of the miracle, not a footnote to it. Mecca in this period had no synagogue capable of supplying such a narrative and no library at all; the Prophet could not read the scriptures of the People of the Book even had they been present. The verse asks the audience to weigh that fact against the fluency of what they are hearing. It is the same argument the chapter presses again in its last verse, where the message is called a confirmation of earlier revelation and not a fabrication (12:111).

**Two Reported Occasions, and What They Have in Common**

The commentators transmit two accounts of why this narrative was sent down. One holds that learned Jews in Mecca persuaded the Quraysh to test the Prophet by demanding a story about Joseph, on the reasoning that only a genuine prophet could supply it; the other holds that the Companions asked him to tell them of those who suffered before them, so that their own endurance might be strengthened. Neither report can be traced to a chain of the first rank [UNVERIFIED: both occasions of revelation are transmitted in the tafsīr literature without a graded chain; presented here as reported, not as established], and the two are not mutually exclusive — a challenge from outside and a need from inside could both have been met by one revelation. What they share is more interesting than which is correct. In both versions the story answers a question that had been put, and in both the answer is meant to settle something: for the challengers, whether this man knows what he cannot have learned; for the challenged, whether suffering has a shape that ends.

**Consolation Written Into the Form**

The setting supplies the third reason for the superlative. This chapter is placed by the reports in the years after Khadījah and Abū Ṭālib died, when the Prophet stood unprotected in a city that had turned violent toward him, and shortly after the journey to Ṭāʾif ended in stones. Into that moment comes the longest continuous narrative in the book, and its subject is a man betrayed by his own family, sold, slandered, imprisoned on evidence everyone knew was false, and finally raised to authority over the people who destroyed him. The form of consolation is deliberate. Not a command, not a list of promises, but a story — the shape in which a grieving mind can hold hope without having to argue for it. Calling it the best of stories is therefore a claim about function as well as craft: this is the account best fitted to do what the moment required. It also explains why the chapter keeps returning to address the Prophet directly between episodes, and why the closing verses draw the lesson out loud instead of leaving it implied. A consolation that must be inferred is not consolation. The story is handed over complete, ranked, and pointed at the one who most needed it.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:4

> ˹Remember˺ when Joseph said to his father, “O my dear father! Indeed I dreamt of eleven stars, and the sun, and the moon—I saw them prostrating to me!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Four Prophets in One Line of Descent**

The boy who speaks is Joseph, and the genealogy standing behind him is one of the most distinguished in the Book. He is the son of Jacob, the son of Isaac, the son of Abraham, and all four are prophets. When the Qur'an lists the line of guided men granted to Abraham, it names Joseph inside it: *“And We blessed him with Isaac and Jacob. We guided them all as We previously guided Noah and those among his descendants: David, Solomon, Job, Joseph, Moses, and Aaron”* (6:84). Ibn ʿUmar reports the Prophet ﷺ saying, “The nobleman, son of the nobleman, son of the nobleman, son of the nobleman — that was Joseph, son of Jacob, son of Isaac, son of Abraham” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 3202; the same wording appears in al-Adab al-Mufrad 896). No other figure in the Qur'an is described with four consecutive prophetic generations behind him. That pedigree explains the confidence of the scene: this is not an orphan's fancy but an heir to a house in which dreams have always been a form of knowledge.

**The Address of a Son Who Is Loved**

Joseph says *yā abati*, rendered here *O my dear father*, a vocative form with an extra letter attached that carries affection and nearness. His brothers use a different word for the same man. Their opening appeal is *“O our father!”* (12:11), and their later reports begin *“Our father!”* (12:17). The possessive plural is a claim on shared property; the singular endearment form is a relation. The distinction is small in Arabic and large in effect, and the chapter keeps it consistently. Every sentence Joseph addresses to Jacob in this sūrah uses the intimate form, including the final one spoken from a throne decades later (12:100), while the brothers never once use it. A reader who notices the grammar hears the family's real structure before any of them state it.

**Eleven Stars, a Sun and a Moon**

The count is exact and it is the reason the dream can be checked. Eleven stars correspond to eleven brothers, the sun and the moon to the two parents; some commentators reverse the last pair, assigning the sun to the mother and the moon to the father, on the grounds that the sun's light is borrowed by nothing while the moon's is received, and Jacob's authority in the household was exercised through a gentler presence. The disagreement is minor and both readings survive the story. What neither reading can avoid is the arithmetic: Joseph is the twelfth son, and the eleven lights in the sky are the eleven who will one day stand before him. When the fulfilment arrives the verse reports it in the same numbers — parents raised to the throne, the family prostrating — and the reader can verify the dream against the event without any explanation being supplied (12:100).

**A Prophet's Dream Is a Channel of Revelation**

Islamic tradition treats the true dream as a genuine mode of knowledge and not as a private curiosity. ʿĀʾishah reports that the first form in which revelation came to the Prophet ﷺ was the true dream, which arrived like the breaking of daylight (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 3), and Anas reports him saying that the good dream of a righteous man is one of forty-six parts of prophethood (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 6983). The commentators differ about Joseph's standing at this moment. Some hold that the dream itself is already revelation and that he is therefore a prophet from childhood. Others place the beginning of his mission later, at the inspiration given to him in the well (12:15), and read this vision as a portent of a prophetic function not yet conferred — precisely the arrangement found in the life of the Prophet Muhammad, whose true dreams preceded the descent at Ḥirāʾ. The second position is the stronger, since the Qur'an nowhere else grants prophethood to a child and it does mark verse 15 with the explicit language of revelation.

**Prostration Before a Creature**

The gesture in the dream is the difficulty the exegetes work hardest on. Prostration to another created being belongs to earlier dispensations: the angels were commanded to prostrate before Adam and did so (2:34), and the practice remained lawful in the law of the Children of Israel, which is how the family's bowing in verse 100 can be reported without censure. Islamic law closed the door, and the jurists read the earlier cases as belonging to those laws rather than as permissions for this one. Jacob's own reading of the vision, as transmitted by the commentators, turned on the same unease: a created being does not prostrate to another created being except where the one receiving the honour is destined for a severe trial, as Adam was honoured by the angels and expelled from the garden shortly afterwards. Al-Rāzī offers a softer alternative, taking the prostration to signify that the family would eventually come under Joseph's care and provision. Both readings agree that the honour in the dream is inseparable from the suffering that precedes it.

**The Vision Sets the Plot in Motion**

Nothing happens in this chapter before this sentence. The brothers' hatred already existed, as verse 8 makes plain, but it had no object until the dream gave it one. A vision of celestial bodies bowing to the youngest son converts ordinary parental preference into a threat of permanent subordination, and the family's ordinary resentments acquire a shape. That is why the father's first reaction is not interpretation but concealment, and why the narrative places the dream at the very front of the story rather than in a flashback. Everything that follows — the well, the caravan, the palace, the prison, the famine — is the working out of eleven stars and two greater lights performing an act of submission, and the reader is given the ending before being given the middle.

The next verse reports the father's answer to what he has just heard.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:5

> He replied, “O my dear son! Do not relate your vision to your brothers, or they will devise a plot against you. Surely Satan is a sworn enemy to humankind.

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Father Who Believes the Vision and Fears the Audience**

Jacob's answer contains no doubt about the dream. He does not ask what the stars mean, does not suggest the boy has eaten badly or slept in the wrong position, and does not treat the report as childish invention. His response moves straight to disclosure, which means he has already accepted the content. That acceptance is what makes the warning severe. A man who disbelieved a vision would have no reason to conceal it; a man who believes it must calculate who can be trusted with it. Jacob performs the calculation and arrives at an answer that no father wants to reach: the danger is inside the house. The verse therefore records a moment of moral clarity rather than of comfort. He sees the blessing, sees the envy it will attract, and understands that between the two stands nothing but silence.

**Conceal a Blessing From Those Who Will Envy It**

The practical rule Jacob applies became a standing instruction in the tradition. A good vision is not to be broadcast; it is told to those who wish the dreamer well and withheld from those who do not. The distinction underlying that discipline is prophetic: Abū Qatādah reports the Messenger of Allah ﷺ saying that the good vision is from God and the distressing dream from the devil, and that one who sees the second should spit dryly to his left three times and seek refuge from its evil, after which it will not harm him (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 2261a). The same collection preserves his instruction that a liked vision be disclosed only to one whom the dreamer loves. [UNVERIFIED: the exact Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim number for the "disclose only to one you love" wording could not be confirmed; the collection and narrator are established, the number is not.] The reason is not superstition but social physics. A blessing announced to a rival becomes a target. The prohibition on envy is itself among the most firmly attested commands in the collections: Anas reports the Prophet ﷺ saying, “Do not hate each other, do not envy each other, do not turn away from each other, but rather be servants of Allah as brothers” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 6065; Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 2559). Envy is treated there as a rupture of the community's basic bond, not as a private mood. Jacob's counsel is the defensive half of that same teaching.

**The Motive Named, Then Its Author Named**

The structure of the reply is worth isolating. First comes the prohibition: do not relate your vision to your brothers. Then the consequence: they will devise a plot against you. Then the ground of the consequence: Satan is a sworn enemy to humankind. The third clause is not decoration. It relocates the threat from ten jealous young men to an adversary older and more patient than any of them, and it does so without excusing them. The brothers remain responsible; they are simply not the origin. The Qur'an states the same arrangement as a command to the reader: *“Surely Satan is an enemy to you, so take him as an enemy. He only invites his followers to become inmates of the Blaze”* (35:6). Take him as an enemy means treat the enmity as declared rather than suspected, which is exactly what Jacob is teaching his son in a single sentence.

**Yakīdū: Plotting as a Craft With Its Own Vocabulary**

The verb behind *devise a plot* is from *kayd*, a stratagem, a plan assembled with care and hidden until it works. The Qur'an reserves this vocabulary for deliberate contrivance, and it uses the same root for both sides of a conflict. Human plotting appears in the plans of Joseph's brothers, of the women of the city, and of the opponents of every prophet; divine counter-plotting appears where the Qur'an says that they plan and God plans, and that God is the best of planners (8:30). The pairing is the sūrah's engine. A *kayd* built on a well, a bloodied shirt and a controlled set of tears is answered across decades by an arrangement involving a bucket, a granary and a famine. Naming the brothers' action *kayd* in advance tells the reader that it will be met by something of the same order and greater reach.

**"A Sworn Enemy" Means the Enmity Is Public**

The adjective *mubīn* attached to the enemy means manifest, open, evident — the same word that in verse 1 describes the Book as clear. Applied to Satan it says the hostility has been announced rather than concealed. He declared it before Adam, swore to it, and has operated in the open ever since; what is hidden is his method, not his intention. The Qur'an repeats the phrase across many chapters — among them at 2:168, 2:208, 6:142 and 7:22 — and repeats it most pointedly in an instruction about speech between believers: *“Tell My ˹believing˺ servants to say only what is best. Satan certainly seeks to sow discord among them. Satan is indeed a sworn enemy to humankind”* (17:53). The identical closing formula appears there and here, which links the brothers' resentment to a general mechanism: harsh words between people who love each other are the openings he works in. The repetition is itself the pedagogy. An enemy whose enmity has been declared cannot later claim to have been misunderstood, and that is why the clause sits at the end of a domestic warning. Jacob is telling his son that the resentment brewing among his brothers is not a family quarrel with a family solution, but the local expression of a campaign whose author has stated his objective in advance.

**A Warning That Delays the Crime Without Preventing It**

The counsel is sound and the story proceeds anyway. Whether Joseph kept silent is not settled by the text; one transmitted account holds that in the innocence of his age he did tell his brothers, and verse 8 shows the resentment already fully formed and looking for its justification. The more important point is that the envy predates the dream. The brothers resented the favour shown to Joseph and his full brother Benjamin long before any vision supplied them with an argument, so silence would have removed the pretext without removing the motive. Jacob's warning therefore accomplishes something narrower and more real: it buys time, it keeps the blessing out of the hands of those who would destroy it, and it puts on record that the father saw the danger clearly. He is not the dupe of the coming deception. The tragedy of the chapter is committed by people who have been warned.

The following verse continues Jacob's speech to his son.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:6

> And so will your Lord choose you ˹O Joseph˺, and teach you the interpretation of dreams, and perfect His favour upon you and the descendants of Jacob—˹just˺ as He once perfected it upon your forefathers, Abraham and Isaac. Surely your Lord is All-Knowing, AllWise.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Choosing Follows the Vision**

The verb *yajtabīka*, will choose you, describes a selection made for a task rather than a preference felt toward a person. Jacob's reasoning runs from what has already happened to what must follow: the God who selected this boy for a vision of that rank will select him for work of a corresponding rank. Several commentators take the choosing to name prophethood outright, since the sūrah's later verses show Joseph receiving revelation, interpreting dreams as a commissioned function and calling his fellow prisoners to the worship of one God. Others keep it broader and read it as appointment to great affairs, of which prophethood is the highest instance. The broader reading has an advantage here, because the sentence pairs choosing with teaching. What follows is a curriculum — selection, then instruction, then completion of favour — and the order suggests a preparation for office rather than the announcement of a status already held.

**Taʾwīl al-Aḥādīth: Reading Events, Not Only Dreams**

The gift named second is *taʾwīl al-aḥādīth*, literally the interpretation of accounts or events, which the translation renders as the interpretation of dreams. The word *aḥādīth* is the plural of *ḥadīth*, a thing spoken of, an occurrence, a report. Three layers of meaning have been drawn from it, and the chapter uses all three. The narrowest is the technical skill of decoding a sleeping vision, which Joseph exercises for two prisoners (12:36–41) and then for a king (12:43–49). The middle layer is the discernment of hidden meanings in scripture, the same faculty praised in *“˹It is˺ Allah ˹Who˺ has sent down the best message—a Book of perfect consistency and repeated lessons”* (39:23). The widest is the ability to see what events are doing, which is what Joseph displays when he reads a famine as a plan and a family's arrival as a reckoning. The phrase *taʾwīl* itself means returning a thing to its origin, so the skill is not guessing but tracing an appearance back to the reality that produced it.

**Where Dreams Come From, and Why the Distinction Matters**

The tradition that grew around this gift began by sorting visions into kinds. A true dream is understood to reach the sleeper from an angelic quarter, while a false one arises from the lower self or from a devil's suggestion. Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī preserves the threefold division as a saying current among the early Muslims, reported there through Ibn Sīrīn: dreams are the reflection of one's waking thoughts, what Satan suggests in order to frighten, or glad tidings from God. The prophetic reports draw the same line more sharply — the good vision is from God and the distressing dream from Satan, and the one who sees the second should spit dryly to his left three times, seek refuge and turn over (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 2262). Only the first kind carries meaning, which is why interpretation is a discipline rather than a pastime. Joseph's gift presupposes the distinction: to interpret a dream one must first know whether it deserves interpreting at all.

**The Favour Perfected on a Whole House**

Jacob does not stop at his son. The blessing is promised to Joseph *and to the descendants of Jacob*, which extends the gift across a family and a century. Two readings of that extension are traditional. The first takes the blessing to be prophethood, so that the house will produce a line of prophets. The second takes it to include the goods of this world along with the goods of the next, which fits Joseph's case unusually well: he receives revelation and a treasury. The second is the better fit for the sūrah, because the plot delivers both and insists that neither is complete without the other — a prophet with no authority could not save the family, and an administrator with no revelation could not forgive it. Zakariyya's prayer in another chapter asks for an heir who will inherit from him and *“from the family of Jacob”* (19:6), showing that the house remained a byword for a certain kind of blessing long after this promise.

**Two Forefathers and the Two Trials Behind Them**

Abraham and Isaac are named as the precedent, and each name carries a trial that ended in deliverance. Abraham was taken by God as a close friend (4:125) and was rescued from the fire his own people built for him: *“We ordered, ‘O fire! Be cool and safe for Abraham!’”* (21:69; the building of the furnace is reported at 37:97). Isaac's blessing is bound to the altar, where he submitted to his father's commission and was ransomed by a substitute at God's command (37:102–107). The Qur'an does not name the son in that account, and the exegetes differ between Ismāʿīl and Isḥāq; Jacob's phrasing here does not settle the question, since he cites both men as recipients of a completed favour rather than as participants in one event. What the pairing establishes is the pattern the boy should expect: elevation is reached through a trial that looks like destruction, and the favour is called complete only when the trial has been survived.

**All-Knowing, All-Wise**

The verse closes on two names, *ʿAlīm Ḥakīm*, and their order is the point. Knowledge precedes wisdom because selection requires seeing what is not yet visible — the boy in the well, the prisoner before the king, the famine years before the harvest — while wisdom concerns the arrangement of what has been seen. A chooser who is all-knowing cannot be mistaken about the person selected; a chooser who is all-wise cannot be mistaken about the sequence. Both names are needed for what Jacob has just promised, since the promise includes delay, imprisonment and false accusation, none of which looks wise while it is happening. The two names also govern the entire chapter, which is a demonstration that God's knowledge of an outcome and God's wisdom in the route to it are not the same attribute and cannot be judged by the same clock.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:7

> Indeed, in the story of Joseph and his brothers there are lessons for all who ask.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Āyāt Where a Story Should Be**

The word used for what the story contains is *āyāt*, the same plural that names the verses of the Qur'an and the signs of God in the world. The earliest commentators read it here as *ʿibrah*, lessons — a crossing over, from the same root as the word for interpretation, because a lesson carries the mind from a visible event to an invisible rule. The choice of word is deliberate. A narrative is not normally described in the vocabulary of evidence, yet this one is: the events of Joseph's life are placed in the same category as the splitting of the sea and the raising of the dead. What the story proves is therefore not a point of history but a point of doctrine, and the proof works by exhibition rather than argument. Envy is not condemned here in the abstract; it is shown building a plot, executing it badly, and losing everything it aimed to gain.

**For Those Who Ask**

The qualification at the end of the verse restricts the audience. *Li-l-sāʾilīn* — for those who ask, for the inquirer — excludes the indifferent reader and the hostile one in the same breath. A sign is only a sign for someone looking in its direction. The Qur'an makes the same demand elsewhere and in the same idiom, instructing the uncertain to put the question: *“So ask the people of the message if you do not know”* (16:43). Asking is treated as a virtue rather than a deficiency throughout the Book, and here it is the condition of receiving anything from the narrative at all. That condition shapes how the chapter is written. Details are withheld, motives are left implicit, and the plot advances by concealment, so a passive listener loses the thread. The lessons are available, but only to the reader willing to work back through the earlier verses to find them.

**The Brothers Are Never Named**

Joseph is named; his brothers are not. Not one of the eleven receives a name anywhere in the sūrah, and the one who dissents from the killing plan is identified only as *one of them* (12:10). The anonymity is a decision, not an omission, since the story is otherwise specific about numbers, objects and places — eleven stars, a shirt, a well, a measured supply of grain. By withholding the names the chapter keeps the brothers as a type. The reader is not asked to judge Reuben or Judah but to recognise a posture: the group that counts itself and finds itself outnumbered by a single favoured member. That posture recurs in every generation, including the one hearing the recitation in Mecca, where a clan of considerable standing was arranging itself against one nephew. Naming the brothers would have turned a mirror into a portrait.

**Envy Is the First Lesson and It Fails**

The moral the commentators draw first is the futility of the motive that drives the plot. Envy produced the plan, the plan produced the well, and the well produced Egypt — the exact opposite of what was intended. The Prophet ﷺ warned against the passion in terms preserved in the collections: “Avoid envy, for envy devours good deeds just as fire devours fuel” (Sunan Abī Dāwūd 4903). The report is graded weak by al-Albānī, yet its meaning stands on stronger ground, since the prohibition itself is recorded in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim among the commands binding the community together. Al-Rāzī draws the parallel that would have struck the first audience hardest: the same envy that moved Joseph's brothers moved the Prophet's own kin against him, and in both cases the envied party ended up holding authority over the enviers. The lesson is not that envy is ugly. It is that envy is incompetent.

**Patience and Gratitude Are the Two Hinges**

Read as instruction, the story divides cleanly into two postures and the trials that produce them. Joseph in the well, in slavery and in prison is the model of patience; Joseph in the treasury, with his brothers kneeling before him, is the model of gratitude. Neither posture is presented as a temperament. Both are achievements under pressure, and the chapter is careful to show the cost: patience that complains to creatures is not the patience Jacob calls beautiful (12:18), and gratitude that forgets the prison is not the gratitude Joseph offers at the end (12:100). Between the two hinges sits the theme that gives the sūrah its particular consolation — that God's care for His friends runs through their afflictions rather than around them, so the trial is not evidence of abandonment. Love and severity arrive in the same parcel, and the story exists to teach the recipient to read the address.

**A Signpost Placed Before the Story Begins**

Structurally this verse interrupts the narrative at its most exposed point. The dream has been told, the father's interpretation given, and the brothers' resentment is about to speak for the first time in verse 8. Between the promise and the crime the chapter steps out of the story to say what the story is for. The effect on the reader is decisive: from this point the plot is watched rather than simply followed. Every reversal is received as evidence for a claim already stated, and the audience knows in advance that the malice they are about to witness will be defeated by it. A narrative that announces its own meaning before delivering it is unusual, and the announcement converts suspense into recognition. The pleasure offered is not what happens next but seeing how what happens next was already contained in the lesson. The same method closes the chapter. Its final verse generalises the move made here: *“In their stories there is truly a lesson for people of reason. This message cannot be a fabrication, rather ˹it is˺ a confirmation of previous revelation, a detailed explanation of all things, a guide, and a mercy for people of faith”* (12:111). Stories are ranked there with guidance and mercy, which is the category this verse had already assigned to Joseph's account. A narrative that can bear that weight is not an illustration of doctrine. In the Qur'an's own accounting it is one of the ways doctrine is delivered.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:8

> ˹Remember˺ when they said ˹to one another˺, “Surely Joseph and his brother ˹Benjamin˺ are more beloved to our father than we, even though we are a group of so many. Indeed, our father is clearly mistaken.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Envy Begins by Counting**

The brothers' first recorded words are arithmetic. Two against ten, and the smaller number is loved more. That is the whole grievance, and the verse states it in the form of an injustice rather than a feeling. Envy rarely announces itself as envy; it announces itself as a discrepancy that demands explanation. Once the count has been made, the conclusion follows almost mechanically: since the numbers are ours and the affection is not, something must be wrong with the distribution. No sentence in the verse expresses hatred. Hatred arrives only after the calculation has been accepted as evidence, which is why the chapter places the counting first. The reader is shown the moment a family turns into a faction, and the turning point is a subtraction.

**ʿUṣbah: A Word That Measures Strength**

*A group of so many* translates *ʿuṣbah*, a term the lexicographers define as a band of roughly ten to forty people, large enough to act together and to be reckoned with. The brothers choose it deliberately. They are not saying that they are numerous; they are saying that they constitute a force, the kind of group that defends a household, works its land and carries its name. In a tribal setting that was a real argument. Adult sons were the security of an ageing father, and a clan's standing was measured in grown men rather than in children. The word will return in their oath a few verses later, where the same self-image is used to promise that no wolf could take a boy from among them (12:14). Both uses rest on a single assumption: that strength of this kind entitles its holders to a share of love proportionate to their usefulness.

**"His Brother" Means Benjamin, and the Distinction Is Real**

The brother named only as *his brother* is Benjamin, Joseph's full brother — the one son of Jacob by the same mother. The phrase does careful work. It marks the two youngest as a pair inside a larger family, and it records the fact that provoked the resentment: Jacob kept them nearer to him than the rest, and the commentators report that he would not let Joseph out of his company night or day. Their youth explains the closeness and does not excuse the interpretation the older brothers placed on it. Affection for two children who had lost their mother was read as a verdict on ten men who had not. The same logic will drive the second half of the sūrah, when Benjamin becomes the object of a plot and the brothers discover that they have not learned anything from the first one.

**A Domestic Complaint in the Language of Unbelief**

The final clause is the most alarming in the verse. *Indeed, our father is clearly mistaken* renders *fī ḍalāl mubīn*, in manifest error — the phrase the Qur'an uses elsewhere for real deviation, spoken by disbelievers about their prophets and by prophets about idolatry. Here it is turned on a father by his sons over a matter of household affection. The escalation is diagnostic. When a grievance cannot be resolved by argument, it borrows the vocabulary of the largest available accusation, and the accuser begins to experience a preference as a heresy. Jacob is not merely unfair in their account; he has gone astray, which means he cannot be reasoned with and must be worked around. Every plot in this chapter follows from that single substitution of categories.

**Two Logics of Value, and Neither One Is About Joseph**

What the verse exposes is a competition between two ways of measuring a person. The brothers measure by capacity: we are many, we are grown, we can defend and provide. Jacob measures by need and by love, which do not scale with usefulness. Both logics are intelligible, and the tragedy lies in the fact that the brothers apply the first to a question it cannot answer. A father's affection is not a ration to be divided according to contribution. Nor is Joseph the object of their complaint in any strict sense; he is the occasion of it. What they want is their father's attention, and the removal of a rival is only the means. That is why the plan they are about to form aims at separation rather than at gain — no property, no inheritance, no office is mentioned. Only the face of the father turning toward them.

**The Sentence Is Turned Inward**

The verse opens with a small marker easily missed: they said it *to one another*. Not one word here is addressed to Jacob. The complaint is rehearsed in private, confirmed by repetition, and hardened by agreement before any of them meets the man they are complaining about. That is the ordinary mechanism of collective wrongdoing. A grievance stated to the person concerned can be answered; a grievance circulated among the aggrieved becomes a settled fact, and each repetition makes retraction more costly. The Qur'an's decision to report the private conversation gives the reader access to something the father never had. Jacob will hear the polished version — a request to take the boy out to play — and will have to judge it without knowing the deliberations behind it.

**What the Verse Does Not Say**

No mention is made of inheritance, land, livestock or office. Ten grown men aggrieved by a father's preference do not ask for anything; they ask to be preferred. That absence narrows the possible remedy. A material grievance can be settled by a division, a payment, a portion allotted in writing. A grievance about love cannot be settled at all, because affection is not a divisible estate and any distribution of it leaves the person who receives less with the same complaint. The brothers have therefore chosen an objective that their own plan cannot reach. Removing Joseph does not produce love for them; it produces an absence, and an absence is felt as loss rather than as gain. Jacob's grief will make that plain within a day, and the chapter will go on for eighty more verses showing that nothing they obtained was what they wanted.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:9

> Kill Joseph or cast him out to some ˹distant˺ land so that our father’s attention will be only ours, then after that you may ˹repent and˺ become righteous people!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Proposals With a Single Objective**

The verse offers alternatives, and the alternatives reveal what is actually wanted. Death and distant exile are very different acts with very different consequences for the victim, yet they are presented as interchangeable, which means neither is the point. What both achieve is absence. The brothers are not choosing between two harms they have weighed; they are naming two routes to one destination, and the destination is a household in which Joseph no longer appears. The construction *kill him or cast him out* also shows the deliberation at an early, unrestrained stage: the violent option is spoken first and plainly, before the moderate voice of verse 10 arrives to negotiate it downward. The Qur'an preserves that order because it matters morally. A group that has already said the word aloud has crossed a line, and the later compromise does not return them to the far side of it.

The two options also differ in what they leave behind for the father. Death closes the case; exile leaves it open. A boy cast out to some distant land — the land is deliberately unspecified, a place where he cannot be looked for — may live, and a living son may one day be found. The brothers accept that residue of possibility because they judge the distance sufficient, and the text records no debate about it. Their tolerance of uncertainty is what distinguishes this proposal from murder, and it is also what the plot will exploit. Every later development in the sūrah depends on Joseph having survived: the caravan, the Egyptian household, the prison, the granaries, the recognition scene. The option that seemed to leave a loose end was the only one that left a story.

**The Object Is the Father's Face**

The purpose clause names the real target. *So that our father's attention will be only ours* renders a phrase built on the word for face — that his face may be toward you alone. Face here means regard, the direction in which a person's care turns. What the brothers want is not an inheritance, an office or a possession; nothing material is mentioned anywhere in their planning. They want exclusivity of affection, and they want it measured: only ours, with no remainder. That demand explains why the crime had to be total. A rival who is merely reduced still receives some part of the father's regard, and any part is unbearable to someone whose objective is the whole. Envy of this kind cannot be satisfied by improvement in its own position, only by the disappearance of the comparison.

**Repentance Placed on the Schedule**

The final clause is the most studied feature of the verse. *Then after that you may repent and become righteous people* puts contrition in sequence after the crime, as a step in a plan rather than a change of heart. The grammar is ordinary and its content is extraordinary: a sin is committed with the cure already booked. The commentators read the repentance in two directions — toward God for the wrong done to Joseph, or toward the father for the disruption caused to the family — and both readings agree that it is postponed. Postponement is what makes the arrangement possible at all. No one in this verse experiences remorse; they schedule it, and a scheduled remorse costs nothing at the moment of decision. The plan therefore includes its own absolution, priced in advance and payable later, which is the most efficient form of self-deception a group can devise.

**The Qur'an's Own Verdict on That Arrangement**

Deferred repentance is treated elsewhere in the Book as a category error. *“Allah only accepts the repentance of those who commit evil ignorantly ˹or recklessly˺ then repent soon after—Allah will pardon them”* (4:17). The condition attached is speed: soon after. The case excluded is named explicitly in the next verse: *“However, repentance is not accepted from those who knowingly persist in sin until they start dying, and then cry, ‘Now I repent!’”* (4:18). Between those two positions lies the brothers' proposal, and it is closer to the excluded case than they think. A wrong undertaken with the intention of repenting afterwards is not ignorance; it is calculation, and calculation is precisely what the first verse does not cover. The mercy the chapter eventually shows them is real — Joseph forgives without condition at 12:92 — but it arrives after decades of consequence, not as the cancellation of a pre-arranged instalment.

**"Become Righteous People" Borrows the Vocabulary of Piety**

The word they choose for themselves is *ṣāliḥūn*, the righteous, the sound, those whose condition is upright. It is among the highest terms of commendation in the Qur'an, used of prophets and of the ordinary faithful alike, and the brothers apply it to their own future as the natural consequence of a murder. The incongruity is the point the verse is built to expose. Wickedness rarely announces itself in wicked language; it announces itself in the language of restoration, of returning to normal, of setting things right. Two readings of the clause survive in the commentaries — repentance toward God, or the mending of relations with their father — and the second is instructive: on that reading, righteousness means the household functioning again, which is to say that the moral measure is comfort rather than justice. Jacob's grief counts as a problem to be solved after the solution has been implemented.

**A Collective Crime Requires a Collective Alibi**

The exhortation is plural throughout: your father's attention will be ours, you may repent, you may become righteous. The speaker addresses the group as a unit, and the unit is what absorbs the guilt. Nobody in this sentence takes an individual role; the killing is proposed impersonally and the absolution is distributed across all ten. That is the ordinary architecture of group wrongdoing, and the Qur'an renders it with exactness. A crime owned by everyone is owned by no one, and the repentance promised to the collective will in practice be performed by nobody in particular. The chapter will take eighty verses to undo the arrangement, and the undoing begins when the brothers stand one by one in front of a ruler they do not recognise and are forced to account for themselves individually.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:10

> One of them said, “Do not kill Joseph. But if you must do something, throw him into the bottom of a well so perhaps he may be picked up by some travellers.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Dissent That Saves a Life and Still Commits a Crime**

The objection is real and it changes the outcome, yet it does not clear the objector. He refuses murder; he proposes disappearance. The distinction he draws is between shedding blood and arranging circumstances in which blood may be shed by someone else, by accident, or by nobody at all. That distinction has always been available to groups planning a wrong, and it is almost always the one that wins, because it lets every participant feel that he has moderated the crime rather than joined it. The Qur'an reports the proposal without praise and without condemnation, which is itself a judgement. The boy still ends up at the bottom of a pit, stripped and alone, and the man who saved him from death is counted among those who threw him in.

**The Eldest Brother, and a Name the Qur'an Withholds**

The commentators identify the speaker as the eldest of the ten, though the text gives him no name. Two candidates are offered from the biblical account: Reuben, who in Genesis 37:21–22 tries to rescue Joseph and return him to his father, and Judah, who in Genesis 37:26–27 proposes selling him rather than killing him. The Qur'anic suggestion resembles the second and the intercession described in the transmitted reports resembles the first, which is why the exegetes have not settled the question. Al-Rāzī notes that the action itself — proposing a commercial outcome rather than a violent one — points toward Judah, while others follow the report that the eldest had given his father a pledge. The withholding of the name is consistent with the chapter's treatment of all ten. Their individual identities are irrelevant to the lesson; what matters is that a group produced a moderate voice and still acted.

**"If You Must Do Something" Is the Idiom of Concession**

The clause *in kuntum fāʿilīn*, if you would take some action, is a formula of reluctant permission rather than of encouragement. It concedes that the decision has already been made and offers only to shape it. The speaker is not arguing the group out of its plan; he is negotiating its terms, which is a weaker position and a commoner one. Anyone who has sat in a room where a wrong decision has hardened will recognise the move. The proposal is framed so that rejecting it means insisting on the worse option, and the burden of refusal falls on those who want the blood. Rhetorically it is effective, and it works here: the majority agrees. Morally it converts a crime into a policy, and policies are easier to live with afterwards.

**The Well as Exile Delegated to Strangers**

The plan has a specific structure worth naming. The brothers do not intend Joseph's death; they intend his removal, and they outsource the outcome. A well on a caravan route is a device for transferring responsibility to travellers who will never be identified. If a passing company lifts him out, he lives somewhere far away and the family is intact in form. If he drowns or dies of exposure, the well did it. Either way no brother holds a knife. Al-Rāzī and others observe that this was precisely the recommendation's appeal: the stain of blood stays off their hands, and the distance they wanted is achieved without the act they feared. The moral cost of that arrangement is not reduced by its ingenuity. A harm arranged so that nobody performs it is still performed, and the sūrah will spend eighty verses collecting the bill.

The vocabulary of the proposal carries the same evasiveness. *Ghayābat al-jubb* — the bottom of the well, more precisely the hidden recess of it — names both a place and a concealment. A *jubb* is an unlined well cut into rock, and its *ghayābah* is the part where light does not reach and where a body cannot be seen from above. The commentators describe the particular well as deep, wide at the bottom and narrow at the mouth. Choosing a location defined by invisibility is a decision about witnesses as much as about geography: nobody will see what happens there, and nobody will be able to look down afterwards and find him. The same noun will do double duty in the story, since concealment is precisely what the plan fails at. He is hidden from his father, and hidden therefore from the brothers' control, and the hiding is what allows him to be found by the wrong people — which turns out to be the right ones.

**How Groups Choose the Middle Option**

The sequence in these verses is a study in collective decision-making. One faction wants death; another wants exile to a distant land; a third proposes the well. The third wins not because it is right but because it is survivable for everyone present — the violent are still acting, the hesitant are still abstaining from blood, and the whole group leaves with a story it can tell. Extremism in a deliberating body rarely triumphs outright; it is usually absorbed into a compromise that keeps its substance and sheds its name. The Qur'an records the mechanic without comment, and the reader is left to notice that the moderating voice in this story belongs to a man who then helps lower his brother into a pit.

**God's Plan Runs Through the Instrument of the Crime**

The well is the hinge of the entire narrative, and it was chosen by the plotters. Everything after it depends on the decision made in this verse: the caravan that draws water, the bucket, the market in Egypt, the household of the official, the prison, the interpretation of a king's dream, the granaries, the famine, and finally the reunion. Had the brothers killed him, none of it would exist. The chapter is built so that the instrument of destruction is also the instrument of elevation, and the reader watches the same pit serve two purposes at once. This is the working form of the principle stated elsewhere in the Book — that a person may dislike a thing while God places much good in it (2:216) — displayed rather than asserted. The verse looks like the point at which the plot succeeds. It is the point at which it is commandeered.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:11

> They said, “O our father! Why do you not trust us with Joseph, although we truly wish him well?

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Opening Move Attacks the Premise, Not the Request**

Nothing is asked for yet. The brothers begin by interrogating Jacob's refusal, which means the refusal already exists and they have met it before. A first request would have run differently: send him with us. Instead the sentence takes the form of a complaint about his judgement, and the complaint does the work of establishing the ground on which the real request will be made in the next verse. Anyone who has been prepared by this method will recognise it. Once the other party has been made to feel that his caution is unreasonable, the favour he grants afterwards feels like a concession to sense rather than a surrender to pressure. The strategy is deliberate, and the Qur'an reports it in the order it was executed, which allows the reader to watch a manipulation being assembled in real time.

**"What Ails You" Is a Question With an Answer Built In**

The Arabic *mā laka* — what is the matter with you, what ails you — is not a neutral inquiry. It presupposes a defect in the person addressed and invites him to explain it. The idiom is common in the Qur'an and always carries that charge; it is the form used when a speaker has decided that something is wrong with the other's perception rather than with his own. Applied here, the phrase converts a father's protective instinct into a symptom requiring diagnosis. Jacob is not told that he is mistaken about a fact. He is asked why he cannot trust his own sons, a question with no honest answer that does not accuse them. The construction traps him: to justify the refusal he must state a suspicion he cannot prove, and stating it will confirm the picture of an unreasonable old man.

**"We Truly Wish Him Well" Claims the Virtue of Sincerity**

The word behind *we truly wish him well* is from *naṣīḥah*, sincerity, the quality of wanting good for another without reserve — a term the tradition treats as one of the highest social virtues, the substance of the pledge the Prophet ﷺ took from his Companions to advise every Muslim sincerely. The brothers appropriate it. Their sentence has two emphatic particles wrapped around the claim, which in Arabic produces something close to an oath: we are, without doubt, sincere well-wishers toward him. The irony is exact. Sincerity is being asserted by men who agreed two verses earlier to throw the boy into a well, and the assertion is aimed at the one person in the family with the standing to test it. Al-Rāzī observes that the claim was designed to present love and compassion for Joseph, implying that no harm could be intended. It is the first lie of the chapter spoken directly to the father, and it is a lie about a virtue.

**Their Question Confirms That Jacob Had Already Refused**

The verse settles a question the narrative has left open. The brothers had asked before and been turned down, which is why the well plan recorded in verse 10 was probably one of several schemes rather than the first. It also explains the shape of verse 8's grievance: a father who refuses his grown sons their younger brother is a father whose preference has become visible in conduct, not merely in feeling. The refusal is evidence of the partiality they resent. Two readings of Jacob's motive are possible and both fit the text — ordinary protectiveness toward a child, or the specific fear that verse 5 records, that these sons would devise something against him. The second is more likely, since the father in this sūrah is never portrayed as merely anxious. He saw the plot coming and said so aloud.

**Credibility Is Built Before the Favour Is Asked**

The division of labour between this verse and the next is a technique. Verse 11 establishes character; verse 12 makes the request. No demand is issued until the demanders have been presented as sincere and the objector as unreasonably suspicious. Modern readers will recognise the sequence from any negotiation in which one side spends its first minutes repairing its reputation rather than stating its case. The Qur'an's decision to reproduce both stages separately, instead of compressing them into one speech, forces the audience to sit through the preparation. The result is that when the request finally comes — send him out with us tomorrow — it arrives already insulated from refusal, and Jacob's hesitation looks like stubbornness rather than insight.

Worth noticing too is what the verse does not contain: an answer. The rhetorical question is left hanging, and the father's reply comes only after the concrete request of verse 12. Jacob does not argue with the accusation. A man asked why he cannot trust his own sons has no reply that does not either insult them or expose a suspicion he cannot substantiate, and the text shows him declining the trap rather than falling into it. He moves the conversation from his character to the boy's safety, which is the only ground on which he can actually stand. That shift costs him the rhetorical exchange and saves him the appearance of accusing ten men of murder to their faces. It also means the brothers' charge goes unanswered on the record, where it will continue to look reasonable to anyone who hears only their side.

**The Father's Suspicion Was Correct, and That Is the Point**

Everything the brothers say here is false, and the verse is constructed so that the reader knows it. The chapter has already shown the private deliberation (12:8), the two proposals for disposal (12:9), and the compromise of the well (12:10). When the polished public version arrives, its falsity is not inferred; it is remembered. That arrangement produces the sūrah's central dramatic effect. Jacob will be deceived, and the audience will not be. His eventual refusal to believe their later story about the wolf will look like paranoia to anyone reading only verse 17, and like accuracy to anyone who has read this one. Distrust is being vindicated in advance, and the vindication depends on the reader holding two versions of the same family in mind at once — the one presented to the father, and the one presented to God.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:12

> Send him out with us tomorrow so that he may enjoy himself and play. And we will really watch over him.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Request That Is Small, Cheerful and Dated**

After the interrogation of the previous verse, the demand arrives disguised as a favour to the boy. Send him out with us *tomorrow* — the adverb does a great deal of work. It forecloses deliberation, since a plan that begins tomorrow cannot be examined at leisure, and it frames any hesitation as obstruction of a child's pleasure. The request is also modest in scale: an outing to the pasture, a place the brothers visit as a matter of routine. Nothing about it looks like the beginning of the worst day of Jacob's life. That ordinariness is the deception's strength. A father can refuse a strange proposal; refusing an everyday one makes him the unreasonable party, which is exactly the position verse 11 was constructed to produce.

**"That He May Enjoy Himself and Play" Uses a True Premise**

The stated purpose is genuine. Joseph was a boy, boys need air and movement, and a child kept permanently at his father's side is deprived of something. The brothers have found the one argument Jacob cannot reject without cost, because rejecting it means admitting that his protectiveness is harming the child it protects. The commentators note that going out to the pasture for leisure was their custom, so the outing itself required no invention — only the passenger. This is the structure of most effective falsehood: the frame is real, the detail inserted into it is not. A lie that required fabricating the entire excursion would have been harder to sustain under questioning; a lie that only required adding one name to an existing plan was almost unfalsifiable in advance.

**"We Will Really Watch Over Him" Is a Sentence They Will Say Again**

The promise closes with two emphatic particles: we are, indeed, for him, guardians. The word *ḥāfiẓūn* is the same they will use decades later, in the second half of the story, when they ask Jacob to send Benjamin to Egypt: *“So send our brother with us so that we may receive our measure, and we will definitely watch over him”* (12:63). The repetition is not accidental. Jacob's answer the second time quotes their history back at them — *“Should I trust you with him as I once trusted you with his brother ˹Joseph˺?”* (12:64) — and the chapter thereby marks the phrase as the family's recurring instrument of deceit. A formula used twice, with the same emphasis and the same result, is a signature. The reader who remembers verse 12 hears verse 63 differently.

**The Pledge the Reports Describe**

The transmitted accounts supply what the verse leaves implicit. Jacob is said to have extracted a solemn undertaking from the brothers before releasing Joseph, and one report preserves the terms in detail: he is little, feed him if he is hungry, give him drink if he is thirsty, carry him if he tires, and bring him back quickly. Whether or not that exchange occurred in those words, the text supports its substance. Verse 66 shows the same method applied later, when Jacob refuses to send Benjamin until they swear by God, and verse 64 shows him reflecting on the earlier trust as something granted and betrayed. A father who demands oaths in the second case plainly demanded assurances in the first. The brothers accepted them, which converts their later act from opportunism into perjury.

**What the Father Weighed**

Two pressures act on Jacob simultaneously and the verse lets the reader feel both. Refusing means keeping a child indoors against his nature and confirming the brothers' charge of mistrust; agreeing means handing his favourite son to the ten men whose resentment he has already identified. He chooses the second risk. Nothing in the text suggests he was deceived about the brothers' feelings — verse 5 shows him predicting a plot — so his decision must be understood as a calculated acceptance of danger rather than a failure of perception. Love of this kind cannot protect its object by permanent withdrawal, and a boy who is never allowed to leave his father's side grows up unable to survive what he will have to survive. The chapter's whole second half depends on Joseph having been sent out.

**Evil Borrows the Vocabulary of Childhood**

The most unsettling feature of these verses is their tone. The plot to kill or exile a brother has been discussed in verse 9; two verses later the same men are speaking of games and guardianship, and the language of the second conversation is warm. Nothing in the transition registers the shift. This is how the Qur'an portrays hypocrisy at the domestic scale: not as a performance of menace but as a performance of affection, delivered by people who genuinely intend the harm and genuinely enjoy the outing. Al-Qushayrī's remark on their later tears applies here too — that a person who has become thoroughly two-faced gains control of his outward signs, including his eyes. The verse asks the reader to hold both facts at once: the words were kind, and the plan behind them was already fixed.

The grammar of the closing promise deserves a second look. *Wa-innā lahu la-ḥāfiẓūn* stacks two particles of emphasis — the initial *inna* and the *lām* prefixed to the predicate — producing something stronger than a plain statement: we are, indeed, his guardians. Verse 11 used the identical construction for its claim of sincerity. Two consecutive sentences, both double-emphasised, both false. Emphasis in Arabic is a tool for closing a gap between what is said and what the hearer suspects, and its accumulation is a symptom rather than a guarantee. Jacob, who has just been told that his distrust is unreasonable, is being handed two oaths in the space of thirty words. He accepts the request, and the reports describe him extracting conditions in return, which is the behaviour of a man who hears the emphasis and does not trust it.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:13

> He responded, “It would truly sadden me if you took him away with you, and I fear that a wolf may devour him while you are negligent of him.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Reasons, One of the Heart and One of the Head**

Jacob's refusal is argued rather than asserted, and the argument has two distinct parts. The first is emotional: it grieves me that you should take him. The second is practical: I fear a wolf will eat him while you are not paying attention. He gives both, in that order, and the order is instructive. A man who offered only the first would be confessing an attachment he could not defend; a man who offered only the second would be inventing a pretext. By stating both, Jacob discloses the real structure of his position — love supplies the motive and reason supplies the form. The verse thus portrays a father who is neither sentimental nor calculating but both at once, which is why his judgement in this chapter consistently proves more accurate than that of everyone around him.

**The Grief of Separation Is Named Without Shame**

*It would truly sadden me* is a plain admission that an hour without this son costs him something. The commentators draw the obvious inference: his love was intense enough that brief absence produced real pain, and the reports add that he kept Joseph with him night and day. The spiritual tradition reads that intensity as an image rather than merely a fact. Just as a father could not bear separation from the beloved son, so the believer is instructed not to be forgetful of the Divine Beloved even for a moment, since heedlessness is itself separation and remembrance is togetherness. The comparison is not decorative. The sūrah will show that Jacob's attachment survives every test applied to it — the bloodied shirt, the decades of silence, the second loss — and it will show him still saying that he knows from God what his sons do not know (12:86).

**Naming the Danger Hands Them the Script**

The wolf appears in this verse for the first time, and it appears in the mouth of the man it will supposedly kill. Jacob names a specific hazard of the pasture, and three verses later the brothers return to report exactly that hazard realised: a wolf devoured him (12:17). The irony is structural, not incidental. Their lie requires no invention because their father has already supplied its content; they only have to agree with him. Anyone who has warned a person about a risk and then been told the risk has materialised will recognise the difficulty of proof that follows. The warning becomes the alibi. The Qur'an places the two sentences close enough together that the reader hears the transfer happening, and hears Jacob's own foresight converted into evidence against him.

**"While You Are Negligent" Locates the Danger in Attention**

The fear is not that wolves exist. Pastures always contain them, and the brothers are ten grown men. What Jacob fears is *ghaflah*, heedlessness — the state of being absorbed in something else while the thing entrusted to you goes unguarded. The clause subordinates the animal to the human failure: the wolf is only lethal in the window where attention has wandered. That diagnosis proves exactly right, though not in the way he intended. No wolf approaches Joseph at any point in the story. The predators are the guardians themselves, and the negligence he feared is replaced by a deliberate act. The verse nevertheless states the true principle, which the rest of the chapter will illustrate repeatedly: what destroys a person is rarely the hazard he was warned about, but the inattention of those standing nearest to him.

**A Specific Wolf, or Ten of Them**

Two readings of the definite article have been preserved. The first takes *the wolf* literally and notes that the definite form suggests one particular animal, known to the family, which is why the brothers can report it so confidently and why Jacob can be accused of it later. The second reading rests on a transmitted account of a dream Jacob had: himself on a mountaintop, Joseph in a valley, ten wolves circling the boy, and one wolf withdrawing before the ground opened to hide him for three days.
