The naming is attributed to two parties: the hearers and their ancestors. That pairing isolates the actual authorities behind idolatry in the Qur'an's analysis — present consensus and inherited custom. Neither is a reason. What a group currently agrees on is not evidence of a thing's existence, and what a group previously agreed on is not evidence either, since ancestors were themselves in the position of the hearers and received the practice from their own ancestors. The verse thereby cuts off the two answers available to anyone asked why he worships what he worships. No appeal to antiquity and no appeal to unanimity survives the formulation, and the sūrah places it in the mouth of a slave addressing two officials, which is the least authoritative position in the room making the most authoritative claim.

**"No Authority Has God Sent Down for It"**

*Sulṭān* is warrant, proof, mandate — the thing that legitimises a claim. The verse states that God has sent down no sulṭān for the names, and the phrase *sent down* fixes the criterion: legitimacy in religion comes by revelation and not by invention, custom, argument or consensus. The criterion is the same one the chapter applied to itself at its opening, where the Book is described as sent down, and the same one verse 3 will invoke when it insists that the Prophet had no prior knowledge of this story. Joseph is applying to the Egyptian cult the test the sūrah applies to its own text. A practice with no descent has no standing, and a practice with descent does not require the approval of the society it addresses.

**Judgement Belongs to God Alone**

*In al-ḥukmu illā li-Llāh* — the judgement is God's, and no one else's. The exclusive construction is emphatic: not shared, not delegated, not distributed among lords. The verse then moves from judgement to command, and the command is the same one stated elsewhere as a decree: *“For your Lord has decreed that you worship none but Him”* (17:23). The sequence is significant. Authority to judge is located in God, and the first exercise of that authority is the command to worship Him alone, so legislation and worship are presented as a single matter rather than two. The household that imprisoned Joseph exercised judgement without authority and reached a decision against the evidence. The verse answers that scene by naming where judgement actually resides.

**"The Upright Faith, But Most People Do Not Know"**

*Al-dīn al-qayyim* — the religion that is straight, upright, self-sustaining. The same description is used of the natural way God has instilled in people: *“So be steadfast in faith in all uprightness ˹O Prophet˺—the natural Way of Allah which He has instilled in ˹all˺ people... That is the Straight Way, but most people do not know”* (30:30). The closing clause of verse 40 is the sūrah's refrain, appearing here for the second time after verse 21 and echoed later at verse 68. It marks the distance between what has been stated plainly and what has been received. Joseph has delivered the argument in four verses, in a prison, to two men. The argument is complete and correct, and the number of people who know it remains small. The verse ends where the chapter began — with clarity offered and understanding not guaranteed.

The sermon as a whole is worth measuring against its setting. Four verses, spoken by a slave to two prisoners, containing a renunciation of the state religion, a genealogy, an argument from cosmic order, a reduction of the pantheon to names, a claim about the location of judgement and a statement of the upright faith. Nothing in it responds to Joseph's own case. He does not mention the seduction, the shirt, the verdict, the banquet or his sentence, and he asks his hearers for nothing — not sympathy, not a message to the king, not help. The two men came to him for a service and received a creed, and the interpretation they asked for is postponed until verse 41. The ordering tells the reader what Joseph considers urgent. Their dreams will be answered, but the answer is not the important thing in the room, and the sūrah arranges the sequence so that nobody can mistake the priority.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:41

> “O my fellow-prisoners! ˹The first˺ one of you will serve wine to his master, and the other will be crucified and the birds will eat from his head. The matter about which you inquired has been decided.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Interpretations Delivered in One Register**

The answer is given without preamble, without qualification and without any difference of tone between the two outcomes. One man will return to his post and pour wine for his employer; the other will be executed and left exposed. The sentence structure treats them as coordinate facts. No consolation is offered to the condemned man, no congratulation to the spared one, and no explanation is given for the difference between them. That flatness is the mark of a genuine interpretation as the tradition understands it: the interpreter reports what has been shown, not what would be kind. The commentators identify the two by their offices — the cupbearer saved and restored, the baker condemned for the plot reported against him — and the Qur'an itself never names either. Anonymity is doing work here. Neither man becomes a type of believer or of disbeliever, because neither is developed at all; both are simply the recipients of a judgement delivered in identical language. What remains for the reader is the register itself — unhedged, unhurried, and given to two people who have no way of checking it against anything they already know.

**Each Dream Returns as an Event Built From Its Own Images**

The method of interpretation is visible in the wording. The first man saw himself pressing wine; he will serve wine. The second saw bread on his head being eaten by birds; birds will eat from his head. Nothing is imported from outside the visions. The images the dreamers supplied are rearranged into the events they signify, and the rearrangement is what the interpretation consists of. That is why the discipline was called *taʾwīl*, from a root meaning to return a thing to its origin: the interpreter does not add meaning to a dream but traces it back to the reality that produced the symbols. The two cases also show that the method is not sentimental. The same procedure that reads a cup as a restoration reads a head as a corpse.

**Crucifixion in the Egyptian Setting**

The punishment named is *ṣalb*, exposure of the body after execution, which in the ancient Near East served as a public warning rather than merely as a method of killing. The detail that birds will eat from the head specifies that the body will not be buried and will remain where scavengers can reach it. The biblical account preserves the same expectation, with the baker's dream interpreted as execution and his body hung on a tree (Genesis 40:18–19). The Qur'an supplies no legal discussion of the penalty and no comment on the justice of the sentence; the crime, on the transmitted reports, was an attempt on the king's life. What the verse records is the interpretation, not an evaluation of Egyptian law. Egyptian practice is known to have used post-mortem display of an executed criminal as a public deterrent, and the wording here fits that setting without passing judgement on it. What carries weight in the chapter is the symmetry of the two sentences: both are delivered in the same voice, in the same breath, to two men who have just been told opposite fates, and neither is softened.

**A Death Sentence Stated Plainly to the Man It Concerns**

Joseph is speaking to the condemned party directly. He does not address the other man privately, does not defer the announcement, and does not frame it hypothetically. The second person is used throughout: one of *you* will serve wine, the other of *you* will be crucified. The choice is a moral one and the chapter endorses it by reporting the speech without correction. A man asked to interpret a dream has undertaken to report the interpretation, and withholding half of it would be a breach of the undertaking rather than an act of mercy. The tradition's later etiquette — that distressing dreams should not be recounted bluntly — concerns dreams a person brings privately about himself, not a case in which two men have jointly requested an answer to a question about their futures.

**"The Matter Has Been Decided" Removes Any Room for Revision**

The closing clause is *quḍiya al-amru alladhī fīhi tastaftiyān* — the affair about which you sought a ruling has been settled. The verb is the one used for a judgement issued and completed. Its function is to close the file: this is not a probable reading, an alternative possibility or a warning that may be averted by precaution. It is a decree already fixed, disclosed to the interpreter rather than produced by him. The clause answers an anxiety the two men must have felt on hearing the sentence, which is whether it could be changed. Joseph's answer is that it cannot, and the chapter treats that as a mercy of a particular kind — the mercy of certainty in a matter where hope would only prolong the waiting. A condemned man who has received a definite answer can spend what is left to him as himself rather than as a petitioner. Joseph's phrasing grants him that, and the chapter counts the granting among the services he performs in the prison, alongside the reading of dreams and the call to belief that precede it.

**Why He Answers After Preaching**

The order of verses 37 to 41 is deliberate. Joseph spends four verses on the oneness of God, on the creed of his fathers, on the comparison between many lords and one, and on the emptiness of names invented by ancestors. Only then does he interpret. Nothing in the sermon was a condition he imposed, and nothing in it was designed to embarrass his hearers into compliance. What the sequence establishes is the standing of the interpreter. A man who has just told two royal servants that their country's religion is a set of invented names is not a man whose prediction they can dismiss as the flattery of a prisoner. The creed gives the interpretation its authority, and the interpretation then confirms the creed by coming true.

**The Fulfilment Is Barely Reported**

The chapter does not dramatise the outcome. Verse 42 identifies one of the two as the man Joseph knew would survive, and verse 45 shows him back at court years later; the other is never mentioned again. No execution scene, no burial, no reaction from the king, no moral drawn. The sūrah's silence is consistent with its method throughout — the Egyptian section reports consequences rather than staging them. What the silence achieves is a certain weight. Two lives were disposed of in a single sentence spoken in a cell, and the narrative moves on, which is how the reader experiences the ordinary operation of decree. The interpretation was accurate, the men knew it was accurate, and only one of them lived to remember it. That asymmetry drives everything after it. A dead man cannot repeat a promise, so the record of Joseph's accuracy passes to the one survivor, and it stays with him, unspoken, through the whole of the years of plenty. The chapter is exact about the price of being right: it produces a witness, and witnesses endure by chance rather than by design.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:42

> Then he said to the one he knew would survive, “Mention me in the presence of your master. ” But Satan made him forget to mention Joseph to his master, so he remained in prison for several years.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Only Thing Joseph Asks for Himself in the Whole Chapter**

Across a hundred verses Joseph makes few requests. He asks God to turn a scheme away from him (12:33), he asks to be put in charge of the storehouses (12:55), and he asks at the end to die as one who submits (12:101). Here he asks a fellow prisoner to say a word on his behalf. The request is small, human and entirely reasonable: a man cleared of every charge, held without a term, appeals to the only person leaving the building who has access to power. The chapter records it without criticism and without endorsement, and it records the consequence in the same breath. That consequence is the reason the verse exists. The sūrah is not interested in the propriety of the request; it is interested in what the request cost.

**"The One He Knew Would Survive"**

The description is precise and it restates the gift. Joseph knows which of the two men will live, and he addresses his appeal to that one. Nothing in the text suggests he chose him out of favour; the choice follows from the interpretation he has just delivered. The clause also has a narrative function. By identifying the man as the survivor, the verse forecloses the possibility that the message failed because it was given to the wrong person. The right man was selected, the right instruction was given, and the message still did not arrive. What defeated it was not incompetence but forgetfulness, and the verse assigns that forgetfulness to a specific agent. Naming Satan as the agent does something unexpected: it removes blame from the man who forgot and from Joseph who asked. Neither is shown acting badly. A palace servant whose position was owed to a prisoner's help had every reason to keep that debt quiet, and the verse records the resulting silence as an operation of the same force that has directed the chapter's opposition from the start.

**Satan Is Named as the Cause**

*Fa-ansāhu al-shayṭānu dhikra rabbih* — Satan made him forget the mention of his lord. The verb is causative: an act of forgetting produced in someone. The Qur'an attributes the failure to Satan rather than to the cupbearer's carelessness, which raises the stakes of the verse considerably. A man who simply forgot is a minor character in a minor lapse; a man made to forget by an adversary is part of a contested operation. The chapter has already established the terms of that contest, in the father's warning that Satan is a sworn enemy to humankind (12:5) and in the sūrah's use of *kayd* for both human and diabolic planning. The forgetfulness is therefore not an accident in the plot. It is the plot's continuation by other means. The brothers used a well and a lie; the women used a locked door and an accusation; here the instrument is memory. Each stage of the opposition adapts to the position Joseph happens to occupy, and none of them needs a villain to appear on the page. That economy is a large part of why the sūrah reads as a single sustained argument rather than as a set of episodes.

**Two Readings of Who Forgot**

The Arabic allows both, and the commentators preserve both. On the first reading, the object of the forgetting is the cupbearer: Satan made him forget to mention Joseph to his master. On the second, the object is Joseph himself — Satan made him forget the remembrance of his Lord, meaning that in the moment of asking a human being to intercede he neglected to rely on God. Ibn ʿAbbās is reported to have held the second reading, with the consequence that the years in prison were the price of that neglect. The first reading fits the sentence more naturally, since the man who is said to have forgotten is the one just addressed. The second is theologically heavier and the tradition kept it alive precisely because of that weight. Both readings agree on the fact that matters: a request was made and nothing came of it.

**"Several Years" Is Left Unquantified**

The phrase is *bidʿa sinīn*, a number of years — the word *bidʿ* covering the range from three to nine, deliberately imprecise. The commentators supply seven, and verse 45 confirms a long interval by describing the cupbearer's remembering as happening *after a period*. The Qur'an's refusal to give a figure is consistent with the way it handles Joseph's suffering generally: the length is stated as long, not as a number, because the number would invite calculation and the experience does not reduce to one. What the imprecision does accomplish is a change of register. The chapter has been counting things carefully — eleven stars, twenty dirhams, seven cows, seven ears — and at the point where Joseph's own endurance is at issue the counting stops. The silence has a clear effect whatever its intent. Estimates in the exegetical literature run from a few years to seven or to twelve, and the Qur'an commits to none of them. What survives the ambiguity is the shape of the sentence: one request, one lapse, and a long undated interval in which the reader is told of nothing happening at all.

**The Delay Places Him Where the Story Needs Him**

Read as arrangement rather than misfortune, the extra years do decisive work. Had the cupbearer remembered immediately, Joseph would have been released into a country with no famine and no need of him, a freed slave with a discredited record and no position. Instead he is still in the prison when the king dreams, and the only man at court who can connect the dream to an interpreter is the man who served time beside him. The chain that runs from verse 42 to verse 45 to verse 54 to verse 56 requires the delay at every link. This is the sūrah's consistent structure: a wrong or a failure produces the exact conditions a later good requires, and the narrator never explains the mechanism, only the sequence.

**The Tradition's Comment on the Sentence**

The Prophet ﷺ is reported to have commented on Joseph's conduct at the end of this period, in a narration preserved in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī from Abū Hurayrah: after mentioning Abraham's request for reassurance and Lot's wish for a strong support, he said that if he had remained in prison as long as Joseph did, he would have answered the caller — that is, he would have accepted the offer of release without first insisting that his innocence be publicly established (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 3372; the same wording is carried in the tafsīr book at Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 4694 and in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). The remark is a comparison in Joseph's favour, not a criticism. It identifies the request in verse 42 as the smaller matter and the insistence in verse 50 as the greater one. A separate report circulating in the tafsīr literature — that Joseph would not have stayed in prison so long had he not said what he said — is a different matter: Ibn Kathīr records it and rejects it, stating that every chain by which it has been transmitted is weak. The authenticated wording is the one that praises Joseph's patience rather than the one that faults his words.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:43

> And ˹one day˺ the King said, “I dreamt of seven fat cows eaten up by seven skinny ones; and seven green ears of grain and ˹seven˺ others dry. O chiefs! Tell me the meaning of my dream if you can interpret dreams.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Ruler Called King, Never Pharaoh**

The sūrah names the ruler of Egypt *al-malik*, the king, and does so consistently across verses 43, 50, 54, 72 and 76. It never calls him Pharaoh. The contrast with the Moses narrative is exact: the ruler who confronts Moses is always *Firʿawn*, and the word appears nowhere in this chapter. The observation is old and has been pressed into service by modern scholars who argue that the Joseph story is set before the period of the native dynasties, during the rule of the Hyksos, whose foreign kings did not use the pharaonic title. That historical identification remains a proposal rather than a settled finding. What is not a proposal is the Qur'an's own consistency. Two Egyptian courts are described in the Book and they are given two different vocabularies, a distinction a borrower from a single source would have had no reason to preserve.

**Seven and Seven, Stated Twice**

The dream has two series and they share a number. Seven fat cows devoured by seven lean ones; seven green ears of grain alongside seven dry. The repetition of seven across two different images is what makes the dream interpretable rather than merely strange, and it is what the king reports first and most precisely. The parallelism also suggests that the two series signify the same thing in two registers — livestock and crops, the two bases of an agricultural economy. Joseph's answer in verses 47 to 49 treats them as one calendar rather than two, giving seven years of plenty, seven of scarcity and a year of relief, which is fifteen years in total. The arithmetic is the key the dream hands to its interpreter.

**"Eaten Up By" Is the Image That Carries the Meaning**

The lean cows do not merely follow the fat ones; they consume them. The verb describes devouring, and the second series repeats it in a different medium — the dry ears standing beside the green. Consumption is the concept the dream is built around: surplus destroyed by scarcity, not simply succeeded by it. That distinction is what makes the dream a matter of policy rather than an omen. If scarcity merely followed plenty, no action would be required beyond endurance. Because scarcity devours what plenty has produced, the only defence is to change the way plenty is handled, which is precisely the instruction Joseph will give. The king's dream contains its own solution in the choice of verb. Consumption is the governing idea of the vision. The fat years do not merely precede the lean ones; they are swallowed by them, and the swallowing is what turns storage from prudence into necessity. Joseph's later programme, with its instruction to leave grain in the ear and to eat little, answers that verb directly, which is why his reading can move from description to policy in a single speech.

**He Asks the Men Around Him**

*Yā ayyuhā al-malaʾ* — O chiefs, O notables, the inner circle of the court. The word denotes the assembly of leading men, and the Qur'an uses it for the ruling councils of other nations as well. The king does not consult priests in this verse, does not send for specialists and does not treat the matter as private. He puts the dream to the people who run the country with him, in a form that makes it a public question. The setting matters for what follows, because the failure of this group is a failure of the state's own intellectual establishment, and the man who answers them will come from a prison rather than from a palace. The plural address matters. A king consulting one adviser would be described differently; this verse shows him putting the question to a body of men, which is how a court actually works. Their failure is therefore collective and institutional, and it opens the gap that Joseph's arrival fills — though he is not sought out to fill it, and nobody at that moment knows his name.

**"If You Can Interpret Dreams" Names a Recognised Skill**

The verb is from the root *ʿ-b-r*, to cross over, and the commentators explain its application here: interpretation is the crossing from the symbolic form a dream presents to the reality it signifies. The skill was a profession, and the king's conditional — if you are people who interpret visions — presupposes that such people exist and that his court should include them. The word for visions in this verse is *ruʾyā*, the term the tradition reserves for the true dream, as distinct from *ḥulm*, the distressing or meaningless one. The king's own description of what he saw uses the vocabulary of a genuine vision, which is what makes the answer in the next verse a dismissal rather than a failure. The conditional also tells us that dream interpretation was a recognised craft, with practitioners and a reputation, something a court could ask for by name. Set against that, the reply which follows is not an admission of ignorance but a professional verdict, and it is the verdict of the establishment: confused dreams, of no determinate meaning, and we are not the people to settle them.

**The Dream Is Treated as a Question About the Country**

Nothing in the king's response suggests he thinks the vision concerns his personal fate. He does not ask what will happen to him, whether he will die, or whether his reign is threatened. He asks for the meaning, and the meaning turns out to be agricultural: seven years of grain and seven of famine. A dream about cows and ears of corn is a dream about the food supply, and the king's instinct to convene his council rather than his household shows that he understood its register immediately. This is why the answer Joseph eventually gives is a plan for storage rather than a prediction about the king, and why a prisoner's interpretation can end with him running the granaries of the country. Read that way, the king's response also becomes intelligible. A dream about his own fate would have called for comfort or for warning; a dream about seven years of national harvest calls for an administrator. The office he goes on to create follows from the reading he has just accepted, not from any favour he intends to do a stranger.

**The Chapter Reproduces the Dream in Full for a Reason**

Every number and every image is given, so that a reader can attempt the interpretation before it is supplied. Seven, seven, fat, lean, green, dry, consumption, consumption. The elements are few, symmetrical and concrete, and the solution — a cycle of agricultural years with a famine in the middle — is available to anyone who takes the images seriously. The sūrah's decision to state the dream at length rather than summarise it turns the reader into a participant. When Joseph's answer arrives in verse 47, it does not feel like privileged information delivered from nowhere. It feels like the correct reading of a text the audience has already been given, which is exactly how the Qur'an wants its own accounts to be received.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:44

> They replied, “These are confused visions and we do not know the interpretation of such dreams.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Classifying the Dream as Meaningless**

The answer is a diagnosis. *Aḍghāthu aḥlām* — jumbled dreams, dreams of confused things. The first noun is a plural whose singular denotes a tangled bundle, a handful of mixed sticks or plants gathered together without order. Applied to a dream it describes imagery with no coherent content, fragments assembled by a sleeping mind rather than signs carrying a message. The classification is technical, not dismissive in tone: the notables are saying that what the king saw belongs to a category which has no interpretation, and therefore no interpreter can be faulted for failing to produce one. The reply is well formed, professionally worded, and wrong.

**Categories Behind the Answer**

The tradition the notables are drawing on divides what people see in sleep into kinds. A true vision comes from a quarter that gives it content and can be traced back to a reality. A distressing dream comes from a devil's suggestion and is meant to frighten. A third kind is the residue of waking thoughts, the mind replaying what it was occupied with. Only the first is interpretable, because only the first corresponds to anything outside the dreamer. The Qur'an's own distinction between *ruʾyā* and *ḥulm* rests on the same ground, as does the Prophetic instruction that a bad dream be met with seeking refuge rather than with analysis. The king's council has placed his vision in the second or third category, which is a decision about the origin of the dream rather than about its difficulty.

**"We Do Not Know" Is Also a Confession**

The second clause can be read narrowly or broadly, and the commentators preserve both. Narrowly: we are not skilled in interpreting dreams of this confused kind — an admission limited to the category just named. Broadly: we are not skilled in dream interpretation at all, which is an admission about the entire court. The broader reading is the more likely in context, since a king who asks his council *if you can interpret visions* has evidently received no such service before, and the council's answer does not offer to fetch someone who can. Either way the confession is total. The governing establishment of Egypt has been asked a question by its ruler and has answered that it has no answer, which is the vacuum the next verse fills.

**An Answer That Is Also Convenient**

A king's dream is a dangerous object. If the notables had offered an interpretation and it failed, the failure would be theirs. If they had offered a grim one and it proved wrong, the offence would be worse. Declaring the dream meaningless costs them nothing, protects them from error, and closes the enquiry in a single sentence. The Qur'an does not accuse them of this calculation, but it does not need to; the structure of the reply is sufficient evidence. This is the same posture the household adopted in verse 35, where people who had seen the proofs chose the option that ended the matter rather than the one that followed it. Twice in the Egyptian section the chapter shows an establishment resolving a problem by declaring that no problem exists.

**Taʾwīl, the Word They Claim Not to Possess**

The term they use for interpretation is *taʾwīl*, from a root meaning to bring a thing back to its origin. In Qur'anic usage it covers the interpretation of dreams, the explanation of events whose meaning is not apparent — as when Moses promises to explain what his companion could not bear patiently (18:87) — and the handling of verses whose sense is not self-evident, which 3:7 assigns to God alone while noting that those with deviant hearts pursue their own. In later Islamic usage the same word came to designate the interior hermeneutic of a text, set alongside *tafsīr*, its outward exposition, though the distinction was never rigid and al-Ṭabarī titled his commentary with *taʾwīl* rather than *tafsīr*. The notables' sentence therefore denies competence in a discipline with a settled name and a recognised method.

**Experts Failing Is the Point of the Verse**

Structurally the chapter needs this answer. If the court had produced a reading, Joseph would have had no route out of the prison and no route into the palace. The sūrah arranges the failure before arranging the reminder: first the council disqualifies the dream, then the cupbearer remembers the man who could not have been disqualified. The order matters because it establishes that Joseph's interpretation is not one opinion among several but the only one offered anywhere in Egypt. When it arrives it meets no competitor, and its verification over the following fifteen years is therefore uncontested. The verse is the mechanism by which a prisoner becomes indispensable.

The failure also disposes of an objection a later reader might raise. Egypt in this period had a professional class of dream interpreters, and the biblical account shows the king summoning all the magicians and wise men of the country for the same purpose (Genesis 41:8). Their inability is reported in both traditions, and the Qur'an's version is the more pointed because it records their reasoning rather than simply their silence. They did not fail for want of effort; they applied a settled classification and reached a settled conclusion. The chapter thereby locates the gap that Joseph fills not in the court's diligence but in the limits of its discipline, which no amount of further effort inside that discipline would have overcome.

**The Dream Was Not Confused at All**

The reader knows something the council does not. The vision had a structure — two series, one number, one relationship between the terms — and its content was about to determine the fate of a country and, through that country, of a family in Canaan. Calling it *aḍghāth* was not a modest admission of limits but an error with consequences: it would have left Egypt unprepared for a famine it could have survived, and it would have left Joseph in a cell for the rest of his life. The chapter allows the error to stand uncorrected in the mouths of the men who made it, and corrects it only by showing what happens next. That is its method throughout. False accounts are not argued with; they are outlived.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:45

> ˹Finally,˺ the surviving ex-prisoner remembered ˹Joseph˺ after a long time and said, “I will tell you its interpretation, so send me forth ˹to Joseph˺.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Memory Returns at the Exact Point of Need**

The verse begins with the cupbearer remembering, and the timing is not incidental. He does not remember when Joseph's own need was greatest, in the years immediately after the release. He remembers when a king has been told by his entire council that his dream means nothing and nobody in Egypt has an answer. The word used for the interval is *ummah*, which carries the sense of a span of time and, in this construction, of a period of forgetting. The commentators give its length as the years of Joseph's imprisonment, commonly seven. What the verse presents is not a gradual recollection but a memory surfacing under pressure, in the one moment when it can do something. The timing is the whole point. A recollection arriving years earlier would have been sentiment; arriving now it is an instrument, and the verse lets the reader watch a private guilt turn into a public remedy. The same delay that punished Joseph equips the one man whose forgetfulness caused it, and equips him at the hour when the court has run out of answers.

**The Man Who Forgot Is the Man Who Remembers**

The chapter keeps its instruments consistent. The person whose forgetfulness cost Joseph years is the person whose recollection ends them, and no other candidate is available. The cupbearer has the only credential that matters: he has watched an interpretation given in a cell come true in his own life. That is what makes his testimony to the king persuasive rather than merely anecdotal, and the reports describe him as arguing from his own experience — the prisoner told me what would happen to me, and it happened. The sūrah does not moralise the coincidence. It records it, and leaves the reader to notice that the failure and its repair belong to the same man, which is a commoner arrangement in providence than in fiction.

**"I Will Tell You Its Interpretation" Claims Nothing for Himself**

The phrasing is careful. He does not say he can interpret the dream; he says he will bring the interpretation. The knowledge belongs to someone else and his own contribution is to go and get it. That distinction protects him in front of a king who has just been disappointed by experts: he is not offering a competing opinion but a source. It also does justice to Joseph, since the cupbearer's whole claim rests on the fact that another man's interpretation proved accurate. The sūrah's interest in accuracy runs through its minor characters. A man who had every incentive to present himself as the interpreter does not do so, and the reason is that his credibility depends on the truth of what he says.

**"Send Me Forth" Returns Him to the Prison**

The request is a commission. The freed man asks to be sent back to the place he left, on an errand, at the king's expense and with the king's authority. The movement is the verse's quiet reversal: the prison, which was where Joseph's story ended, becomes the place the state sends to for answers. Nothing in the text describes the journey or the arrival; verse 46 opens inside the cell with the cupbearer already speaking. The elision is typical. The chapter skips what its audience can supply and keeps what it needs — the address, the dream restated, and the answer. The ellipsis has a further effect: it denies Joseph any scene of vindication at the precise moment the reader wants one. He is let out of the narrative only to be put back into a cell, and the journey outward is never shown. The chapter reserves his public clearing for later, when it will come from the women's own mouths and from a king's enquiry rather than from an opened door.

**Why the Story Waits Years for This Moment**

The delay between verse 42 and verse 45 is the longest single gap in the narrative, and it is doing three things at once. It keeps Joseph in prison long enough for the release to be an event rather than a formality. It brings the famine close enough that an interpretation has immediate practical value, which is what converts a prisoner into a minister. And it establishes that Joseph's rise was not arranged by anyone who loved him: his father is a thousand miles away, his brothers believe him dead, the household that cleared him has forgotten him, and the one man who owed him a word forgot it for years. When the elevation arrives in verse 56 it is described as God's act, and the preceding verses have removed every human alternative.

**The Verse Restates the Dream in the Next Line**

One structural feature deserves notice. The cupbearer does not summarise the dream or refer the king to it; verse 46 reproduces the whole thing again in his mouth, image for image, number for number. The repetition is unusual in a narrative that elsewhere compresses years into clauses. Its function is to put the dream before the interpreter exactly as it was before the king, so that the reader can compare the question with the answer without any distortion introduced by an intermediary. The chapter is careful about evidence at every stage — a torn shirt, a wound in a hand, a stated dream — and here it protects the evidence by refusing to paraphrase it. That care matters because what follows is a policy document. Had the dream been summarised loosely, the storage plan would rest on hearsay; because it is quoted, the plan rests on a text the king himself supplied and can check against his own memory. Joseph's authority in the verses to come is built here, by the narrator's refusal to paraphrase.

**Providence Working Through Forgetfulness**

The mechanism of this verse is the same one named in verse 42. Satan caused a forgetting; the forgetting produced years in prison; the years placed Joseph in Egypt at the moment the country needed an interpreter; the memory then released him into the office. Every link in that chain depends on the failure of a human memory, and the Qur'an describes the failure without softening it. This is the sūrah's theology in miniature. Human beings are unreliable, the unreliability has costs that fall on the innocent, and the costs are nevertheless absorbed into an arrangement that nobody involved designed. The verse asks the reader to hold both halves at once: Joseph lost years to a man's forgetfulness, and Egypt gained its grain supply through the same man's recollection.

There is a further consequence worth stating. Because the release comes through a servant's memory rather than through a petition, Joseph's freedom is owed to nobody in a position of power. No patron claims him, no benefactor expects a return, and no courtier can say he made the man. The only debts outstanding when he walks out are to God, who turned the scheme away from him (12:34), and to a cupbearer who remembered late. That arrangement is what allows the following verses to describe his elevation as an act of divine establishment rather than as a reward for political service, and it is why he can ask for the storehouses in verse 55 without appearing to solicit a favour from anyone.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:46

> ˹He said,˺ “Joseph, O man of truth! Interpret for us ˹the dream of˺ seven fat cows eaten up by seven skinny ones; and seven green ears of grain and ˹seven˺ others dry, so that I may return to the people and let them know.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Title He Is Given in a Prison Cell**

*Yūsufu ayyuhā al-ṣiddīq* — Joseph, O man of truth. The word *ṣiddīq* is an intensive form denoting a person for whom truthfulness is a settled characteristic rather than an occasional act. It is the same quality the family witness attributed to him in verse 27 and the same one the ʿAzīz's wife will confirm before the king in verse 51. Three attestations, separated by years and given by people with every reason to say otherwise: a relative of the accuser, a fellow prisoner who watched him in confinement, and the woman who prosecuted him. The commentators note two grounds for the cupbearer's use of the title here — Joseph interpreted his own dream truthfully, and he never told a lie in all the time they shared the cell. Both grounds are experiential. Nobody in Egypt has testified to Joseph's character on the basis of hearsay.

**The Dream Is Restated in Full, Not Summarised**

The messenger reproduces the vision image by image and number by number: seven fat cows eaten by seven lean, seven green ears and seven dry. He does not abbreviate, does not offer his own reading and does not ask a general question. The precision serves two purposes. It guarantees that Joseph is answering the dream the king actually had rather than a version of it, and it allows the reader to verify the interpretation against the original when it arrives in the next verse. The chapter's care with evidence extends to its messengers. A man sent on an errand of state repeats what he was told word for word, which is what makes the answer that follows admissible.

**"Interpret for Us" Speaks on Behalf of a Government**

The request is plural. The messenger is not asking for himself; he carries the question of a king and a council that has already failed to answer it. That plural changes the character of the exchange. In verses 36 to 41 two prisoners asked a fellow prisoner about their own fates, and the answer concerned two men. Here the state asks, and the answer will concern a country's food supply for fifteen years. The sūrah marks the shift by the register of the speech: no sermon, no theological preamble, no reference to creed. Joseph is addressed as an expert by an official envoy, and he responds as one. The envoy's commission is what lifts the exchange above a personal favour. He has been sent, he is expected to come back with something, and what he brings will be reported to a court. Joseph is therefore speaking into a record, and the chapter places his first official act not in the palace but in the cell, addressed to a messenger who has no idea whom he is addressing.

**"So That I May Return to the People and Let Them Know"**

The clause states the messenger's objective, and the commentators read it in two directions. The narrower reading is that the people — the king and his council — should know the interpretation itself. The broader adds that they should know Joseph's standing and knowledge, and therefore release him. Both are supported by the sequel, since the king's immediate response in verse 50 is to summon the man rather than merely to record the answer. What the clause establishes is that the errand has a second purpose from the start. The cupbearer is not only fetching information; he is presenting a person, and the presentation is what will move Joseph from the prison to the palace. The phrasing tells us how the cupbearer intends to account for himself at court. He is not laying claim to the interpretation; he is claiming to know the man who can give it. That distinction is what allows Joseph's words to reach the king unfiltered and in the first person, carrying the authority of their author rather than of a nervous intermediary.

**Joseph Sees Two Opportunities and Takes Both**

The commentators observe that Joseph recognised in this request both a route out of the prison and a chance to serve a country facing an agricultural catastrophe, and that he treated the two as compatible rather than as competing. Nothing in the text suggests he negotiated his release in exchange for the interpretation; he gives the answer and adds nothing about his own case, and the demand about his case comes later and separately (12:50). That separation is what makes verse 50 significant. He does not bargain with the interpretation. He answers the question as asked, and only afterwards insists that the record about him be corrected before he will walk out of the cell.

**The Answer That Follows Is a Programme, Not a Reading**

Verses 47 to 49 will contain almost no symbolism. Joseph does not explain that the cows signify years, although the identification is implicit and the biblical account states it explicitly. He moves directly to what should be sown, what should be stored, what should be eaten, what will be consumed and what will follow. The interpretation and the policy arrive in a single continuous statement, which is why the king's response is to appoint him rather than to thank him. A dream that has been decoded produces information; a dream that has been decoded into a fifteen-year storage plan produces an office, and the chapter understands the difference. It also fixes the sequence, which is easy to lose. The plan is delivered before the appointment, and it is delivered with no condition or bargain attached to it. Joseph names the years, the method and the discipline required, and only afterwards is he given authority to carry out what he has already designed — so the promotion reads as ratification rather than as reward.

**A Different Audience, A Different Content**

The contrast with verses 37 to 41 is exact and it deserves to be stated plainly. When two condemned men asked Joseph for an interpretation, he spent four verses on the oneness of God before answering them. When an envoy of the king asks for the same service, he answers without a word of creed. Nothing in the chapter suggests inconsistency. The two prisoners were idolaters with nothing to lose and years ahead of them; the envoy is a servant of a government with a famine approaching and no time. Joseph preaches where preaching can be received and serves where service is required. The sūrah presents both as prophetic conduct, and it leaves the reader to notice that the same man adjusted his speech to his audience without adjusting his convictions.

The title the envoy uses also tells the reader something about what happened in the prison. *Ṣiddīq* is not a form of address one official applies to a stranger, and it is not a compliment a servant invents on a king's behalf. It is what a man calls someone whose word he has watched tested over years — a fellow inmate whose interpretations came true and whose conduct in confinement matched his claims. The cupbearer has carried that assessment out of the cell and into the palace, and the chapter treats it as evidence: the same mouth that once forgot a message now delivers a character reference, and the reference is the reason a king will send for a prisoner he has never met.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:47

> Joseph replied, “You will plant ˹grain˺ for seven consecutive years, leaving in the ear whatever you will harvest, except for the little you will eat.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Answer Opens With an Instruction**

Joseph does not begin by decoding the symbols. The first thing he says is what the country will be doing and what it should do with the result: you will sow for seven successive years, and what you reap you should leave standing in its ear. The interpretive work — cows are years, ears are harvests — is never spelled out, because it does not need to be. A reader holding verse 43 and verse 47 side by side performs the identification without assistance, and that is presumably the intention. The answer's form is that of an agricultural directive rather than an oracle, and the change of form is the change that matters: Egypt is not being told what its dream means so much as what its next fifteen years require.

**Seven Years Described as Successive**

The word attached to the seven years denotes continuation, one after another without interruption. The specification is not padding. An agricultural cycle with good years scattered through bad ones can be managed locally, by household storage and by trade between regions. Seven uninterrupted years of plenty followed by seven uninterrupted years of scarcity cannot. The consecutive character of the sequence is what makes central planning necessary, since no individual farmer and no single province can bridge that gap alone. Joseph's first contribution is therefore to identify the shape of the problem, and the shape is temporal rather than quantitative. Stress on sequence closes off the comforting readings available to a court. Were the years interchangeable, a bad harvest could be met from a good neighbour; because they run consecutively there is no neighbour to borrow from, and the entire famine must be provisioned out of the entire plenty. This is why the answer to the king's dream is a storage policy and not a prayer for rain.

**"Leave in the Ear" Is a Storage Technique**

The instruction is specific and agronomic. Grain kept in its ear and husk is protected from damp, from insects and from spoilage in ways that threshed grain is not, and it can be stored for long periods under ordinary conditions. The commentators take the phrase in exactly this sense: store the harvest as it comes off the field, rather than processing it immediately. That detail matters because it makes the plan executable by a society without specialised facilities. No new technology is required, no unusual construction, no imported expertise. What is required is a decision to withhold a large quantity of food from consumption and from threshing for seven years running, which is a decision only a government with real authority can enforce. The technique is also chosen for durability rather than for symbolism. Grain left standing on the stalk keeps longer and resists the damp and the pests that attack threshed stores, so the instruction fits an economy whose storage capacity was limited to what could be protected in bulk. It is the kind of detail that survives only in a plan meant to be carried out.

**"Except the Little You Eat" Imposes Rationing During Plenty**

The exception is the hardest part of the plan and Joseph states it in the same breath as the abundance. In seven years of exceptional fertility, the country is to eat little. The discipline is not a technicality. Every agricultural society facing surplus is subject to expansion of consumption in good years — more bread, more livestock, more feasting, more planting of luxury crops — and the expansion is precisely what destroys the margin needed for the bad years. The instruction therefore regulates appetite rather than logistics. The commentators note that the first phase is described as a time of great fertility, which makes the command to eat little counter-intuitive and, for that reason, the real test of the plan. Rationing during abundance demands an authority that scarcity never supplies by itself, because nothing visible justifies it at the time it is imposed. The plan therefore rests on belief in a projection nobody can yet see, and the chapter is candid that the discipline required is hardest precisely when it looks least necessary — in a year when the harvest is coming in fat.

**A Prediction and a Policy in One Sentence**

The verse contains a statement of fact and a statement of obligation, and does not separate them. You will sow for seven years is prediction; leave what you harvest in its ear is instruction. The two are joined because the prediction is only useful as the ground of the instruction. A ruler told that seven good years are coming receives news; a ruler told that seven good years are coming and that the harvest must be retained receives a policy. This is what distinguishes Joseph's answer from the one the court declined to give in verse 44 and from the kind of interpretation a diviner would supply. The knowledge is delivered in the form in which it can be acted on, and the acting is included in the delivery.

**The Plan Requires an Office, and the Chapter Knows It**

Nothing in this verse can be carried out by the person speaking it. A prisoner can describe a storage programme; he cannot enforce one across a country for seven years. The demand implied by the instruction will be met in verse 55, when Joseph asks to be placed over the storehouses of the land on the grounds that he is a reliable and skilled keeper. The sequence is worth noting because it reverses the usual order: the competence is demonstrated first, and the request for authority comes afterwards, grounded in the demonstration. The sūrah is describing a form of political legitimacy in which office follows from a plan that has been stated publicly and can be checked against events.

The plan also has a distributional consequence the verse leaves unstated. Retaining the harvest in the ear for seven years removes grain from ordinary markets, which raises prices in the very years when prices would otherwise be lowest. Egypt's farmers will therefore experience the programme as hardship during plenty and as rescue during scarcity, and only the second half of that experience will be visible to them at the time. Joseph's instruction asks a whole country to accept a cost it cannot yet justify, on the word of a prisoner. That is the political difficulty the following verses resolve, and it explains why the office requested later is not merely administrative but coercive.

**No Tax Is Imposed, and the Omission Is Notable**

The biblical account of the same consultation has Joseph propose that the crown take a fifth of the produce during the seven plentiful years and store it under royal authority (Genesis 41:34–36). The Qur'anic version contains no fraction, no levy and no reference to royal appropriation. What it prescribes is retention in the ear and restraint in eating, both of which fall on the producers themselves as much as on the state. Whether the difference reflects a different tradition or a deliberate choice cannot be settled from the text, but the effect is clear: the Qur'an's Joseph gives Egypt a plan for managing abundance rather than a plan for taxing it, and the authority he later asks for is over storehouses rather than over revenue.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:48

> Then after that will come seven years of great hardship which will consume whatever you have saved, except the little you will store ˹for seed˺.

**Expanded Commentary**

**"After That" Makes the Sequence Inevitable**

The second phase is not presented as a possibility, a risk or a warning contingent on behaviour. It comes *after that* — the phrase fixes it in the calendar established by the previous verse. Nothing Egypt does in the seven good years will prevent the seven bad ones, and nothing it neglects will cause them. The certainty is what gives the plan its urgency. A hazard that may arrive can be insured against gradually; a hazard that will arrive on a known date requires preparation to begin immediately and to continue for seven years without visible reward. Joseph's answer supplies both the date and the inevitability, and the two together are what make his instruction actionable rather than advisory.

**The Years Themselves Devour**

The verb is the one the dream used. Seven lean cows *ate* seven fat ones; now seven hard years *eat* what has been stored. The agent of the consumption has been transferred from animals to time. That transfer is the whole of the interpretation, and the verse performs it without announcing it. The reader who compares verse 43 with verse 48 can see exactly where the symbol was cashed: the devouring in the dream was not a picture of animals but of years eating surplus. The precision of the correspondence is what makes the interpretation convincing to the king and to the reader, and it is achieved without a single explanatory sentence. The correspondence also settles the objection that Joseph invented the famine to advance himself. The dream belonged to the king and reached the court before any interpretation existed, so the order is fixed by the narrative itself: the vision comes first, the reading follows, and the reading is then tested against years that have not yet arrived and cannot be arranged.

**"Except a Little That You Store" Preserves the Possibility of Recovery**

The exception at the end of the verse is small and decisive. Of everything saved during the good years, almost all will be consumed; a residue will remain, and the residue is what makes the following year possible. The commentators gloss it as seed corn — grain held back not for eating but for planting. The distinction between consumption stock and seed stock is one of the oldest in agriculture, and its presence here shows that the plan is not merely about surviving a famine but about restarting after it. A country that eats its seed grain in the last hard year will not recover in the fifteenth; it will continue to starve through the relief. Joseph's programme therefore has a third component nobody asked for: the protection of the means of production inside the disaster.

**Seven Years Is Beyond the Reach of Ordinary Preparation**

The scale of the hardship deserves to be stated plainly. A single bad year is absorbed by reserves, by neighbouring harvests and by migration. Three or four are survived with difficulty by a well-governed country. Seven consecutive years across an entire region — the famine reaches Canaan as well, which is why the brothers eventually appear in Egypt — exceed every ordinary mechanism of coping. Nothing short of a centralised store accumulated through seven years of abundance can bridge it. This is why the plan requires an office with authority over the whole country's grain, and why the king's response to the interpretation is not gratitude but appointment. Gratitude would have been the right response to a warning; appointment is the response to a plan. The king has been handed not only a date but a method, and a method implies an administrator capable of holding it against seven years of public appetite. The office follows the interpretation because the interpretation was already an exercise of the office.

**The Hardship Is Not Presented as Punishment**

The verse assigns no moral cause to the famine. No sin is named, no warning is issued, no connection is drawn between the Egyptians' conduct and the coming scarcity. The famine is agrarian and meteorological, and it arrives because that is what the dream signified. That treatment is unusual in the Qur'an, where deprivation is often described as a consequence sent to a people. Here it is a fact of the calendar, disclosed in advance so that it can be managed. The distinction matters for how the chapter understands providence: not every affliction is a judgement, and an affliction that is not a judgement still requires a response, and still rewards foresight. This is the practical teaching of the passage. Egypt's famine is nowhere traced to Egyptian wrongdoing, and no repentance is required as its remedy; what is required is grain, discipline, and a man prepared to say eat little in a year of plenty. The chapter keeps the meaning of a trial and the management of a trial clearly apart, and it asks for competence in the second.

**The Two Sevens Are Defined by Their Relation**

The second set of seven years has no content of its own. Nothing is said about what the hardship will feel like, how it will distribute itself across the country, or what it will do to the population beyond eating the stores. The years are described entirely by what they consume. The asymmetry is instructive. Plenty has a positive content — sowing, harvesting, storing, eating a little. Scarcity has only a negative one — the removal of what plenty produced. That is an accurate account of famine as an economic event: it creates nothing, it withdraws. The chapter's plan is built on the asymmetry, since the only available defence against a purely negative seven years is a positive seven that precedes them.

**What the Verse Asks of a Ruler**

The political content of these three verses is a theory of government in miniature. The state's task in good years is not to expand consumption, celebrate, or distribute the surplus, but to withhold it. The task in bad years is to release what was withheld in measured quantities, keeping enough seed to restart. Both tasks are unpopular, and neither produces a visible benefit during the period in which it must be performed. A ruler who stores for seven years will be resented for it by people who cannot see the famine coming, and the only reason to do it is that someone knows. That is the function the chapter assigns to revelation in public life: not to supply policy in detail but to supply the knowledge of timing on which policy depends.

The verse also fixes the limits of what preparation can achieve. Seven years of scarcity will consume whatever has been saved. The stores do not prevent the famine; they do not shorten it; they do not soften it. They allow a population to survive an event that is otherwise unsurvivable, and that is all. Nothing in the chapter suggests that a sufficient stockpile could have averted the hardship itself. The distinction between mitigation and prevention is drawn sharply here, and the plan Joseph has proposed belongs entirely on the mitigation side — which is why it can be delivered by a prisoner to a government and still be the most useful thing said in Egypt that year.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:49

> Then after that will come a year in which people will receive abundant rain and they will press ˹oil and wine˺.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Fifteenth Year That Was Not in the Dream**

The king saw fourteen items: seven fat cows, seven lean, seven green ears, seven dry. Joseph's answer accounts for all of them and then adds one more. Nothing in the imagery of verse 43 corresponds to a year of rain and pressing, and no interpretation of the cows or the ears could have produced it. The additional year is therefore not derived from the data given; it is knowledge supplied from elsewhere. The exegetes have long noted the point, because it establishes something the earlier verses could not: Joseph's information exceeds the dream. A skilled interpreter can decode symbols a dreamer supplies. Only someone receiving information can add a term the dreamer never saw. The addition is also a testable one. Joseph is not extending a symbolic pattern; he is introducing a specific claim about weather for which the dream supplied no basis. If the year of rain does not arrive, the interpretation is falsified along with the entire policy built upon it, and the chapter permits that exposure rather than insulating its prophet from it.

**"People Will Be Given Rain" Uses the Passive Deliberately**

The verb *yughāthu al-nās* is passive, and the noun behind it — *ghayth* — is the specific word for rain that comes as relief, rain sent to a people in need. The passive leaves the agent unnamed while the vocabulary makes him obvious. The relief is given, not produced; it arrives, and it arrives after seven years in which no human management could have created it. The verse closes the programme with an act that has no place in the plan. Joseph's storage scheme covers the first fourteen years. The fifteenth is handled by someone else, and the wording records the fact without comment. That is the sūrah's characteristic way of marking the limit of human technique. The passive also keeps attention where the plan requires it. Storage can be organised, measured and enforced by human decision; the ending of a drought cannot. By separating the two, Joseph's speech tells the court exactly which parts of the coming fifteen years are theirs to manage and which are not, and asks them to act on the first without presuming upon the second.

**"They Will Press" Returns the Cupbearer's Verb**

The word for pressing is from the root *ʿ-ṣ-r*, and the chapter has already used it. The first of the two prisoners said: *“I dreamt I was pressing wine”* (12:36). The verb that introduced Joseph's public career in Egypt — the dream whose interpretation restored a servant to his post and eventually carried a message to a king — reappears at the end of his forecast as the sign of national recovery. The echo is exact, and it does structural work. The small private vision and the large public one are joined by the same action, which is how the chapter ties the prison to the palace. A reader who notices the repetition hears the whole Egyptian section as one connected movement rather than two separate episodes.

**Two Readings of the Final Verb**

The commentators record a minority reading in which the verb is understood not as pressing but as receiving relief, drawing on a related noun meaning a year of abundance and ease. On that reading the clause would simply restate the rain: people will be given relief. The majority reading, reflected in the translation, keeps the literal sense of pressing oil and wine, which has the advantage of specifying what the abundance consists of. The disagreement is worth reporting because it shows how the exegetes handled an ambiguous term: one side preferred the concrete agricultural image, the other preferred a general statement of deliverance, and the first was judged stronger because it continues the pattern of concrete images that runs through the whole forecast.

**Hope Is Placed at the End of the Sequence, Not the Beginning**

The order of the three verses is a rhetorical choice as much as a chronological one. Seven years of instruction, seven years of consumption, then one year of rain. The relief is last, and it is the shortest of the three phases — a single year set against fourteen. A forecast arranged to comfort would have led with the recovery. This one ends with it, which means the hearers spend the whole of the message inside the difficulty and emerge from it only at the close. The effect on the king's court must have been considerable: a plan that requires seven years of restraint, survives seven years of famine, and is guaranteed to end in abundance. The guarantee is the only thing that makes the restraint acceptable.

**The Year of Rain Is Outside the Plan, and the Brothers Are Inside the Famine**

The timing of the narrative depends on this verse. Joseph's brothers arrive in Egypt during the years of scarcity, seeking grain from the stores he controls (12:58). Had the fifteenth year come earlier, they would have had no reason to travel and the recognition scene would never have occurred. The chapter is arranging a calendar. The famine is the mechanism that brings a family from Canaan to an Egyptian granary, and the year of relief is placed after that mechanism has done its work. Nothing in the text says so. The reader assembles it from the sequence, which is the method the sūrah has used from the beginning: give the facts in order and let the arrangement speak. Here that method produces a striking result: the one element of the future Joseph cannot control is the element that closes his forecast, and the sūrah leaves it standing at the end of the verse with nothing built upon it. The plan terminates exactly at the limit of what planning can reach, and the reader is shown where that limit falls.

**The Forecast Is Verified by Events Nobody Controls**

Over the following years the prediction will be tested in the only way a forecast can be tested. The sowing happens, the stores accumulate, the famine arrives on schedule, the grain is released, and the brothers of a man sold for twenty dirhams arrive to buy food from him. The verification is complete and public, and it is the reason the Qur'an can describe the interpretation as knowledge taught by God rather than as skill acquired by study. Verse 49 supplies the final element of that verification: the year of rain was not in the dream, was stated years in advance, and arrived. The chapter does not report the arrival. It does not need to.

The single year also sets a limit on the programme Joseph has described. His plan covers fourteen years and no further; nothing in it provides for a second famine or for a generation after the one that survived the first. The year of rain is where human management ends, and the verse marks the boundary without comment. A reader who expects a permanent solution to scarcity from a prophetic forecast will not find one here. What the chapter offers is something narrower and more useful: knowledge of when the hardship is coming, a technique for surviving it, and a fixed point beyond which the technique is no longer required. Each of the three is stated in the space of three verses.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:50

> The King ˹then˺ said, “Bring him to me.” When the messenger came to him, Joseph said, “Go back to your master and ask him about the case of the women who cut their hands. Surely my Lord has ˹full˺ knowledge of their cunning.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Release Offered and Not Taken**

The king's order is unqualified: bring him to me. The messenger arrives at the prison with the authority to empty it, and the prisoner refuses to leave. Nothing in the verse explains the refusal as a negotiation, a demand for terms or a display of pride. Joseph does not ask for an office, for money, for an apology or for the punishment of anyone. He asks that a question be put: what was the matter of the women who cut their hands? The request is for an enquiry, and until the enquiry happens he stays where he is. A man who has spent years in a cell on a charge that was disproved inside his own household declines freedom because freedom without correction would leave the record as it stands.

**The Prophet's Own Comment on the Refusal**

The tradition treats this moment as the high point of Joseph's patience. Abū Hurayrah reports the Prophet ﷺ saying, after mentioning Abraham's request for reassurance and Lot's wish for a strong support: if I had remained in prison as long as Joseph did, I would have answered the caller (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 3372). The comparison is in Joseph's favour and is explicit. The remark identifies what the refusal cost and what it required: a man who had been held for years, offered release at last, chooses to remain inside until his name has been cleared publicly. The statement is not a criticism of anyone else's weakness; it is a measure of the standard Joseph met, delivered by the one person in the tradition qualified to set it. That is why the comment sits beside the narrative rather than against it. It honours the length of the waiting by refusing to pretend that anyone else would have endured it, and it locates Joseph's distinction not in his purity at the locked door but in his patience across all the years that followed the accusation.

**"Ask Him About the Case of the Women" Names the Evidence**

*Mā bālu* — what is the state of, what happened with — introduces the specific matter to be investigated: the women who cut their hands. The reference is to the banquet of verse 31, where the female establishment of the city wounded themselves on seeing Joseph and declared him an angel rather than a man capable of indecency. Joseph chooses that evidence deliberately. The torn shirt of verse 26 was examined inside a household and never published. The husband's verdict of verse 28 was spoken in a room nobody else entered. The banquet was attended by dozens of women of standing, all of whom saw the same thing and all of whom said the same sentence aloud. He points the king to the only testimony in Egypt that was given in public.

**Why the Women, and Not the Wife Alone**

The request is plural. Joseph does not ask for the ʿAzīz's wife to be interrogated, although she is the person who accused him and the only one who can confess. He asks about the women as a group, which includes her. The reason is evidentiary: a single recantation can be explained as pressure, as fear or as favour, while the agreement of a whole group cannot. The chapter shows the strategy working in the following verse, where the women answer first and the wife answers after them. Her confession arrives inside a chorus of exculpation rather than alone, which is what makes it conclusive. Joseph has asked for the strongest available form of testimony, and he has asked for it while standing in a prison he could have walked out of.

**"My Lord Has Full Knowledge of Their Cunning"**

The word is *kayd*, the chapter's term for concealed planning, used of the brothers in verse 5, of the women in verse 28, of the same women in Joseph's prayer at verse 33, and of God's turning of it away at verse 34. Here it is applied to the whole female establishment collectively. The clause has a double function. It states that nothing about the affair was hidden from God, which is the ground on which Joseph has lived since the well. And it declines to pronounce judgement himself. He does not call the women liars, does not describe what they did, and does not ask for their punishment. He refers their planning to a knower and asks a king to ask a question. Both halves of that sentence are necessary to it. Without the appeal to God's knowledge the demand for enquiry would read as an accusation; without the demand the appeal would be a private consolation. Together they allow Joseph to hold his case open before God while pursuing it, in the same breath, before a human court that can actually do something about it.

**Vindication, Not Revenge**

The distinction governs everything that follows. Joseph's demand produces an enquiry whose outcome is a public statement of his truthfulness (12:51) and a woman's admission of her own guilt. It produces no execution, no confiscation, no dismissal and no humiliation of anyone. When the brothers eventually stand before him, decades later and in a famine, he does not use his power to settle the older account either: *“There is no blame on you today”* (12:92). The pattern is consistent. Joseph seeks correction of the record and nothing beyond it, and the sūrah presents that restraint as strength rather than as weakness — the strength of a man who does not need another person to suffer in order to be free. It is also a strategic choice. A demand for punishment would have made his release conditional on someone's ruin and given the court every reason to delay; a demand for the truth made it conditional only on an enquiry, which a king can order at no cost to himself. Joseph asks for the cheapest available form of justice, and he receives it.

**The Cost of the Demand Is Paid in Full**

The verse does not say how long the enquiry took, and the narrative moves directly from the demand to its outcome. What is certain is that Joseph remained in the prison until the king's investigation was complete, since he is summoned again in verse 54 after the women have testified. He paid for his insistence with the only currency he had. The chapter records the payment without remarking on it, which is how it records all of Joseph's costs: the well, the beating, the sale for twenty dirhams, the years in the cell, and now the additional time bought by a refusal to leave it. The reward arrives in the next few verses, and it arrives in a form the verse has already earned. The form matters as much as the arrival. He is not merely freed; he is cleared, and cleared by the testimony of the women who ruined his reputation and by the confession of the woman who accused him. What he waited for is precisely what he receives — a public record of innocence rather than an administrative release from custody.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:51

> The King asked ˹the women˺, “What did you get when you tried to seduce Joseph?” They replied, “Allah forbid! We know nothing indecent about him.” Then the Chief Minister’s wife admitted, “Now the truth has come to light. It was I who tried to seduce him, and he is surely truthful.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The King Asks the Question Joseph Specified**

The enquiry happens exactly as the prisoner requested. The king summons the women and puts the matter to them, using a form of words that asks for an account: what was your affair, what did you arrive at, when you sought to seduce Joseph. The verse does not describe the setting, the number of women present or the procedure, but it records that the state conducted the investigation rather than leaving it to the household. That transfer is the substance of Joseph's demand. The case had been settled inside a private residence by a husband with an interest in silence (12:29). It is now reopened before a ruler with no such interest, and the sūrah treats the change of forum as the change that matters.

**The Question Itself Presumes the Attempt**

The king's wording is worth examining. He asks what they got *when they tried to seduce Joseph*, using the same verb the narrator used in verse 23 and Joseph used in verse 26. The attempt is taken for granted in the question; what is asked is its outcome. That framing is either the result of information reaching the king from the cupbearer's errand, or a deliberate device that leaves the women no room to deny the premise. Either way it disposes of the version of events the household had maintained for years. Nobody in the room can now claim that nothing happened, and the only available answers concern who did what — which is the question the torn shirt already answered in private and that the banquet answered in public.

**The Women's Answer Clears Him in One Sentence**

*Ḥāsha lillāh, God forbid; we know nothing indecent about him.* The exclamation is the same one they used at the banquet (12:31), and its function is the same: to register that the suggestion is unthinkable. What follows is a statement about knowledge rather than opinion — we know no evil of him — and it is spoken collectively by the women of the city's establishment. Their testimony is not that Joseph was attractive or that they understood the ʿAzīz's wife. It is that they possess no information damaging to him. Since the accusation against him originated in this same social circle and circulated among these same households, their joint denial removes its entire social basis. The verse therefore does more than acquit him; it withdraws the report.

**"Now the Truth Has Come to Light"**

The wife of the Chief Minister speaks after the others, and her opening words are an idiom of arrival: *al-āna ḥaṣḥaṣa al-ḥaqq*, now the truth has become fixed, settled, established. The verb carries the sense of something that has come to rest after movement, and its use here marks the end of a long displacement. The truth about this affair had been available since verse 28, when a husband examined a shirt and announced the result to two people in a room. What verse 51 supplies is the same truth in public, before the king, in the mouth of the person who displaced it. Her confession is complete: she names herself as the one who sought the seduction, and she names Joseph as truthful. Nothing is qualified, nothing is shared with him, and no mitigating circumstance is offered.

**A Love That Has Changed Its Nature**

The commentators track a progression through her three appearances in the chapter. In verse 25 she protects herself by prosecuting him, which is desire preferring its own survival. In verse 32 she admits the attempt in front of forty women, which is desire seeking exoneration from scorn. Here she admits it before the king, with no advantage to be gained and every disadvantage to be borne, which the tradition reads as the point where her feeling ceases to be self-interested. Al-Maybudī's account is that her love, having begun as appetite, has by now become a recognition of his inward quality — the truthfulness she names in the same breath. Whether or not that reading is pressed, the structural fact stands: her last recorded statement about Joseph costs her everything and gains her nothing.

**The Testimony Is Given in His Absence**

Joseph is not in the room. He is still in the prison, waiting for the result of the enquiry he demanded.
