A father hearing this account learns that Egypt will not supply further grain unless a son travels there, and he is not told who imposed the condition or on what grounds.

**"Send Our Brother With Us"**

The request is direct and it names no one. The brother is identified only by relation, which is how the chapter refers to Benjamin throughout: never by name, always as the brother from their father (12:59), his brother (12:64), our brother (12:65), his full brother (12:77). The anonymity is a consistent feature of the Qur'anic account and it has an effect on the reader. A character without a name is defined entirely by his relations, and Benjamin's relation to Joseph is the one the narrative is building toward. The brothers use the same form of reference Joseph used, which means the request as they state it echoes the minister's wording exactly. The borrowing is deliberate and it does real work. By using the minister's own phrase they import his authority into their plea, so Jacob hears the request not as his sons' wish but as a condition imposed by the government that feeds them. What Joseph said to them in Egypt becomes their strongest argument in Canaan.

**"We Will Surely Be His Keepers"**

The assurance repeats the guarantee they gave about Joseph. In verses 12 to 14 they asked their father to send Joseph with them and undertook to protect him; the pledge failed, and the chapter recorded its failure in verse 16 with a false report and a bloodied shirt. The same undertaking is now offered for a second son, in the same emphatic form, by the same men, to the same father. The brothers do not acknowledge the earlier instance. The verse contains no reference to Joseph, no explanation of what has changed, and no admission that the previous guarantee was not kept. Jacob supplies all three in his answer. The claim is comprehensive, and that is its weakness. They undertake to protect him, to return him and to answer for him, in a household that has already watched them fail at precisely this. The verse lets the assurance stand at full strength so that the reply which follows can take it apart without needing to argue against anything weaker.

**The Verse Reports the Plea and Stops**

No reply is given here. Verse 63 ends with the brothers' assurance, and verse 64 opens with their father's response. The separation is a deliberate pause: a plea is stated in full, and the answer arrives in its own verse rather than inside the same sentence. The chapter frequently gives a single utterance its own verse when the utterance carries weight — verse 61 did the same with a promise of nine words. The effect is to let the assurance stand alone in front of the reader before Jacob dismantles it. A reader who has followed the story from verse 8 knows what is coming, and the pause allows the knowledge to arrive before the words do. Stopping there is a form of respect for the reader. The narrative does not need to rehearse the precedent, which has already been established, and the pause allows it to surface unaided. When Jacob speaks in the next verse the reader is ahead of the brothers, which is exactly the position the chapter wants for the scene that follows.

**The Rhetoric Is Structured, Not Spontaneous**

Three moves in three clauses: the loss, the remedy, the guarantee. First, we have been refused further supplies — establishing that a problem exists and that it is external. Second, send our brother with us so that we may obtain our measure — presenting the solution as a condition imposed by Egypt rather than a desire of theirs. Third, we will surely watch over him — anticipating the objection and answering it before it is raised. The structure is that of people who have rehearsed the conversation on the road home, and the chapter's silence about their rehearsal lets the reader infer it from the shape of the speech. Every element of the speech has a function and none is incidental. The provisions establish that the journey succeeded, the minister's condition explains why another son is needed, the pledge answers an objection before it is voiced. This is not a report delivered on arrival but a case prepared in advance and presented in order.

**What Is Left Out of the Report**

Several things the brothers know are absent from what they tell their father. They do not describe the minister — his age, his bearing, his treatment of them beyond the full measure. They do not mention the demand's peculiar form, a brother from their father, which ought to strike a parent as oddly specific. They do not report the refusal of access threatened in verse 60. And they say nothing about the money, which at this point may not yet have been discovered. The report is accurate and incomplete, and its incompleteness is what produces verse 64: Jacob answers the assurance offered and cannot answer arguments he has not been given. The omissions are the point. Nothing is said about the money returned, about the brother they lost, or about anything that might weaken the request; the speech contains only what supports it. Jacob is answering a curated account, and the chapter shows him answering the single part of it he can, because the rest has been withheld from him.

**The Famine Is the Unstated Premise of the Whole Speech**

Behind the three clauses lies a fact the brothers do not need to state because their father already lives inside it. Canaan is in the same scarcity as Egypt; the journey was necessary and the next one will be too. Verse 63 therefore does not argue that the family needs food. That is assumed. What it argues is that the food cannot be obtained again on the present terms, and that the change of terms requires Benjamin. The economy of the plea depends on the assumption, and the chapter relies on the reader having absorbed verses 47 to 49 well enough to supply it. A father hearing this speech is not weighing whether to feed his household; he is weighing whether feeding it costs him a son.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:64

> He responded, “Should I trust you with him as I once trusted you with his brother ˹Joseph˺? But ˹only˺ Allah is the best Protector, and He is the Most Merciful of the merciful.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question Rather Than a Refusal**

Jacob's answer opens with an interrogative: shall I entrust him to you except as I entrusted his brother to you before? The form is rhetorical and it does the work of a refusal without stating one. Nothing in the question asserts that Benjamin will be lost; it asks whether the precedent permits confidence. The chapter's method here is precise. A father who has been through this once does not need to argue, because the argument has already been conducted and settled by events. He needs only to name the precedent, and the naming is enough to make the sons' assurance in verse 63 sound like the assurance they gave in verse 12. The question also refuses the premise the plea rests on. His sons have presented themselves as changed men offering a new guarantee; by asking whether he should trust them as he trusted them before, Jacob declines to treat that guarantee as new. He files it with the previous one and lets the filing do the arguing for him.

**"His Brother" — Joseph Is Never Named Here**

The reference is relational throughout. Jacob does not say Joseph. He says *his brother*, meaning Benjamin's brother, and the phrasing carries a particular force: the son you once asked me to entrust to you was this boy's brother, and the outcome is known to both of us. The avoidance of the name is consistent with the chapter's practice — Benjamin is never named either — and it has an emotional register the commentators note. Jacob's speech about Joseph elsewhere is equally oblique: he will say *my grief* rather than my son's name in verse 84, and his eyes will go white with sorrow (12:84). A man who has not accepted a death does not use the dead person's name in an argument about a living child.

**"God Is the Best of Keepers"**

The second clause changes direction entirely. *Fa-Llāhu khayrun ḥāfiẓan* — God is the best of those who guard. The same noun was used of Joseph's self-description in verse 55, where he asked to be put over the storehouses as a *ḥafīẓ*, a careful keeper. Here the word is transferred to God, and the superlative form does the work: of all who keep a thing, God keeps it best. The clause is Jacob's way of releasing the matter. He cannot guard Benjamin himself, his sons have proved unreliable guardians, and the only remaining custody is divine. The commentators read this as trust in God combined with resignation of the whole affair to Him, which is what the wording supports. The superlative is doing quiet work. By naming God the best of keepers rather than a keeper among others, Jacob implicitly ranks his sons' undertaking beneath it, and that ranking is the whole of his objection. He never says they will fail; he says that if anyone is to guard the boy there is a better candidate, already present in his sentence.

**"The Most Merciful of the Merciful"**

The verse closes with *arḥam al-rāḥimīn*, a superlative of mercy that the Qur'an places in the mouths of prophets at moments of loss. Moses uses it after the golden calf, asking forgiveness for himself and his brother: *“You are the Most Merciful of the merciful”* (7:151). Joseph will use it at the end of this chapter, forgiving the brothers who sold him: *“May Allah forgive you! He is the Most Merciful of the merciful!”* (12:92). Three speakers, three occasions, one formula, and in each case the formula is uttered by someone who has been wronged or bereaved and has no remedy left. Jacob's use of it here places him inside that pattern before he has agreed to anything. The placement matters more than the assent it precedes. The name is offered not as a reason to let the boy go but as a description of the One he is trusting with the outcome, which allows Jacob to consent without ever claiming the journey is safe. He transfers the risk instead of minimising it, and states the transfer in a single phrase.

**Two Clauses That Pull Apart**

The verse contains reproach and surrender in immediate succession. The first half refuses confidence in the sons; the second half places the son in God's keeping. A reader might expect an intervening negotiation, a set of terms, or a refusal followed by persuasion. The chapter gives none. Jacob moves from an accusation to an act of trust in a single sentence, and the movement is what defines his faith in this story. He does not cease to know what his sons are capable of. He ceases to rely on his own capacity to prevent it. Verse 66 will show him imposing an oath anyway, which means the trust and the precaution are held together rather than resolved. The tension is never resolved and the chapter makes no attempt to resolve it. Jacob's reliance is total in his wording and partial in his conduct, and both halves are recorded without comment. The reader is given a believer who prays for protection and then requires an oath as security, with no explanation of why he needs both.

**Grief That Has Not Diminished Faith**

Between this verse and verse 84 Jacob will lose a second son, weep until his eyes turn white, and be told by his remaining family that he is going to die of grief. His answer there is the chapter's clearest statement of his position: he complains of his grief and sorrow to God alone and knows from God what they do not know (12:86). Verse 64 anticipates that stance. A man who says God is the best of keepers and the most merciful of the merciful, while refusing to trust his own children with a son, is holding two convictions that most people would find incompatible. The chapter presents the combination as faith rather than as contradiction, and the sequel — Joseph and Benjamin both alive in Egypt — vindicates it.

**The Reply Answers the Assurance, Not the Argument**

Jacob responds to *we will definitely watch over him* and to nothing else in verse 63. He does not address the shortage of grain, the need for the eleventh load, or the famine's duration. The omission is deliberate: the material case is not in dispute, and Jacob is not refusing because Egypt's demand is unreasonable. He is refusing because the guarantee offered is the same guarantee that failed before. When the brothers produce new evidence in verse 65 — the money returned, the family's need, an extra load easily obtained — Jacob still does not withdraw the objection. He converts it into an oath, which is a way of accepting the journey while recording his distrust of the travellers. The asymmetry is deliberate. Jacob has no answer to the economic case his sons have made, because the case is sound: without grain the household starves, and the minister has attached a condition to the grain. What he can answer is their reliability, and he answers it by consenting in the same breath as he records that he does not trust them.

**The Verse Names the Only Custody Jacob Accepts**

Between the two clauses sits an unstated judgement about human reliability. Jacob does not say his sons are liars, does not accuse them of the well, and does not repeat the bloodied shirt. He says that he once trusted them with a brother and asks whether the present case is any different. The question leaves the earlier event undefined, which is more severe than defining it would have been: the sons know exactly what is meant, and the reader knows too. What Jacob then does is transfer the custody rather than negotiate it. God is the best of keepers. The formulation concedes that no human guardian in this family has proved adequate, and it is the only ground on which he will eventually let Benjamin go.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:65

> When they opened their bags, they discovered that their money had been returned to them. They argued, “O our father! What more can we ask for? Here is our money, fully returned to us. Now we can buy more food for our family. We will watch over our brother, and obtain an extra camel-load of grain. That load can be easily secured.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Discovery Happens at Home**

The verse places the opening of the bags after the return to their father, and the placement is what gives the discovery its rhetorical use. Had the money been found on the road, it would have been a private matter among ten travellers. Found at home, in front of Jacob, it becomes evidence in an argument already under way. The chapter's arrangement is exact: verse 62 has Joseph order the money into the bags; verse 63 reports the brothers' first plea; verse 64 records Jacob's refusal; verse 65 produces the object that reopens the case. The object arrives at the moment of greatest need, which is the moment the persuasion appears to have failed. Its arrival is timed by the narrative rather than by the journey. The bags are opened after the provisions have been counted and after the request has been refused, so the money functions not as a bonus but as a rebuttal. Found on the road it would have been news; found in Canaan after the refusal, it is an argument.

**"What More Can We Ask For?"**

The opening of their second speech is a rhetorical question: *mā nabghī* — what do we seek, what more could we want. The force of the question is that the answer has already been supplied. They are not asking their father to imagine a benefit; they are pointing at one that has arrived unasked. The question converts the discussion from a prediction about Egypt's minister into an observation about what he has already done. Jacob's refusal in verse 64 rested on a precedent — the last time you took a son. The brothers' counter rests on a present fact, and a present fact is harder to argue with than a memory. That is the whole of their case and it is a strong one. They have received full measure, had their capital refunded and been treated as honoured guests by the most powerful administrator in Egypt; on the record before them, suspicion would be unreasonable. Jacob's memory of the well is not answered by their argument, it is outweighed by their circumstances.

**The Argument Is Built on an Act Nobody Explained**

The brothers know what they found and not why they found it. Joseph's reason is stated in verse 62, and the reader has it; the brothers do not. They interpret the return of their money as generosity and reliability, and they build their case on that interpretation. The chapter allows the inference because it is sound: a man who restores the price of grain he has already delivered is not likely to harm a guest. But the reader knows something further, which is that the money was placed there to bring them back. The generosity and the design are the same act, and verse 65 shows only the generosity being received. The reader holds both halves at once and the brothers hold one. Every element they cite as proof of goodwill — the measure, the refund, the escort — was arranged by a man who intended to keep their brother in Egypt. The verse lets their sincerity stand unqualified, and that is what makes the scene work: they are honestly persuaded by a design they cannot see.

**Three Benefits Listed in Sequence**

The brothers itemise what the second journey will produce. First, provision for their family — the food itself. Second, safe conduct for their brother — repeating the assurance of verse 63. Third, an additional camel-load, the eleventh measure that was withheld. The list moves from the household's need to the father's objection to the material gain, and it ends with the smallest item. That ordering is persuasive rather than logical: the largest benefit is placed first, the contested promise second, and the marginal gain last, where it appears as a bonus rather than as the point. The commentators gloss the final clause as describing the extra load as easy to obtain — a light burden, a small addition to what they are already carrying.

**Jacob Is Persuaded by Something He Cannot Verify**

The money is real and its return is a fact. Everything the brothers draw from it is inference. They cannot tell their father who the minister is, what his character is beyond one act, or why he demanded a brother from their father specifically. Jacob is being asked to send his youngest son into a foreign country on the strength of an unexplained refund and his sons' second assurance of safe conduct. The chapter does not resolve the difficulty for him. Verse 66 shows what he does with it: he requires an oath sworn in God's name, with God as witness, and he attaches an exception clause for circumstances beyond their control. Precaution is his answer to an argument he finds partly convincing and partly unverifiable.

**The Second Plea Repeats the First and Adds a Fact**

Compare verse 63 with verse 65. Both open with an address to the father. Both state the shortage. Both offer the assurance that they will watch over their brother. What verse 65 adds is the money, and the addition changes the character of the plea from a request into a demonstration. The brothers have not altered their promise; they have acquired support for it. The chapter is describing how persuasion actually works in a family under pressure: the same assurance, offered twice, succeeds the second time not because it is stronger but because something else has arrived to carry it. The added fact is what carries the second attempt. Money in the bags cannot be argued with, discounted, or attributed to the brothers' eloquence; it is simply there, and it converts a promise into a demonstration. The chapter is exact about how persuasion works in this family — not through better wording but through evidence arriving from outside the conversation.

**The Verse Sets Up the Oath and the Gates**

Everything in verses 66 to 70 follows from what happens here. The oath is extracted because the argument succeeded but not completely. The instruction about entering by separate gates follows the oath, and is Jacob's last precaution before his sons leave with Benjamin. The private disclosure in verse 69 and the planted cup in verse 70 then test the assurance that verse 65 has just renewed. The brothers will be given a chance to abandon a second son of Jacob in Egypt, and the whole of the chapter's second half depends on whether they take it. Verse 65 is the moment the second son is committed to their care, and the sūrah records it in the brothers' own words rather than in the narrator's.

**Money as the Chapter's Recurring Measure of Value**

The returned price belongs to a pattern the sūrah established early. Joseph was sold for a few counted dirhams by men who set no store by him (12:20), and the chapter used the smallness of the sum to measure the smallness of their regard. Now the same brothers receive money back from a man who values them enough to give it, and they use it to persuade their father. The two transactions are not connected in the text, and the contrast does not need to be stated. A person worth twenty dirhams to his own family is worth a full refund and a granary's hospitality to his brother. The chapter lets the arithmetic speak for itself across forty-five verses. The pattern runs from twenty dirhams for a boy to a refund that buys his brother's return, and it is never commented upon. Joseph was sold at a price, and he now spends his own goods to bring a brother to him; the chapter records both transactions in the same currency and leaves the comparison to whoever is counting.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:66

> Jacob insisted, “I will not send him with you until you give me a solemn oath by Allah that you will certainly bring him back to me, unless you are totally overpowered.” Then after they had given him their oaths, he concluded, “Allah is a Witness to what we have said.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Refusal Turned Into Terms**

Verse 64 opened with a rhetorical question that functioned as a refusal. Verse 66 opens with *I will not send him with you until*, which is not a refusal but a price. Something has changed between the two verses, and the change is the money found in the bags (12:65). Jacob has not been convinced that his sons are reliable guardians; he has been shown that Egypt's minister is generous, and that alters the calculation enough to make the journey acceptable on conditions. The chapter tracks the movement precisely: distrust of the travellers survives, distrust of the destination lessens, and the result is a covenant rather than a prohibition. That conversion is the verse's achievement. A prohibition would have been negotiated away or simply disobeyed; a covenant creates an obligation the sons have sworn themselves and can be held to. Jacob stops trying to prevent the journey and begins trying to bind the travellers, which is the only form of control left to a man who has decided to let his son go.

**The Form of the Oath**

The word Jacob requires denotes a binding covenant, the term the Qur'an uses for the most serious kind of undertaking between people and between people and God. It is not a promise and not a word of honour. It is sworn *bi-Llāh*, by God, and it is given collectively: *fa-lammā ātawhu mawthiqahum*, when they had given him their covenant. The religious form is the point. Jacob has already stated that God is the best of keepers (12:64); he now makes God a party to the arrangement, so that the obligation runs to God as well as to him. A son sent to Egypt under a family promise can be lost to an accident of memory. A son sent under an oath sworn in God's name is held by something the oath-takers cannot quietly set aside.

**The Exception Clause Is Jacob's Own**

*Unless you are totally overpowered* — the commentators gloss this as a general condition meaning unless you reach a situation in which you cannot uphold the oath. The clause is Jacob's addition, and it does two things. It makes the covenant realistic: ten men cannot guarantee an outcome against overwhelming force, and a father who demanded an unconditional guarantee would be demanding something impossible. And it narrows the oath to what the sons can actually control — their own willingness to return with Benjamin. Everything else is exempted in advance. The clause also becomes the hinge of verse 80, where the eldest invokes it to explain why he will not go home without his brother. Its function is to make the oath survivable. A bond with no exit would have been broken the moment the brothers were overwhelmed, and a broken oath is worse than no oath; the exception gives them a lawful way to fail without perjuring themselves. Jacob legislates for the possibility of defeat, and verse 80 shows the legislation used exactly as designed.

**"God Is a Witness to What We Say"**

The formula is one the Qur'an uses when a covenant is concluded between two parties who cannot otherwise enforce it. Moses speaks the same words to his future father-in-law when agreeing the term of service for marriage: *“˹Then˺ it is ˹settled˺ between you and I. Whichever term I fulfill, there will be no ˹further˺ obligation on me. And Allah is a Witness to what we say”* (28:28). In both cases the sentence closes a negotiation in which one party is leaving home and placing himself under another's authority. Jacob's use of it here is the chapter's way of recording that the oath is not a private family arrangement but a transaction with a divine counterparty who will remember it even if the sons do not.

**No Oath Was Taken the First Time**

The contrast with verses 11 to 14 is exact and deliberate. When the brothers asked for Joseph, they gave assurances in ordinary language: send him with us, we will guard him, we are his sincere well-wishers. No covenant was required, no oath was sworn, and no exception clause was attached. Jacob's grief in this chapter is not that he was deceived once; it is that the absence of formality then and its presence now measure how much has been lost. The verse therefore records a father's second act of trust conducted entirely differently from the first, and the difference is the cost of the first. The difference is procedural, and procedure is what a frightened father has left. In the first case he received a verbal assurance and no security; in the second he specifies the terms, invokes God as witness and excludes one circumstance that would release them. What he has learned between verse 12 and this one is not to trust less but to document more.

**Precaution and Trust Held Together**

Verse 64 ended with God as the best of keepers and the most merciful of the merciful. Verse 66 begins with an oath and ends with a witness. A reader might expect the trust to replace the precaution, since a man who has surrendered the matter to God need not bind his sons. The chapter refuses that simplification. Jacob does both. He places Benjamin in divine custody and he extracts a human covenant, and neither act is presented as undermining the other. The sūrah's treatment of means and reliance is consistent throughout: taking every available precaution is not a failure of trust, and trusting God is not a substitute for precautions. The chapter presents the combination without defending it. Jacob has just named God the best of keepers, and he now requires an oath from his sons as the condition of the journey; both statements sit in adjacent verses and neither is treated as inconsistent. What the sūrah offers is reliance that takes practical steps, not a theory of reliance that forbids them.

**The Oath Is the Measure Applied in Verse 80**

The covenant sworn here is what the brothers are judged against later. When Benjamin is detained and the eldest refuses to return to Canaan, his reason is stated in terms of the oath: *do you not know that your father took a solemn pledge from you in God's name, and how you failed before regarding Joseph* (12:80). The verse invokes both halves of Jacob's arrangement — the covenant about Benjamin and the earlier failure it was designed to prevent — and treats the oath as binding even when keeping it means staying in Egypt indefinitely. Verse 66 is therefore not a piece of family negotiation; it is the instrument the rest of the chapter uses to measure these men. Everything the brothers do afterwards is measured against it. Their return with provisions, their departure from Egypt without Benjamin, their eldest brother's decision to remain — each is weighed in the following verses against a covenant sworn before God in this one. The chapter supplies the standard first and the conduct second, so the reader can judge without being instructed to.

**Eleven Sons and One Father Left at Home**

The verse also records the household's composition at the moment Benjamin departs. Jacob keeps no son behind as a guarantee and no son stays voluntarily; the whole remaining family travels to Egypt except the father himself. The oath is therefore the only thing binding the travellers to a return, and the witness invoked is the only party who can hold them to it in a foreign country. That isolation is what gives the covenant its weight and what makes verse 80 so consequential. When the eldest declares he will not leave the land, the effect is not merely a delay; it removes one of the sureties from the arrangement and leaves Jacob with nine sons, one detained, and a father's oath that two parties now consider unfulfilled.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:67

> He then instructed ˹them˺, “O my sons! Do not enter ˹the city˺ all through one gate, but through separate gates. I cannot help you against ˹what is destined by˺ Allah in the least. It is only Allah Who decides. In Him I put my trust. And in Him let the faithful put their trust.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Second Instruction, Practical in Form**

The oath of verse 66 concerned whether Benjamin would travel. This verse concerns how the party will arrive. Jacob gives a specific operational direction: do not enter by one gate, enter by separate gates. The instruction is concrete and it is about a city's architecture — Egypt's capital had multiple gates, and eleven men arriving together through one of them would be conspicuous. The chapter places the direction immediately after the covenant, which means the reader receives both the religious bond and the practical measure as parts of a single act of fatherly care. The pairing is characteristic of the man. Jacob secures an oath and then gives directions about gates, and the chapter places both in his mouth without distinguishing their weight. Care for his sons' souls and care for their physical safety are treated as one continuous responsibility, which is the same understanding of providence Joseph has been working from in Egypt since verse 47.

**The Reading That Names the Evil Eye**

The commentators' primary explanation is protection from the evil eye. In the traditional cultures of the Mediterranean and Near East the evil eye is associated with harm that reaches a person because he is an object of envy, and Jacob's sons were conspicuously handsome. One account adds that they had already fallen victim to it on their previous visit to Egypt. Entering by different gates would disperse the group, make their arrival less noticeable, and reduce the exposure to envious attention. The reading is not exotic; it belongs to a world in which a band of well-favoured strangers attracts attention that can turn into harm, and a father who has already lost one son takes that possibility seriously.

**The Reading That Names Ordinary Caution**

Other commentators decline the evil-eye explanation and read the instruction as straightforward prudence. The brothers had become recognisable after their first journey, and a large group of foreign men entering a famine-stricken capital together might attract hostility, suspicion or jealousy. Dispersal is the obvious countermeasure. On this reading the instruction is about security rather than about envy, and it belongs to the same category as the oath: a father taking every available step to protect a son he is obliged to send. Both readings are recorded in the tradition and the Qur'an's wording does not decide between them, which is characteristic of a verse reporting an action without reporting a motive. The ambiguity is preserved rather than resolved. Jacob's instruction is reported in his own words, and the reason attached to them concerns God's judgment, not crowds, gates or security. Whatever practical wisdom lies behind dividing eleven travellers at a city entrance, the verse declines to make it the explanation, and it declines without denying it either.

**"I Cannot Avail You Anything Against God"**

The clause is the verse's centre and it qualifies the instruction before the instruction has been acted on. Jacob tells his sons what to do and tells them, in the same breath, that the doing cannot protect them from what God has decreed. Nothing is withdrawn by the qualification. The precaution stands; its efficacy is limited and the limit is stated by the person imposing it. This is the chapter's clearest formulation of a distinction Islamic thought has returned to constantly: the taking of means is obligatory, and the outcome of means is not in the hands of the person who takes them. Jacob does not resolve the tension between the two claims. He holds both in one sentence.

**"Judgment Belongs to God Alone"**

*In al-ḥukm illā li-Llāh* — the decision is God's and nobody else's. The same formula appears in Joseph's own preaching, where it grounds the demand for exclusive worship: *“It is only Allah Who decides. He has commanded that you worship none but Him”* (12:40). Father and son use identical words about divine authority in different registers — Joseph in a theological argument with two prisoners, Jacob in a practical instruction to eleven sons. The repetition is one of several that tie the two halves of the chapter together, and it shows that the conviction Jacob teaches is the conviction Joseph preaches. The correspondence is exact in substance. Joseph told two prisoners that judgment belongs to God alone and that He commanded the worship of none other; his father, sending ten sons into a famine, states the same principle as the ground of his own composure. Neither knows the other has said it, and the chapter arranges for the reader to hear both.

**Trust Stated and Then Generalised**

The verse closes with two clauses: *upon God I rely*, and *let those who rely, rely upon Him*. The first is personal; the second turns it into a rule addressed to believers generally. The movement from the singular statement to the general injunction is characteristic of prophetic speech in the Qur'an, and here it converts a father's parting words into a formulation available to every reader. The chapter has shown trust tested in a well, in a prison and now in a departure from home with a youngest son. The generalisation at the end of verse 67 is the sūrah's way of stating what those tests have produced: not a private consolation but a principle. The generalisation is what lifts the instruction out of its occasion. A father protecting his sons in Egypt is a particular case; the principle that judgment belongs to God alone is not, and it is the principle Joseph has already used to refuse a prison door, to answer two dreamers and to decline a king's first summons. The verse gives the family's shared conviction in a form that outlasts the journey.

**The Verse Is Followed Immediately by Its Own Limitation**

Verse 68 begins by reporting that the brothers entered as their father had commanded and then states that it did them no good whatever against God. The chapter therefore places the instruction and its ineffectiveness in adjacent verses. Nothing in verse 67 is contradicted — Jacob said himself that he could not avail them anything — but the sequel confirms it in the narrator's voice. The precaution was taken, the outcome was unaffected, and the trial that followed came anyway. That arrangement is what makes verse 67 a statement about trust rather than about technique: the means are prescribed, performed and shown to be insufficient, all within two verses. That structure makes the verse a statement about reliance rather than about technique. Entering by separate gates is presented as obedience to a father's instruction, and the verse that follows records that it availed nothing against God's decree. What survives the failure is the intention, and the chapter preserves it by showing the measure taken seriously and then exceeded.

**Eleven Men Dispersed at Their Father's Word**

The practical content of the instruction deserves a moment. Eleven travellers arriving at a city's walls would naturally enter together, since travelling as a group is safer in a famine and the group carries the documents, the money and the cargo. Jacob asks them to separate at precisely the point where separation is least convenient, and the chapter records their compliance without comment in verse 68. That compliance is itself informative: these are men who once disposed of a brother and lied about it, and here they follow an instruction whose purpose their father has explained only partly. The oath of verse 66 and the obedience of verse 68 together describe a family that has been changed by what happened at the well, even before the change is complete.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:68

> Then when they entered as their father had instructed them, this did not help them against ˹the Will of˺ Allah whatsoever. It was just a desire in Jacob’s heart which he satisfied. He was truly blessed with ˹great˺ knowledge because of what We had taught him, but most people have no knowledge.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Precaution Is Performed and Then Evaluated**

The verse reports compliance first: they entered as their father had commanded them. The obedience is complete and unremarked on, which is how the chapter records the brothers' conduct when they are not being tested. Then comes the narrator's evaluation, and it is blunt — it would not have availed them anything against God. The two statements occupy a single verse, so the reader is not allowed to hold the precaution as a success or as a failure. It was performed, and it changed nothing. The evaluation is not a criticism of Jacob; verse 67 had already contained the same judgement in his own mouth. What verse 68 adds is the narrator's confirmation, given before the events that will demonstrate it.

**"But It Fulfilled a Need in Jacob's Soul"**

The second half of the evaluation is the verse's real contribution. The instruction was useless as protection and valuable as an act. The commentators gloss the phrase as meaning that Jacob's advice, together with his sons' acceptance of it, served to assuage his fear of losing his children. A father who cannot prevent a danger can still do something about it, and the doing matters to him even when it cannot matter to the outcome. The chapter records the psychological fact without sentimentality and without dismissing it. The precaution is honoured precisely because it did nothing — it existed for the person who imposed it, and it was accepted by the people it concerned. That is an unusual economy of value and the verse states it plainly. An action whose entire benefit accrues to the person performing it, and whose failure costs nobody else anything, is still worth performing if it expresses something true about the one who performs it. Jacob's instruction is honoured as an act of fatherhood rather than assessed as a security measure.

**The Trial Arrives Anyway**

The verse's judgement is confirmed within two verses. The brothers enter Egypt, are received, are fed, and then a cup is placed in a saddlebag and a theft is announced (12:70). Benjamin is detained under a law the brothers themselves supplied (12:75–76), and the eldest refuses to return home (12:80). Nothing about entering by separate gates delayed or softened any of it. The chapter is describing a trial that has been arranged independently of the travellers' arrangements, and it states that fact in advance so that the reader does not look for the failure of the precaution as the cause of what follows. The cause is elsewhere, and verse 76 will name it. The deferral is exact. Nothing here explains why the brothers' arrangement fails, and the explanation is withheld for eight verses, until the moment it can be given in full. The chapter is willing to let the reader sit inside an unexplained reversal because it intends that reversal to be understood as part of an arrangement rather than as an accident.

**"He Was a Possessor of Knowledge Because We Taught Him"**

The clause is a statement about the source of Jacob's understanding. He is described as *dhū ʿilm*, a possessor of knowledge, and the possession is immediately attributed to divine teaching: *li-mā ʿallamnāhu*. The same construction governs Joseph's gifts. God established him in Egypt *so that We might teach him the interpretation of dreams* (12:21), and gave him wisdom and knowledge at maturity (12:22). Father and son are described in the chapter's terms as taught rather than as clever, and the attribution matters for how the reader is to take Jacob's conduct. His trust, his precaution and his limits on both are not the product of experience alone. The attribution rules out the reading that Jacob's care is merely accumulated grief. A man taught by God knows something about decree that experience cannot supply, and the verse uses that knowledge to explain how he can impose a precaution and declare it insufficient in the same breath. Both statements come from the same instruction, and the chapter credits the instruction rather than the years.

**"But Most People Do Not Know"**

The coda is one the sūrah returns to. Joseph used it at the end of his sermon on divine authority: *“That is the upright faith, but most people do not know”* (12:40), and the narrator attached it to the statement that God's will prevails over the affair of Joseph's sale (12:21). Here it qualifies Jacob's knowledge: he possessed knowledge because God taught him, and most people lack it. The repetition ties three moments together — a betrayal, a theological argument and a father's parting instruction — under a single claim about the rarity of understanding. The verse does not say what most people fail to know, and the omission leaves the claim general enough to cover every instance the chapter has produced. The instances the chapter has produced are specific enough to fill the gap. Most people do not know that a father's instruction about separate gates will avail nothing, that a planted cup will yield a lawful detention, or that knowledge can be taught rather than accumulated; each of these has been shown, and none of them was known by the people living through it. The generalisation summarises what the reader has just watched rather than adding a new claim.

**Two Kinds of Knowledge Distinguished**

The verse holds a distinction that the rest of the chapter depends on. Jacob knows something about God's decree — that no human precaution can avert it, that judgment belongs to God alone, that trust is the only adequate response. He does not know what the reader will soon learn: that Joseph is alive, that the minister of Egypt is his son, and that the cup is about to be planted. His knowledge is theological and certain; his information is incomplete. The chapter's irony depends on the gap between the two. A man with real knowledge of God sends his sons into a situation he cannot read, and the situation turns out to be governed by the very knowledge he has.

**No Rebuke, and That Is the Verse's Point**

A reader expecting the precaution to be exposed as superstition or as a failure of trust will not find that here. The verse says the instruction did not avail them and that it satisfied a need in Jacob's soul, then calls him a possessor of knowledge taught by God. All three statements stand together. The chapter thereby refuses two easy positions: that human measures work, and that they are pointless. Measures do not determine outcomes, and they are not on that account worthless, because the person who imposes them is doing something other than determining an outcome. The sūrah leaves the reader with a father's care described in the same verse as its ineffectiveness and his knowledge. The absence of rebuke is what completes the description. Had the verse corrected Jacob for relying on gates it would have taught that precautions are worthless; had it shown them working it would have taught that they are sufficient. Holding both facts together without comment is the only formulation that keeps reliance on God intact while still taking the means seriously.

**A Verse About a Man Who Is Not Present**

Jacob does not travel to Egypt in this section, and the verse keeps him in Canaan while narrating an event in an Egyptian city. The chapter is unusual in this: it reports the sons' entry, evaluates the father's instruction, and assesses the father's knowledge, all while the father is hundreds of miles away and unaware that his sons have arrived. The effect is to hold both locations in a single frame. Canaan contains a grieving man with real knowledge and no information; Egypt contains a ruler with full information and a plan. Verse 68 belongs to both, and its judgement about the uselessness of the precaution is spoken from the side that has neither the information nor the power to act on it. That is also the reader's position. Nobody reading this verse knows what Joseph is doing in Egypt or why he has detained Benjamin, and the judgement passed on Jacob's precaution is made with exactly the information the family possesses. The chapter asks its audience to occupy the same standpoint as the people it describes, and then to notice that the standpoint is not the whole of what is true.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:69

> When they entered Joseph’s presence, he called his brother ˹Benjamin˺ aside, and confided ˹to him˺, “I am indeed your brother ˹Joseph˺! So do not feel distressed about what they have been doing.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Entry and a Private Moment Inside It**

The brothers have arrived again, this time with Benjamin, and the verse opens with their entry upon Joseph. The public business — provisions, measures, the condition of verse 60 — will occupy verse 70. What this verse records is something that happens inside the arrival and apart from it. Joseph takes his brother aside. The chapter gives no setting for the withdrawal: no room, no pretext, no time of day. The withdrawal is simply reported, and the report is what the whole second half of the story has been moving toward since verse 58, when one side recognised and the other did not. Everything the brothers have travelled for arrives in this scene and none of them knows it. The provisions are loaded, the condition is met, the minister has received the brother he asked for, and the recognition building since verse 58 has finally occurred — on one side only. The chapter places the reader inside the moment and keeps the brothers outside it.

**"He Drew His Brother Close"**

The verb describes a physical act of bringing someone near, and the commentators explain that Joseph used it to speak to Benjamin privately and to tell him everything that had happened to him. The disclosure is therefore not a single sentence but an account: the well, the caravan, the sale, the household, the accusation, the prison, the dreams, the granaries. The chapter compresses all of it into the words *I am your brother*, and leaves the rest to the reader's knowledge of the preceding sixty-eight verses. What the verse supplies is the fact of a full explanation given in confidence, and the fact that it was given to Benjamin before it was given to anyone else. The physical detail carries more weight than the speech attached to it. A powerful minister embracing a stranger from a foreign caravan in front of his own officials is an act requiring explanation, and the chapter supplies one: these are brothers, and one of them has been grieving for twenty years. The gesture is the identification; the words only confirm it.

**The First Explicit Identification in the Chapter**

Until this moment Joseph has been recognised only by himself. Verse 58 recorded his recognition of the brothers; verse 62 showed him acting on knowledge they did not share; verse 69 is the first time the knowledge passes to another person. The wording is emphatic — *innī anā akhūk*, I am indeed your brother — and it names the relation rather than the history. Joseph does not begin with the well or with the years of slavery. He begins with the fact that establishes everything else: the man standing in front of Benjamin is his brother. The chapter's disclosure follows the order of importance rather than the order of events. That ordering explains why the disclosure comes here rather than at the first meeting. What matters is not that Joseph recognised his brothers, which he did long before and the narrative recorded, but that the recognition is at last spoken, and spoken to the one person in Egypt who never harmed him. The verse gives the secret its proper recipient before giving it to anyone.

**"Do Not Be Grieved at What They Used to Do"**

The consolation is specific about tense. The verb form denotes repeated or continuing action in the past — what they used to do, what they have been doing. The commentators read the clause as Joseph telling his brother that these events now belong to the past and occurred according to God's will. The formulation does two things. It prevents Benjamin from being distressed by what he has just been told, which is the immediate purpose. And it states Joseph's own settled judgement on his brothers' conduct: the matter is closed, it was in the past, and it happened within a divine arrangement. Verse 92 will say the same thing publicly, in the words *there is no blame on you today*. The private form is more specific than the public one will be. Here Joseph addresses the harm done to him by the men standing outside; in verse 92 he releases them from blame in their own hearing. What is said first to a brother in confidence is said later to the offenders themselves, and the earlier version is what makes the later one credible.

**Benjamin Is Asked to Keep the Meeting Secret**

The commentators record that Joseph instructed his brother not to disclose the meeting to the others. The Qur'an does not state the instruction, but the narrative requires it: the brothers behave throughout verses 70 to 82 as though Benjamin is their brother and the minister is a stranger, and their reactions make no sense otherwise. The secrecy also explains the shape of what follows. A plan is about to be executed in which Benjamin will be detained in Egypt, and Benjamin will not appear surprised or resist. The verse's private disclosure is what makes the public events of the next thirteen verses possible, and the chapter trusts the reader to supply the silence. The request also explains Benjamin's conduct in everything that follows. He is detained, accused through a planted object and claimed under a law he knows to be false, and at no point does he protest or reveal what he has been told. His silence is not passivity but compliance with an instruction from the only brother he trusts, given here and kept for thirteen verses.

**Two Traditions About the Plan That Follows**

The commentators differ on how much Benjamin knew. One account holds that Joseph and his brother together devised the arrangement by which Benjamin would remain in Egypt — that is, Benjamin was a party to the plan of the cup. Another, from al-Ṭabarī, holds that the plan was not known to Benjamin, who was told only who Joseph was and not what was about to happen. Both accounts are transmitted and the Qur'an does not settle the question. The difference affects how verse 70 is read: as a joint operation, or as a device executed around a brother who has been told the truth but not the next step. The chapter's silence leaves the reader with the disclosure and nothing more.

**Two People in Egypt Now Know**

The verse changes the distribution of knowledge in the story, and the change is what drives the irony of everything that follows. Joseph has known since verse 58. Benjamin knows from verse 69. Ten brothers, a father in Canaan, an Egyptian administration and a caravan still do not. The reader stands with the two who know, which means the next sequence — a cup planted, a theft announced, a law invoked, a brother detained, ten men pleading — will be watched from inside a secret rather than from outside it. The chapter has been building toward this reversal for eleven verses, and it announces the reversal in a single sentence spoken privately to a boy who has just learned that his dead brother is the ruler of Egypt.

**The Consolation Is Given Before the Trial**

Note the order of Joseph's two acts in the following verses. He comforts his brother first and then arranges the cup. Benjamin is told who Joseph is, told not to grieve over the past, and asked to keep the meeting secret — and only after that does the accusation fall on him. The sequence means that the one person in the caravan who will be publicly named a thief is also the only person who knows the charge is a device. The chapter is deliberate about this: it does not put an innocent boy through a humiliation he cannot understand. What it does is place the humiliation inside a disclosure, so that the reader watches a trial whose outcome the accused has already been assured of.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:70

> When Joseph had provided them with supplies, he slipped the royal cup into his brother’s bag. Then a herald cried, “O people of the caravan! You must be thieves!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Provisions First, Again**

The verse repeats the order established in verse 59. Joseph prepares their provisions, loads them, and only then acts. The brothers have received what they travelled for before the cup is placed and before the accusation is made. The repetition is not a narrative habit but a consistent feature of Joseph's conduct in this section: whatever he does to his brothers, he does after supplying them. The pattern matters for how the reader is meant to judge the coming events. A man who starves his guests and then accuses them is acting from malice. A man who feeds them fully and then arranges an accusation is acting toward an end the chapter will disclose two verses later. The repetition of the sequence matters. As before, the brothers are loaded, supplied and sent on their way before the device is sprung, so the accusation cannot be read as a pretext for withholding grain. Joseph has already given them everything they came for; what he arranges afterwards is a reason for one of them to stay, and he arranges it at his own expense.

**The Cup Is Both a Drinking Vessel and a Measure**

The commentators describe the object in two functions. It was the king's goblet, used for drinking, and it also served as a measuring vessel for food — which is why the translation of verse 72 can call it a measuring cup. One account adds that it was made of gold and set with jewels. Both functions matter to the plot. A drinking vessel is a personal possession of the ruler, so its loss is a serious matter; a measuring vessel belongs to the granary's operation, so its absence is noticed administratively as well as personally. The object Joseph chooses is therefore conspicuous in two registers at once, which is what makes the public outcry that follows credible. The dual function also explains the penalty attached to it. A stolen measure is a theft against the state's own accounting, which in a rationing economy is a public offence rather than a private one, and the law the brothers invoke attaches the thief's own person to the crime. The object was chosen so that its loss would carry exactly that consequence.

**Placed in the Brother's Saddlebag**

The verse specifies the location: his brother's saddlebag, the bag containing Benjamin's own belongings. The commentators note that the bag was filled with Benjamin's things, so the cup is hidden among a traveller's ordinary possessions rather than left in plain sight. The choice of bag is the point of the whole operation. Joseph has ten bags available and one brother he wants to keep, and the cup goes into the one that will produce the result he needs under the law the brothers themselves are about to state (12:75). Nothing in the verse explains the reasoning; the explanation arrives in verse 76, where the narrator says God inspired the plan and that Joseph could not have taken his brother under the king's law.

**Why a Theft Charge**

The commentators supply the mechanism. Jacob's household had a rule about stealing: whoever was caught stealing would be handed over or distanced, given to the person he had stolen from. Joseph knew that rule, and he arranged a situation in which the rule would apply to Benjamin. The charge of theft is therefore not an accusation Joseph believes; it is a device for bringing Benjamin under a law that will place him in Joseph's own custody. The brothers' answer in verse 75 states the law exactly as it was known to them, and the chapter shows the device working precisely because the law invoked is theirs, not Egypt's. That choice is what makes the detention lawful in the brothers' own eyes. A charge under Egyptian procedure could have been resisted or appealed to the king; a charge governed by their own law binds them, since they cannot dispute a penalty they would themselves impose. Joseph builds the situation so that the only authority available to object to it is one they have already accepted.

**The Herald's Public Accusation**

*Yā ayyatuhā al-ʿīr, innakum la-sāriqūn* — O people of the caravan, you are indeed thieves. The cry is addressed to the whole party, not to one man, and it is delivered with emphasis. The commentators identify the speaker as the herald who called out after them, and the charge as collective. That collectivity is what forces the exchange of verses 71 to 75: the brothers must answer for the group, deny the group's guilt, and then state what should happen to the individual if the denial is false. A charge against one man could be answered by producing the innocent man's bag. A charge against a caravan requires a general statement of law, and a general statement is what Joseph needs. The generality is also what makes the reward acceptable. A charge naming a suspect would have invited denial; a charge offering a camel-load to whoever produces the object invites cooperation and places every man in the caravan under the same suspicion at once. Joseph needs the brothers to consent to a search, and consent is what a general announcement produces.

**The Accusation Is False and the Verse Does Not Soften It**

Joseph knows the cup is where he put it. The herald is announcing a theft that did not occur, in a public place, about a party that has been fed and sent on its way. The verse reports the placement and the cry in the same breath and offers no moral comment on either. The chapter is not embarrassed by the device and does not defend it here. Verse 76 supplies the only justification the text gives — God inspired the plan, and without it Joseph could not have kept his brother under the king's law. The reader is left with a false accusation used for a lawful end within a divine arrangement, stated without apology and without elaboration. That refusal to soften is the chapter's method throughout. It records Joseph's device in the same flat register as his generosity, with no clause explaining that the accusation was merely apparent or that the end justified the planting. The reader must hold both facts — that a falsehood was used, and that it served a purpose larger than itself — without being told how to reconcile them.

**The Engine of the Next Thirteen Verses**

Everything from verse 71 to verse 82 follows from the cry. The brothers turn back and ask what is missing. The herald names the king's cup and offers a camel-load for its return. The brothers swear they came to do no harm. They are asked what penalty applies if they are lying, and they answer with their own family's law. The bags are searched in order, the cup is produced, and Benjamin is retained. The eldest invokes the oath of verse 66 and refuses to go home. The remaining brothers return to a father who has now lost two sons. The whole sequence is generated by an object placed in a bag, and the chapter gives that placement its own verse.

**What the Accusation Costs**

The cry is not free. Eleven men who entered Egypt under a father's oath, who were fed at full measure, whose money was returned, and who have just received their youngest brother's company are now publicly called thieves in a foreign capital during a famine. The reputational cost is real and the brothers will feel it — verse 73 opens with an oath and verse 77 shows them defending the family's honour against a charge that touches Joseph himself. Joseph has weighed that cost against the object he wants, and the chapter reports the weighing in verse 76 rather than here. Verse 70 gives only the act, which is the sūrah's habit when it describes something that will be justified later rather than at the moment of doing.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:71

> They asked, turning back, “What have you lost?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**They Turn Back at Once**

The verse records a movement before it records a question. The caravan has left Egypt, loaded and on the road, and at the herald's cry the brothers turn around. There is no hesitation, no debate and no refusal to stop. A group of foreign traders accused of theft by an official of the granaries has very little room to decline an enquiry, and the brothers exercise none. Their immediate return is also a fact about their position: they need Egypt. The famine is in its third or fourth year, the household in Canaan depends on these loads, and verse 60 has already told them that access to the minister is conditional. Turning back is not a choice they can afford to make otherwise.

**The Question Is Genuinely Innocent**

*What have you lost?* The brothers do not know about the cup. They have watched their provisions being loaded, they have received the same full measure as before, and none of them has taken anything. The question they ask is the question a person asks who has no information and no suspicion: what is missing, so that we can help you look. There is no defensiveness in the wording and no anticipation of an accusation. Nine of the eleven men in the party are in exactly this position. The tenth, Benjamin, knows what is in his bag, and the eleventh — the man they are addressing — put it there. The irony is structured rather than incidental. Of the eleven men present, the one asking has arranged everything, the one carrying the object knows about it, and the remaining nine are answering honestly in complete ignorance. The verse holds all three positions at once and reports only the question, leaving the reader to keep the arithmetic the brothers cannot perform.

**Three Levels of Knowledge in One Question**

The verse is the chapter's clearest instance of dramatic irony, and the irony is graded. The reader knows everything: the cup was placed by Joseph, in a specified bag, for a specified purpose (12:70). Benjamin knows the first part and has been told not to disclose it (12:69). Joseph knows all of it and is waiting for a particular answer. The nine brothers know nothing. A single four-word question is therefore spoken by a man with no information to an official with complete information, about an object whose location is known to three people in the scene and to the reader. The chapter constructs this arrangement deliberately and lets the question stand alone in its own verse.

**The Question Invites the Reward**

By asking what is missing, the brothers open the way for the herald to name the object and attach a reward to its return. Had they stayed silent or continued on the road, the accusation would have hung over them without an opportunity to answer it. Their question converts a public charge into a transaction. What follows — a named object, a camel-load offered, a personal guarantee — depends on the brothers having asked. The chapter shows them cooperating with an enquiry they have every reason to want over quickly, and their cooperation is what produces the exchange of the next four verses. The invitation is genuine, and its genuineness is what disarms them. Nothing in the officials' manner suggests that a particular bag is suspected or that the search was arranged in advance, so the brothers respond as innocent travellers do: with indignation, with a counter-question, and with an offer of their own that exceeds what was asked of them.

**A Whole Verse for Four Words**

The Qur'an frequently allocates a verse to a single short utterance when the utterance carries the narrative forward. Verse 61 did the same with a nine-word promise; verse 95 will do it with a dismissal of an old man's claim. Here four words of dialogue receive a verse of their own, and the allocation signals that the question is a turning point rather than a detail. Everything from verse 72 to verse 82 is generated by the herald's cry and answered by this question. The chapter's pacing gives the reader a moment to register the brothers' ignorance before the mechanism begins to work. Expanding so short a statement into a verse of its own is a deliberate slowing of the scene. The reader has just watched the cup being placed; the brothers have not, and the extra space allows that gap in knowledge to be felt before the search begins. Everything that follows depends on the reader knowing more than the speakers, and the verse makes certain of it.

**The Chapter's Dialogue Verses**

Verses 71 to 75 form a tight exchange: a question, an answer with a reward, a denial under oath, a question about the penalty, and a statement of the penalty. Five verses of dialogue and almost no narration. The sūrah uses this method at several points — the prison conversation of verses 36 to 41, the negotiation with Jacob in verses 63 to 66 — and in each case the absence of narrative comment forces the reader to judge the speakers from their words alone. Here the effect is sharp. The reader hears nine innocent men deny a theft, and hears them state a penalty that will fall on the one innocent member of their party.

**They Have Just Been Fed and Sent On Their Way**

The incongruity of the accusation is worth stating. Joseph loaded their provisions himself, as he had done on the first journey (12:59), and the verse before this one reports the loading in the same terms. The brothers have been served at full measure by the official now accusing them, in a country whose rationing he controls, and they are being called thieves as they travel home with what he gave them. The charge is not merely false; it is false in a way the brothers can point to, and verse 73 shows them doing exactly that — appealing to the officials' own knowledge of their conduct in the land. The appeal is available to them only because of the treatment described here. A caravan that had been cheated, shorted or detained would have had no standing to invoke the officials' own observation; these men can, and they do, precisely because the generosity was real and visible. Joseph's earlier kindness is what supplies his brothers with the confidence to protest.

**The Question Also Protects Them**

By asking what is missing rather than denying a charge they have not yet heard specified, the brothers place themselves in the position of cooperating witnesses rather than suspects. That position will matter in verse 73, when they appeal to what the officials already know about them, and in verse 75, when they are asked to state a penalty. A party that had fled or resisted the herald's cry would have had no standing to do either. Their question, innocent as it is, establishes the footing on which the rest of the exchange proceeds — and on which Benjamin is eventually retained.

The chapter gives them that footing without assistance. Joseph does not soften the accusation, the herald does not explain it, and no official offers the brothers a chance to withdraw before the search. What they receive is a question they asked themselves, answered in a way that obliges them further. Nine men who have done nothing wrong are being led, step by step and entirely by their own words, toward the detention of the tenth. Verse 71 is the first step, and it is taken voluntarily.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:72

> The herald ˹along with the guards˺ replied, “We have lost the King’s measuring cup. And whoever brings it will be awarded a camel-load ˹of grain˺. I guarantee it.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Object Is Named as Royal Property**

The answer identifies what is missing: the king's cup. The commentators describe it as a goblet belonging to the ruler, used both for drinking and as a measuring vessel for grain, and one account adds that it was of gold and set with jewels. The specification matters legally. This is not a traveller's possession or a merchant's utensil; it is crown property, and its loss is a matter of state rather than of private complaint. In a famine administration where the same vessel served as the standard measure for food, the disappearance of the king's cup would be noticed by the granaries as well as by the palace. The charge the herald announces is therefore serious in a way that ordinary theft is not.

**Two Speakers and One Announcement**

The commentators note a division of labour in the announcement. The identification of the missing object is attributed to Joseph's companions, while the offer of a camel-load and the guarantee are attributed to the herald. The Qur'anic verse presents the speech as a single reply, and the translation reflects the composite by naming the herald along with the guards. The detail is worth recording because it shows the scene: an official party pursuing a caravan, one of them stating the loss and another stating the reward. The chapter does not need the division and does not mark it, but the tradition preserves it, and it gives the exchange a realistic texture. The texture is that of an actual roadside procedure. A herald calls out the charge and the reward; an official adds the guarantee that makes it acceptable. Two voices, two functions, one continuous announcement, reported by the Qur'an without marking its boundaries. What reaches the reader is the exchange as the brothers experienced it — a single demand from the government.

**The Reward Is a Camel-Load**

The prize offered is a camel-load of grain, and the amount is not incidental. Verse 59 recorded that the brothers arrived with eleven camels and requested eleven loads — ten for themselves and one for a brother at home — and that Joseph refused the eleventh until the brother appeared. The reward now on offer is precisely that eleventh load. A reader who has followed the arithmetic will notice that the object the brothers could not obtain by request is being offered as a prize for returning a cup they did not take. The chapter does not point this out. It simply sets the same measure in front of them twice, once as a refusal and once as an inducement.

**"I Am Its Guarantor"**

The herald adds a personal undertaking: *wa-anā bihī zaʿīm* — I am guarantor for it. The commentators gloss the phrase as meaning that whoever produces the cup will certainly receive the camel-load. The guarantee converts a general offer into a binding commitment from a named official, which in a famine economy is a meaningful addition. Grain is scarce, promises of grain are cheap, and a party searching a caravan has no obvious reason to trust that a reward will be paid. The guarantee removes the doubt and makes the search worth conducting, which is the immediate practical purpose of the whole announcement. The undertaking is also legal theatre with a practical function. By staking himself on the outcome the official converts an arbitrary demand into a bounded one, and bounded demands are the kind a caravan can accept. The brothers submit to the search because they have been given a warrant for it, and the warrant is a man's own person.

**A Charge Turned Into an Incentive**

The verse performs a rhetorical shift. Verse 70 was an accusation: you are thieves. Verse 72 is an offer: whoever brings the cup will be rewarded. The second formulation does not withdraw the first; it works alongside it. The effect on the accused party is to give them something to do other than protest. A caravan told only that it is composed of thieves can deny and move on. A caravan told that a reward is available for producing the missing object has a reason to submit to a search, and the search is what Joseph requires. The offer is therefore not generosity but procedure, and the following verses show the procedure working. The transformation is complete within the verse. What begins as an accusation of theft ends as a contract, with a specified reward, a named guarantor and a search conducted by consent. Joseph has produced, from a planted cup and a camel-load of grain, the legal instrument that will let him detain his brother before nine witnesses who will testify the process was fair.

**The Brothers Are Included in the Offer**

Nobody in the announcement excludes the accused from the reward. The wording is general — whoever brings it — and the brothers, who have just denied nothing and asked only what is missing, are as eligible as anyone in the caravan. That inclusion is what makes verse 73 possible: they answer with an oath about their own character rather than with a suspicion about someone else. The chapter places them in the position of people who could earn a camel-load by finding a cup they know nothing about, and who respond instead by insisting that they did not come to the land to do harm. Their inclusion is what makes the denial necessary. A reward offered only to outsiders would have left them as witnesses; offered to everyone present, it makes each of them a potential claimant and therefore a potential thief. The protest that follows is not indignation at the search but at the suspicion it implies, and it is answered by their own conduct in the next verse.

**Why the Object Must Be Publicly Named**

The whole device depends on specificity. A general accusation of theft would produce a general denial and no search. Naming the king's cup, offering a defined reward and guaranteeing it creates a procedure with a definite endpoint: the bags are opened and the object is either there or not. Joseph needs the search to happen and to happen in a particular order, and verse 76 will report that order. This verse supplies the public justification for it. The reader watches a legal process being constructed out of a reward announcement, and the construction is complete enough that the brothers accept it and submit. Naming the object also fixes the penalty in advance. Once it is identified as the king's drinking measure, its theft becomes an offence against the state's supply system, and the punishment that follows is no longer at the discretion of whoever finds it. The verse assembles, out of a reward announcement, a procedure whose outcome the accused cannot negotiate.

**The Verse Sets Up the Penalty Question**

Once the object is named and the search is agreed, the next question is what happens if the object is found. That question is asked in verse 74 and answered in verse 75, and the answer will come from the brothers themselves. Verse 72 does not state a penalty; it states only a reward. The omission is the mechanism. Had the herald announced the consequence of theft along with the reward, the brothers would have had nothing to supply and Joseph would have been bound by Egyptian law — which, as verse 76 explains, would not have allowed him to keep his brother. The reward-only formulation is what leaves the penalty open, and what leaves it open is what makes it the brothers' to state.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:73

> Joseph’s brothers replied, “By Allah! You know well that we did not come to cause trouble in the land, nor are we thieves.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**An Oath Opens the Denial**

The brothers' first word is *tallāh*, by God — an oath formula that the chapter uses at moments of maximum seriousness. Jacob's sons swear by it when reporting their first loss of a brother to their father (12:16), and they will use it again when telling their father that his grief will kill him (12:85). Its use here is not decorative. Eleven men accused of theft by an official of the Egyptian crown have no documentary defence, no witnesses of their own and no standing in the country. What they have is an oath, and the verse shows them reaching for it immediately, before any argument. The sequence is telling. An innocent man confronted with a false charge would normally begin with the facts; these men begin with God. The oath does the work that evidence cannot yet do, because the bags have not been searched and because the one fact that would settle the matter is a fact they do not possess.

**An Appeal to What the Officials Have Seen**

The second clause is the more interesting: *you know that we did not come to work corruption in the land*. The brothers do not ask to be believed; they point to knowledge their accusers already possess. The party has now entered Egypt twice, has been served at full measure on both occasions, has had its money returned without asking, and has conducted its business without incident. The officials who loaded their provisions and who accompanied the herald are the same people who observed all of it. The argument is therefore empirical rather than rhetorical — you have watched us, and what you watched does not look like the behaviour of people who came to do harm.

**Two Denials of Different Kinds**

The verse contains a denial of an act and a denial of a character. We did not come to work corruption in the land concerns purpose and conduct. We are not thieves concerns identity. The second is the more sweeping and the more vulnerable, since a person who is not a thief has never stolen, whereas a person who did not come to cause trouble may simply have kept his intentions to himself. The brothers make both claims together, and the chapter records both without comment. Verse 77 will show how the second claim is received by the one person in the scene qualified to judge it. The first denial concerns a cup and is true; the second concerns a family and is false. The brothers cannot tell the two apart and the chapter does not help them. Both are placed in the same mouth in the same verse, so the reader hears an honest statement about Egypt delivered by men whose account of Canaan is a lie they have kept for twenty years.

**The Irony the Chapter Does Not Spell Out**

These men stole a brother. They took a child from his father, put him in a well, sold him to a passing caravan for a handful of counted coins, and produced a bloodied shirt to explain his absence (12:16–20). The denial *we are not thieves* is true of the cup and false of the larger matter, and the chapter knows it. Nothing in the verse signals the irony; the reader supplies it from sixty verses of prior narrative. Joseph supplies it aloud in verse 77, in a sentence he keeps to himself, and the sūrah's decision to place the judgement inside his mind rather than in the narrator's voice is what gives the moment its force.

**Their Record in Egypt Supports the Denial**

Whatever the brothers' history in Canaan, their conduct in Egypt has been impeccable, and the verse is careful to keep the claim within those limits.
