They say they did not come *to the land* to work corruption — the reference is to Egypt, to this jurisdiction, to the officials' own observation. They do not claim a spotless life. The restriction is either instinctive or deliberate, and it makes the denial stronger than a general protestation of virtue would have been: it is falsifiable by the people being addressed, and they have not falsified it. The chapter is describing an argument that works because of its narrowness. Its narrowness is also its strength. They claim no general innocence, no good character and no divine favour; they claim that officials have watched them load grain, receive full measure and travel home without incident. Every element is verifiable by the men they are addressing, which makes it the one argument in the scene that cannot be dismissed as self-serving.

**Benjamin's Silence**

One member of the party knows what is in his bag and says nothing. The verse reports the brothers' answer collectively, and Benjamin is included in the collective. Whether he speaks with them, stays silent, or is answered for by the others, the text does not say. The silence is consistent with the instruction recorded in the tradition that Joseph asked him not to disclose their meeting (12:69). A reader aware of that instruction reads this verse differently from the way the officials in it do: the denial is honest for ten of the eleven men present, and the eleventh is bound by a confidence he cannot break in a public accusation. His position is the hardest in the scene and the chapter does not relieve it. He knows the cup is in his bag, he knows who put it there, and he has been told not to grieve over what his brothers used to do. To speak would betray the one man in Egypt who has protected him; to stay silent is to stand accused before nine brothers who believe him guilty.

**The Denial Requires a Penalty to Be Stated**

An accusation answered with a denial has reached an impasse unless someone proposes a test. The brothers have said they are innocent; the officials have said a royal object is missing; the bags are present and unopened. Verse 74 resolves the impasse by asking what should happen if the denial is false, and the question can only be asked because the denial was so emphatic. A hesitant answer would have justified a search on suspicion. An oath produces a demand for a standard: if you are lying, what should the consequence be? The brothers' confidence in this verse is what obliges them to answer that question in the next two. That question arrives within two verses and they answer it themselves, unpressed. The confidence expressed here is what makes the later answer possible: men who have just sworn by God that they came to do no harm are the men who will specify the penalty for a thief out of their own law. The oath commits them to the rule that will bind them.

**The Verse Shows Men Who Believe Themselves Safe**

Everything about the answer — the oath, the appeal to observed conduct, the flat denial of the character charge — indicates that the brothers do not expect a search to produce anything. They have loaded their own bags, or watched them being loaded, and they have taken nothing. On the evidence available to them the accusation is absurd, and their reply is the reply of people who expect the matter to end within the hour. The chapter lets that confidence stand unqualified. Verse 75 will have them state a penalty in the belief that it cannot apply to any of them, and verse 76 will report the search that ends the belief.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:74

> Joseph’s men asked, “What should be the price for theft, if you are lying?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question About Law, Not About Fact**

The officials do not ask whether the brothers stole the cup. That question has already been answered by the oath in verse 73, and pressing it would produce only a repetition. What they ask is what the penalty should be if the denial is false. The shift from fact to law is the whole of the verse's significance. A search establishes whether an object is present; a rule establishes what happens to the person in whose possession it is found. Joseph's men are constructing the second half before the first has been conducted, which tells the reader that the outcome of the search is not in doubt for the people conducting it.

**A Condition That Is Careful**

*If you are liars* — the question is conditional and the condition leaves the denial formally intact. Nobody in the exchange has accused the brothers of lying to their faces; the officials ask what should follow if the oath turns out to have been false. The formulation is polite, legal and entirely trap-like. It allows the brothers to answer without conceding guilt, and it commits them to a rule that will be applied whether or not they intended the commitment. The chapter's interest in legal procedure shows here: the wording of a question determines what an answer can mean, and this question is drafted so that any answer binds the answerers. The care lies in what the question does not assume. It presupposes no guilt, names no suspect and proposes no penalty; it asks only what the thief's recompense should be, in terms the answerers accept. Any reply therefore comes from them as a voluntary statement of law, and a voluntary statement of law cannot afterwards be disowned by the people who made it.

**Why the Accused Are Asked for the Penalty**

The unusual feature of the verse is who is being consulted. In most jurisdictions the penalty for theft is fixed by law and announced by the authority. Here the authority asks the accused to state it. The commentators explain the reason in verse 76: under the king's law Joseph could not have retained his brother. The Egyptian rule, on one account, required the thief to be beaten and to pay double the value of what was stolen, after which he went free. If that rule had been applied, Benjamin would have returned to Canaan with his brothers. The penalty had to come from somewhere else, and the only available somewhere else was the accused party's own custom. Asking them for it is not a courtesy; it is the only route to a lawful detention, and the question is drafted so that the answer cannot be avoided.

**The Brothers Become the Source of the Rule**

Verse 75 shows the device working. The brothers state their family's law: the one in whose bag the object is found is himself the recompense, handed over to the person he stole from. That is the custom of Jacob's household, not of Egypt, and it produces exactly the result Joseph needs — the detention of Benjamin. The chapter therefore has the accused supply the sanction that will be applied to one of them, in a jurisdiction where the local sanction would not have produced that result. The verse is the hinge of the mechanism, and its brevity conceals how much of the plot turns on it. Their authorship removes every ground of complaint. Had the penalty been announced by an Egyptian official, the brothers could have objected to the jurisdiction, appealed to the king or refused the search. Because it comes from them, in their own terms, as the law of their father's house, objection would mean contradicting themselves before the men they have just sworn an oath in front of.

**The Question Is Neutral in Tone**

Nothing in the wording conveys hostility, suspicion or triumph. It is a procedural enquiry put by officials to travellers, and the travellers answer it in the next verse without protest or hesitation. The neutrality is consistent with Joseph's conduct throughout this section: he does not accuse, does not insult and does not gloat. He asks a question whose answer he needs and whose answerers do not know what it is for. The sūrah's presentation of the device is therefore clinical rather than dramatic, and the drama is supplied by the reader's knowledge of what is in the bag. The neutrality is what supplies the tension. A shouted accusation would have let the brothers react with outrage; a procedural enquiry allows them only to answer. They are being cross-examined by a man who already knows the response, and the form of the question stops them noticing that they are the ones under examination rather than the ones giving evidence.

**The Chapter's Interest in Legal Mechanism**

Verses 70 to 76 form a compact study in how a legal outcome can be produced without breaking a law. A cup is placed, an accusation is announced publicly, a reward is offered, a denial is sworn, a penalty is requested from the accused, the penalty is stated in the accused's own terms, and the search is conducted in an order that produces the object last. Every step is procedurally ordinary. The chapter reports the whole sequence and then says in verse 76 that God inspired the plan and that without it the objective could not have been reached under the king's law. The device is presented as revelation working through jurisprudence rather than around it.

**The Reader Knows Where the Question Leads**

By verse 74 the reader has been told three things the brothers have not: that the cup is in Benjamin's bag (12:70), that Joseph is Benjamin's brother (12:69), and that Joseph wants his brother to remain. The question about the penalty therefore arrives with its answer already visible. The tension in the verse is not what will happen but how these particular men will bring it about, and the answer is that they will do it themselves, in their own words, under oath, in a jurisdiction that would otherwise have released the accused. The chapter is showing a plan executed through the free speech of the people it affects. That is the chapter's most striking claim about providence. Nothing here is coerced; the brothers are not threatened into stating the law, not misled about its content, and not prevented from saying anything else. They speak freely, accurately and at length, and what they freely say is the instrument that detains their brother. The plan needs their words rather than their silence.

**Smallest Verse in the Sequence, Most Consequential**

Four verses carry the exchange: a question, a reward, a denial, a penalty question. The fourth is the shortest and does the most work. Without it the search would establish a fact and leave the consequence to Egyptian law, which would have released Benjamin. With it the consequence is fixed by the brothers' own statement before the search begins, so that when the cup is produced there is no room to negotiate the outcome. The sūrah gives this verse its own line in the sequence rather than folding the question into verse 73 or verse 75, and the isolation is a marker of its function. The isolation also controls the pacing of the disclosure. Because the question stands alone, the reader is held between the accusation and the rule that will settle it, and in that pause the brothers are free to answer without suspicion. A longer verse would have signalled importance; a short one lets the importance arrive unannounced, which is exactly what the plan requires.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:75

> Joseph’s brothers responded, “The price will be ˹the enslavement of˺ the one in whose bag the cup is found. That is how we punish the wrongdoers.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Penalty Is the Person**

The brothers' answer is unusual in form. They do not name a fine, a beating or a period of servitude; they say that the recompense is the man in whose bag the object is found — he himself is the penalty. The wording identifies the thief with the compensation owed for the theft, so that the person is handed over rather than punished and then released. The commentators explain the custom behind the formula: in their tradition a thief was given over to the person he had stolen from, just as the thief had taken that person's property. The logic is one of equivalence rather than retribution, and it produces a transfer of the offender into the victim's custody.

**A Family Law, Not an Egyptian One**

The clause *that is how we punish wrongdoers* specifies whose law is being stated. Not the king's, not the land's — ours. The brothers are describing the practice of their own household, and they offer it as the rule to be applied if their denial proves false. The distinction is what makes verse 76 necessary. The narrator explains that under the king's law Joseph could not have taken his brother, and one account of the Egyptian rule describes beating plus double restitution, after which the offender went free. The brothers have therefore supplied, unprompted, the only legal basis available for the outcome Joseph wants. The specification is what makes it operative. Egyptian practice would have imposed a fine or forced labour; the law of Jacob's household attaches the thief's own person to the offence, which is the only formulation under which a man may be lawfully detained for a theft found upon him. Joseph needed that formulation and he received it from the men he was prosecuting.

**They Answer Without Hesitation**

The verse records no deliberation. Ten men, one of whom is about to be searched along with the rest, state a penalty that would put the offender into the custody of the official accusing them, and they state it immediately and confidently. The confidence is explicable: they believe the cup is not in any of their bags. A person certain of his innocence does not calculate the cost of a hypothetical penalty. The chapter is careful here. The brothers are not foolish; they are misinformed, and their misinformation is the result of a disclosure made privately to one of them and withheld from the rest (12:69). Hesitation would have been natural, and its absence is the point. Nine men who believe their brother innocent have no reason to qualify a statement of law, and none of them suspects that the law being recited is about to be applied to one of their own. Their confidence is not stupidity; it is the ordinary confidence of people with no reason to think the question concerns them.

**The Trap Is Theirs**

The whole device reaches its completion in this verse, and the completion is spoken by the trapped party. Joseph placed the cup, the herald announced the loss, the reward was offered, the denial was sworn and the penalty question was asked — but the rule that will actually be applied comes from the brothers' mouths. Nothing is imposed on them from outside their own legal tradition, and nothing is done to them that their own stated custom does not authorise. The chapter's interest in this arrangement is evident in verse 76, which attributes the plan to divine inspiration precisely because it required the brothers to supply the sanction voluntarily. The voluntariness is why the attribution is needed at all. A trap set by one man's cleverness would leave the detained party a grievance and the reader a question about fairness; a sanction supplied by that party's own mouth, under a law his own father taught him, leaves neither. The chapter settles the ethics of the scene before the scene has ended.

**Why the King's Law Could Not Be Used**

Verse 76 states the constraint plainly: Joseph could not have taken his brother under the king's law, unless God willed. The commentators explain the point in practical terms. Egyptian procedure for theft would have punished Benjamin and released him, so the objective — keeping him in Egypt — could not be reached by applying the local rule. Joseph was the ruler's minister and was bound by the ruler's law; he could not invent a penalty for his own purposes. What he could do was ask a party of foreign traders what penalty their own tradition prescribed, and then apply that penalty to a case in which they had accepted its application in advance. The verse is a study in jurisdiction.

**Benjamin Is Included in the Statement**

The brothers say *we* punish wrongdoers this way. Benjamin is one of them, has been travelling with them, has sworn nothing separately and is present when the family's law is announced. He knows the cup is in his bag. He hears his brothers state the consequence of its being found, and he does not interrupt. The tradition reports that Joseph asked him to keep the disclosure secret, and the verse is consistent with that instruction: silence here is obedience to a brother rather than complicity in a theft. The chapter does not explain Benjamin's silence at this point and does not need to, since verse 69 has already supplied the reason. His inclusion completes the bind. The law he has just heard his brothers state applies to him as the person in whose bag the object was found, and he cannot contest it without revealing what he was told in confidence. Benjamin stands caught between a rule he accepts and a secret he has promised to keep, and the chapter leaves him inside both.

**The Verse Establishes the Ground of Verse 79**

When the brothers plead in verse 78 for one of themselves to be taken in Benjamin's place, Joseph's refusal in verse 79 rests on the rule stated here: God forbid that we take anyone other than the one with whose property we found our goods — to do so would make us wrongdoers. The refusal is not arbitrary. It appeals to the standard the brothers supplied in verse 75, under which the offender and only the offender is handed over. Joseph holds them to their own law at the precise moment when relaxing it would serve them, and the chapter presents that as justice rather than as severity. That ground is supplied entirely by the accused. Joseph never states the penalty himself, never argues for it, and never has to defend it when he applies it; he enforces a rule his prisoners announced and then repeated. The chapter is careful to make the detention lawful in the eyes of everyone present, including the nine men who leave Egypt without their brother.

**A Rule Stated in Confidence Becomes a Rule Applied in Grief**

The distance between verse 75 and verse 78 is the chapter's sharpest irony. The brothers state a penalty they believe cannot reach them, and three verses later they are begging to be substituted under it. What was offered as proof of innocence becomes the instrument that detains their youngest brother and keeps their eldest in Egypt (12:80). The sūrah does not comment on the reversal. It reports the statement, reports the search, reports the discovery and reports the plea, and leaves the reader to observe that the law these men invoked was invoked by them, in their own words, about a brother they had once sold and were now about to lose.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:76

> Joseph began searching their bags before that of his brother ˹Benjamin˺, then brought it out of Benjamin’s bag. This is how We inspired Joseph to plan. He could not have taken his brother under the King’s law, but Allah had so willed. We elevate in rank whoever We will. But above those ranking in knowledge is the One All-Knowing.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Search Starts With the Other Bags**

The verse specifies an order. Joseph begins with the brothers' baggage and leaves his brother's bag until last. The order is the whole of the device's credibility. Had he gone straight to Benjamin's saddlebag, the search would have looked like a retrieval rather than an enquiry, and the brothers — who have just sworn to their innocence and stated a penalty — would have had grounds to object that the official knew where the object was. By working through ten bags first, Joseph conducts the kind of search that a charge of theft actually requires, and the object emerges at the end of a procedure rather than at its beginning. Beginning elsewhere is what gives the search its evidential value. A bag opened first would have implied a target; ten bags opened in sequence, with the object appearing in the last, produce a result that looks like the outcome of a procedure rather than its premise. The procedure was conducted properly and it found what had been planted, and both facts are true at once.

**What the Order Produces in the Brothers**

Ten bags are opened and found empty. Each one narrows the field, and each narrowing makes the eventual discovery harder to contest. By the time the last bag is reached the brothers have watched the accusation fail nine times over, which is precisely why they are unprepared for the tenth. The chapter is describing how certainty is built in a group of observers: repeated confirmation of a negative result produces confidence that the pattern will hold, and the confidence is what makes the exception devastating. Verse 77 records the brothers' response — an attempt to shift the implication away from themselves — which is the response of people who have just watched their certainty collapse. The collapse is total and immediate. Two verses earlier these men swore by God that they had come to do no harm and offered a penalty drawn from their own law; now they are reaching for a dead brother as an explanation. The chapter measures the distance between those positions without comment, and the distance is the price of a certainty built on ignorance.

**"Then He Brought It Out of His Brother's Bag"**

The discovery is reported in a single clause with no elaboration. There is no description of the object being held up, no reaction from the crowd, no statement from the herald. The cup is produced, and the verse moves immediately to the narrator's comment. The chapter's restraint here is consistent with its treatment of the whole Egyptian section: the significant events are stated and not dramatised, and the significance is supplied by interpretation rather than by description. A reader who has followed verse 70 already knows what is in the bag, so the verse's economy costs nothing. The economy also preserves the dignity of the procedure. The verse describes no face, no reaction and no astonishment; it records a search conducted in order and an object produced at the end of it. What the chapter withholds in drama it returns in procedural exactness, and the exactness is what every verse that follows will rely upon.

**"Thus Did We Devise a Plan for Joseph"**

The narrator's comment uses the chapter's central word. *Kidnā li-Yūsufa kaydan* — We devised a plan for Joseph. The noun is *kayd*, the same term the sūrah applied to the brothers' plot against a child (12:5), to the women's pressure on Joseph (12:28, 12:33), and to the treachery that God does not guide (12:52). Here the word is attached to God and directed in Joseph's favour. The chapter has spent fifty verses showing *kayd* as a human instrument used against the innocent; it now uses the same word for a divine arrangement that protects him. Verse 52 said God does not give success to the stratagem of the treacherous. Verse 76 shows whose stratagem succeeds.

**"He Could Not Have Taken His Brother Under the King's Law"**

The constraint is stated explicitly, and the commentators explain it. Egyptian procedure for theft, on one account, required the offender to be beaten and to pay double the value of the stolen item, after which he went free. Applied to Benjamin, that rule would have returned him to Canaan with his brothers. Joseph was the ruler's minister and was bound by the ruler's law; he could not substitute a penalty of his own. What made the outcome possible was that the accused party supplied the applicable rule from their own tradition (12:75), and that the rule they supplied retained the offender in the custody of the person stolen from. The verse records the jurisdictional difficulty and its resolution in a single sentence.

**"Unless God Had So Willed"**

The exception is attached to the constraint. Joseph could not have taken his brother under the king's law — but God willed otherwise, and the willing is what produced the brothers' own statement of the penalty. The chapter attributes the mechanism to divine initiative rather than to Joseph's cleverness, and the attribution is the same one made about his knowledge elsewhere: God taught him the interpretation of events (12:21), gave him wisdom and knowledge at maturity (12:22), and here devises the plan through him. A reader who takes the verse at its word sees a prophet executing a revelation rather than a minister manipulating a legal system. That is the reading the verse demands, and it changes the moral register of the episode. If the plan is Joseph's cunning, the detention of Benjamin is a stratagem; if it is willed by God, the same detention is a stage in a family's restoration that neither brother can yet see. The chapter offers both descriptions and subordinates the first to the second.

**"We Raise in Degrees Whom We Will"**

The statement generalises beyond the scene. The same assurance appears in the account of Abraham's argument with his people: *“We elevate in rank whoever We please”* (6:83), and the Qur'an describes God elsewhere as exalted in rank and Lord of the Throne (40:15). In this chapter the raising of degrees has already been demonstrated — a boy sold for counted coins now controls a country's grain — and the clause attaches the demonstration to a principle. Elevation is not the reward of a merit that compels it; it is given according to a will, and the chapter's own hero is its instance rather than its exception. The generalisation is set at the summit of Joseph's career for a reason. He has been raised from a well to a slave household to a prison to the granaries of Egypt, and no step in that sequence was earned by ordinary reckoning. The verse names the mechanism behind it and refuses to make it a system: elevation is willed, not calculated, and nobody can claim it as an entitlement.

**"Above Every Possessor of Knowledge Is One More Knowing"**

The closing maxim is one of the most quoted sentences in the Qur'an. The commentators read it on two levels. In context, it notes that Joseph's brothers were themselves men of knowledge and standing, and Joseph knew more than they did; and that all knowledge terminates in God, who is named throughout the Qur'an as the Knower. In general use the sentence expresses a hierarchy: however much a person learns, there is a higher degree of knowledge above him. The maxim is placed at the end of a verse about a plan nobody in the scene understood, which is the chapter's way of saying that the knowledge operating here exceeded every participant's — including, on the tradition's account, the brother who had been told the truth in private.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:77

> ˹To distance themselves,˺ Joseph’s brothers argued, “If he has stolen, so did his ˹full˺ brother before.” But Joseph suppressed his outrage—revealing nothing to them—and said ˹to himself˺, “You are in such an evil position, and Allah knows best ˹the truth of˺ what you claim.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Defence That Damages Someone Else**

The brothers' first reaction to the discovery is not a protest of Benjamin's innocence. It is a comparison: if he has stolen, a brother of his stole before. The move is defensive and it works by association — the offence is presented as a family trait rather than an individual act, which spreads it across a household and thereby dilutes it. The chapter records the strategy in its own parenthetical: they spoke in order to distance themselves. Ten men who have just watched a penalty they proposed become applicable to one of their number respond by attaching the offence to a person who is not present and, as far as they know, not alive. The mechanism is familiar and the chapter identifies it without naming it. A group under suspicion protects itself by locating the fault in someone who cannot answer, and in this family that person has been unavailable for twenty years. What the brothers do here is what they did in verse 18, and the second performance is offered to the reader as evidence about the first.

**The Brother They Mean Is Joseph**

The commentators are unanimous that the reference is to Joseph, and they record the incident behind it. He is said to have taken an idol belonging to his maternal grandfather and destroyed it so that it would not be worshipped. The story is not in the Qur'an and cannot be verified from it, but its function in the brothers' argument is clear: they are claiming a precedent of theft inside the same maternal line, which is Benjamin's line as well as Joseph's. The accusation therefore does two things at once. It defends Benjamin by comparison and it disparages Joseph, and the brothers do not know that the man they are disparaging is the man they are addressing.

**The Claim Reaches a Dead Brother**

There is a further consequence the brothers cannot see. Joseph is, in their account, long dead — devoured by a wolf, according to the report they gave their father (12:17–18). They are now naming him as a thief in a public setting, in a foreign country, before an official of the crown. The slander falls on a person who is standing in front of them. The chapter has produced ironies before, but this is the sharpest: a false statement about a dead man, made to save a living one, addressed to the dead man himself. The verse does not remark on it. It reports the statement and Joseph's response. The report is flat and the flatness is deliberate. Twenty years of grief, a bloodied shirt, a father whose eyes went white and an invented wolf are compressed into a clause offered as legal precedent, and the verse records the clause without marking what it costs to say. What the brothers have turned into an argument was the worst thing that ever happened to their father.

**"He Concealed It in Himself"**

The narrator's comment is precise. Joseph keeps the matter inside himself and does not disclose it to them. The commentators specify what was concealed: the sentence that follows was spoken to himself rather than aloud. The chapter therefore shows a man receiving an insult about his own character, in public, from the people who sold him, and not answering. The restraint is not indifference. The verse names what is being restrained — the response he would have made — and reports that it stayed inside him. Nothing in the narrative suggests he lacked the power to speak; he had the power to detain a man under a law his brothers supplied. What he conceals is knowledge rather than weakness, and the concealment has a purpose. To reveal himself now would end the process he has built, confronting the brothers with their crime before they had reached any understanding of it. Joseph withholds the disclosure because a confession extracted at the moment of maximum shock is worth less than one arrived at by a long and difficult road.

**"You Are in a Worse Position"**

The words Joseph does not say are the verse's judgement. The commentators gloss them as meaning that the brothers stand in a worse position than Benjamin, because they stole Joseph from his father and treated him unjustly, while Benjamin took no part in that act. The comparison is exact and it reverses the brothers' own move. They argued from a lesser theft to a greater one, claiming that Benjamin's offence has a precedent in his brother's. Joseph's unspoken answer accepts the logic of comparison and applies it correctly: the real theft in this family was of a person, and it was committed by the men now protesting about a cup. The comparison is exact in its terms. They have just accused a brother of stealing a cup; the speaker knows that the same men stole a brother and sold him for twenty pieces of silver. The verse lets the true statement be made in a form its audience cannot recognise, so they hear a rebuke they will understand only years later, when they are told who is speaking.

**"God Knows Best What You Describe"**

The second half of the unspoken sentence refers the matter to divine knowledge. The brothers have described a situation — a family prone to theft, a precedent, a character trait — and the description is false. Joseph does not correct it in front of them. He states that God knows best what they are describing, which is both a rejection of their account and a refusal to argue with it. The formula has appeared earlier in the chapter, in Jacob's mouth, when the brothers brought the bloodied shirt: *God alone must be sought for help against what you describe* (12:18). Father and son use the same recourse about the same men and the same habit of describing falsely.

**Restraint as the Chapter's Portrait of Joseph**

Verse 77 belongs to a consistent pattern. Joseph does not reveal himself in verse 58, does not accuse the brothers in verse 59, does not retaliate in verse 70, does not gloat in verse 76 and does not answer here. The disclosure comes in verse 90, after the brothers have been tested and after they have admitted their fault. The chapter is describing a person who withholds a true statement because saying it at the wrong moment would destroy the thing he is trying to accomplish. What he is accomplishing is not revenge but a reunion, and a reunion requires that the brothers arrive at their own admission rather than be confronted with his. That requirement shapes every scene from here to the end. Joseph could have spoken at any moment and the story would have closed; instead he waits through a search, a detention, a private conference and a second journey, until the men who sold him describe their own crime to his face without knowing it. The reunion he is preparing is an admission before it is an embrace.

**The Last Unknowing Insult**

After this verse the brothers stop defending themselves and start pleading. Verse 78 asks for a substitution; verse 80 records the eldest's refusal to leave Egypt; verse 81 has them return home with a report their father does not believe. The insult in verse 77 is therefore the last thing they say about Joseph while believing him dead, and the chapter places it in the mouth of men who will spend the next twenty verses discovering that everything they said about him was wrong. The verse is also the only place in the sūrah where Joseph's feelings about his brothers are recorded at all, and they are recorded as feelings he did not express.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:78

> They appealed, “O Chief Minister! He has a very old father, so take one of us instead. We surely see you as one of the good-doers.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Title Joseph Once Served Under**

The brothers address him as *ʿAzīz*. The chapter has used the word before, and its earlier appearances are uncomfortable. The city's women used it of Joseph's master when gossiping about his wife: *“The Chief Minister's wife is trying to seduce her slave-boy”* (12:30). It was that man's household Joseph was sold into, that man's wife who accused him, and that man's residence he was imprisoned after leaving. The tradition adds that when the Chief Minister died, the king gave Joseph his position and with it his title. The brothers are therefore addressing, by his official style, the person who once stood in that office as a slave — and they do not know it. The address reverses a relationship the reader has already seen from the inside. These men petition the minister of Egypt in the language of petition, and the minister is the boy their father's household lost and their own hands delivered to a caravan. Every formula of respect they use is accurate, and every one of them is spoken to a brother, which is what gives the scene its weight.

**An Appeal to a Father's Age**

The argument the brothers put forward is humanitarian and specific: he has a father, an old man. The commentators explain the force of the reference. Jacob loves Benjamin above the others, and Benjamin has been his consolation in place of Joseph; losing him would produce a grief the old man could not survive. The brothers are not asking for mercy on abstract grounds. They are describing a household they know intimately and a bereavement they have already caused once, and asking the official in front of them not to cause it again. Everything in the appeal is true. What is missing from it is the identity of the person being appealed to — the son whose loss produced the age they are now citing, and whose absence made Benjamin the consolation they are describing.

**Ten Men Volunteer to Be Taken**

The offer is the verse's most striking element. Ten men volunteer to be substituted for one, in a jurisdiction where the penalty they themselves stated involves being handed over to the person robbed. The offer is not rhetorical; it is made to an official with the power to accept it, and it is refused in the next verse. Whatever else the brothers are, they are not here attempting to escape at Benjamin's expense — they are proposing to pay the cost themselves. The chapter records the proposal without comment and without accepting it, and the refusal is grounded in law rather than in unwillingness.

Note what the offer concedes. By proposing a substitution the brothers accept the finding: the cup was in the bag, the penalty applies, someone will be detained. They do not contest the search, do not question the herald's account and do not claim the object was planted. Their defence in verse 77 was about character, not about fact, and here it collapses into a request about who pays. That concession is what makes the following verses possible, since a party still disputing the discovery could not negotiate its consequences.

**The Verse Is the Brothers' Best Moment**

Compare this appeal with the conduct recorded in verses 8 to 18. The same men planned the disposal of a brother, argued about killing him, agreed on a well, and brought their father a bloodied shirt with a fabricated story. Here, faced with the loss of a second brother, they offer themselves. The sūrah does not announce the change and does not praise them for it. It simply places the offer in the narrative, where a reader can compare it with what came before. The chapter's account of these men is not that they were wicked and became good; it is that they were capable of both, and that a long sequence of events eventually made the second capacity operative.

**"We See You Among the Doers of Good"**

The closing clause praises Joseph with the chapter's own central term. *Muḥsin* — a doer of good — is what the narrator called Joseph when he reached maturity (12:22), what Joseph called himself implicitly when describing his conduct in prison (12:36), and what the reward clause of verse 56 refers to. Joseph will use the same noun in verse 90 when identifying himself to these men. The brothers are therefore describing him accurately in the very sentence in which they misunderstand him completely. The compliment is true, and it is addressed to a man they believe to be an Egyptian official, and its truth is confirmed four verses later by the person receiving it. The confirmation comes four verses later, from the man being addressed, in private, to a brother who cannot repeat it. The compliment is therefore true twice: as the brothers' honest assessment of an official who has dealt with them fairly, and as a statement about a prophet that the sūrah has spent seventy verses establishing. Neither speaker knows that both descriptions apply to one person.

**The Irony of the Compliment**

They praise him as a good-doer while he is executing a plan that has just detained their brother. The reader knows both halves. Joseph has fed them at full measure twice, returned their money unasked, and disclosed himself privately to the boy they are pleading for; he has also planted a cup, conducted a search in a calculated order, and allowed them to state the penalty that would retain him. Whether all of this is the conduct of a doer of good is the question the chapter answers in verse 76, where the plan is attributed to God. The brothers' judgement and the narrator's coincide, from opposite sides of a secret. The coincidence is produced by twenty years of hidden evidence. The reader has watched Joseph refuse a locked door, decline a prison release, feed a famine and forgive a brother in private; the brothers have watched him load their camels and run a search fairly. Both sets of observation reach the same verdict, and the chapter lets the compliment stand as true from either direction.

**The Appeal Fails, and the Failure Is the Point**

Verse 79 refuses the substitution on legal grounds. Verse 80 reports the brothers' despair and the eldest's decision to remain in Egypt. Verse 81 has nine of them return to Canaan with a report Jacob will not accept. The plea in verse 78 therefore produces nothing — except that it is recorded. The chapter keeps it because it matters that these men asked to be taken instead of their brother, and because the asking is part of the evidence that the family has changed. Joseph hears it. The reader hears it. The refusal that follows is a refusal of the mechanism, not of the intention, and verse 92 will show what Joseph eventually does with both.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:79

> Joseph responded, “Allah forbid that we should take other than the one with whom we found our property. Otherwise, we would surely be unjust.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Same Formula He Used at the Door**

Joseph's first word is *maʿādha Llāh* — God be my refuge, God forbid. The chapter has heard the formula before, in his mouth, in the most tested moment of his life. When the Chief Minister's wife locked the doors and said come to me, he answered: *“Allah is my refuge! It is ˹not right to betray˺ my master, who has taken good care of me”* (12:23). The women of the city used the same exclamation when clearing him before the king (12:51). Here it introduces a refusal of a different kind — not a refusal of temptation but a refusal of an accommodation — and its return ties the two moments together. In both cases the formula marks a line Joseph will not cross, and in both cases the line is drawn by reference to God rather than to preference.

**The Refusal Is Grounded in the Rule They Supplied**

Joseph does not say he cannot take another because it would defeat his purpose. He says it would be wrong to take anyone other than the person in whose possession the property was found. That is the standard the brothers themselves stated in verse 75: the recompense is the one in whose bag the object is found. He holds them to their own law at the moment when relaxing it would serve them, and he presents the relaxation as injustice rather than as mercy. The move is legally precise. A substitution would mean detaining a person against whom nothing was found, which no rule in either jurisdiction authorises. The grounding is what makes the refusal unanswerable. Joseph asserts no Egyptian authority over foreign travellers and claims no discretionary power; he applies the exact formulation the brothers recited two verses earlier. To object they would have to withdraw their own statement of law, in front of the officials who heard it, at the moment when they most need those officials to believe them.

**"Then We Would Be Wrongdoers"**

The word is *ẓālimūn*, and it is the chapter's term for the injury Joseph has spent his life receiving. The wrongdoers never succeed, he said at the door of the ʿAzīz's house (12:23). The brothers described their own conduct to their father in the same vocabulary — *we were wrongdoers* — in verse 64's context and again in verse 91. Now Joseph applies the term prospectively to himself: if he accepted the substitution, he would join the category of people he has been injured by. The refusal is therefore not only legal but self-defining. He will not do the thing that has been done to him, and the verse states the reason in his own word for it.

**Why He Cannot Relax the Rule Even If He Wanted To**

The chapter has already explained the constraint. Joseph could not have taken his brother under the king's law (12:76); what made the detention possible was the penalty the brothers supplied from their own custom. If he now set that penalty aside and took a different man, the whole arrangement would lose its basis. The detention of Benjamin would appear arbitrary — an official seizing a member of a foreign caravan for reasons he would not state — and the brothers would have grounds to contest it. Joseph's insistence on the rule is what preserves the legality of the outcome he wants, and the verse shows him maintaining it against the people whom it disadvantages. Maintaining the rule is also what preserves it. A rule applied selectively in favour of the powerful stops being a rule, and Joseph has staked his administration on being the man whose procedures can be relied upon. The verse shows him refusing the easy exits — taking another brother instead, or simply releasing this one — because either would undo the legal basis on which the detention stands.

**He Speaks as an Office, Not as a Brother**

The refusal is in the plural: *we* should not take anyone other than the one with whom *we* found our property. Joseph speaks as the administration, with the herald and the guards included in the pronoun. The plural is not merely formal. It records that the search was conducted publicly, that the object was found in the presence of officials, and that the penalty is being applied by a jurisdiction rather than by a private person. Nothing in the verse permits the brothers to suspect that the official in front of them has any personal interest in the outcome, which is exactly what Joseph requires if the detention is to be accepted rather than resisted. The office is also the only position from which he can speak. A brother could not detain Benjamin without revealing himself, and a revelation now would collapse the entire arrangement. So Joseph answers as the minister of Egypt, in the third person, citing a law he did not make, and the disguise that began as concealment becomes the working instrument of the plan.

**The Refusal Is Not Cruelty, and the Chapter Shows Why**

Everything Joseph has done for these men in the preceding verses argues against reading the refusal as severity. He fed them at full measure twice (12:59, 12:70), returned their money into their bags without being asked (12:62), and disclosed himself to the brother they are pleading for (12:69). The verse's refusal concerns one thing only: who is detained. It does not withdraw provision, does not impose a fine, does not punish anyone beyond the retention of a single person, and does not close the possibility of a later resolution. The chapter is careful to keep the refusal narrow, and its narrowness is what allows verse 80 to describe the brothers as despairing of persuading him rather than as fleeing from him.

**The Verse Completes the Legal Mechanism**

Verses 70 to 79 form a closed sequence: an object placed, a loss announced, a reward offered, a denial sworn, a penalty requested from the accused, the penalty stated, a search conducted in a deliberate order, the object produced, a substitution offered and refused. Every step is procedural and every step is recorded. The chapter's interest in the mechanism is not antiquarian; it is what allows verse 76 to say that God devised the plan. A divine arrangement executed through ordinary legal steps is the sūrah's account of how providence works in public life — not by suspending procedure but by arranging the statements people make inside it.

The refusal also preserves the position Joseph will occupy in verse 90. When he finally identifies himself, he does so to men who have watched him apply a rule evenly, refuse an advantage offered to him, feed them twice at full measure and return their money unasked. Nothing in the detention can be read back as an act of personal malice, because verse 79 has already shown him declining the one accommodation that would have served the malice reading. The brothers will admit their fault in verse 91 without ever having been accused by him, and this verse is part of the reason the admission is possible.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:80

> When they lost all hope in him, they spoke privately. The eldest of them said, “Do you not know that your father had taken a solemn oath by Allah from you, nor how you failed him regarding Joseph before? So I am not leaving this land until my father allows me to, or Allah decides for me. For He is the Best of Judges.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Despair of Persuasion, Not of the Situation**

The verse opens with a specific kind of hopelessness. The brothers despair *of him* — of moving the official, of obtaining a substitution, of arguing further. They do not despair of Benjamin's survival or of returning home eventually. The narrowing is important. Verse 78's appeal has been refused on legal grounds, and the refusal is final enough that no further plea is attempted. What remains is not negotiation but a decision about what each of them will do next, and the verse records the decision being taken privately among themselves rather than in front of Joseph. The privacy is significant. For twenty years these men have acted as a group with a shared story and a shared silence; here they withdraw from the officials, from the crowd and from the brother in custody to work out what comes next. The chapter marks the withdrawal because what emerges from it is not a plan of escape but the first honest sentence any of them has spoken.

**"They Conferred Privately"**

The word denotes a private consultation, a closed conversation among a group. The Qur'an uses the same noun of intimate counsel elsewhere and states that no such conversation occurs without God's knowledge: *“If three converse privately, He is their fourth. If five, He is their sixth”* (58:7). The chapter's use of it here marks a boundary — the brothers have moved out of the official's hearing and into a discussion among themselves. It is the first time in the Egyptian section that they deliberate as a group, and the deliberation produces the only morally serious statement any of them has made in front of the reader. The deliberation matters because it is deliberation. Nobody prompts them, no official accuses them and no brother confronts them; alone with the consequence of a journey they undertook freely, they reach a judgement about themselves. The chapter shows conscience working without external pressure, which is the condition that makes the confession in verse 91 possible when it finally arrives.

**The Eldest Speaks**

The commentators identify the eldest as Reuben, though the Qur'an does not name him. He is the one who speaks, and what he says is addressed to his brothers rather than to Joseph or to God. The chapter gives the floor to the eldest at the moment of crisis, which is consistent with the family structure it has described throughout: these men act as a group and the group has an order. It may also be significant that verse 10 records one of the brothers proposing that Joseph not be killed but thrown into a well instead, and the tradition often identifies that speaker with the eldest. If so, the man who once proposed a lesser harm is now the man who refuses to go home.

**He Invokes the Oath and the Earlier Failure Together**

The speech has two references and they are joined deliberately. First, the covenant their father took from them in God's name (12:66). Second, their earlier failure regarding Joseph — the pledge they gave about a son they did not return, and the report they brought their father instead. The eldest does not mention the oath without the failure or the failure without the oath. He sets them side by side so that his brothers hear the second inside the first: we are now in the position we were in before, and last time we did not keep our word. The chapter's whole account of these men turns on that juxtaposition. The juxtaposition is the whole of the chapter's moral argument about them. Two failures are named in one breath: the loss of Joseph, for which they handed their father a false account, and the loss of Benjamin, for which they have sworn before God. The first cannot be repaired and the second they refuse to compound, and that refusal is where the change in them becomes visible.

**"I Will Not Leave This Land"**

The vow is personal and open-ended. He will remain in Egypt until his father permits him to return or until God decides for him. The commentators gloss the first condition as Jacob calling him home, and the second as God's decision either to restore Benjamin or to send him back without him. Neither condition is within the eldest's control, which is the point: he has committed himself to a stay whose length is fixed by someone else. The vow is the only act of reparation any of the brothers performs before the reconciliation, and it costs him the one thing he has — his own freedom of movement. The cost is what distinguishes him from the others. Nine brothers return to Canaan with provisions and a story; the tenth stays in a foreign country without family, without freedom and without any prospect of release, because he swore an oath and will not break it. The chapter records the decision in a single sentence and lets it stand as the first reparation the family receives.

**"He Is the Best of Judges"**

The clause closes the vow and transfers the decision. The same words appear in the Qur'an's account of a prophet addressing a divided people: *“then be patient until Allah judges between us. He is the Best of Judges”* (7:87). In both cases the formula is used by a person who cannot resolve a dispute by his own action and does not intend to leave it unresolved. The eldest is not abandoning the matter; he is declining to decide it himself and declining to let his brothers decide it by going home. The chapter places the formula in the mouth of the one brother who has begun to understand what the family owes.

**The Verse Divides the Brothers**

Until this point the ten have acted and spoken as one body. Verse 80 splits them: one stays, and the remainder return to Canaan with a report their father will not believe (12:81–83). The division is the beginning of the chapter's reconciliation, because a group that cannot be held together by a lie eventually has to be held together by something else. The eldest's refusal to travel home is the first act in that direction. He will not carry a second false report to Jacob, and he will not appear before his father without the son his father extracted an oath about. The chapter marks the change without naming it. The division produces a family structure the narrative has not had before. Nine sons arrive in Canaan with grain and a report; a tenth remains in Egypt as surety for the eleventh; and the eldest, who until now has spoken only as part of the group, speaks as an individual carrying a commitment of his own. The chapter marks the change without naming it.

**The Speech Is Addressed to Men Who Know It Is True**

Nothing in the eldest's argument would persuade a stranger. He cites an oath, an earlier failure, and a decision he cannot make. His audience is nine brothers who were present at the well, who produced the bloodied shirt, who heard Jacob's refusal in verse 64, and who have just watched a second son detained under a penalty they themselves stated. The force of the speech is entirely internal to the family, and the chapter's decision to place it in a private conference rather than in front of Joseph means the reader overhears a reckoning that no outsider in Egypt is aware of. The reckoning is the real event of verse 80.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:81

> Return to your father and say, ‘O our father! Your son committed theft. We testify only to what we know. We could not guard against the unforeseen.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Eldest Finishes the Speech He Began**

Verse 80 ended with a vow not to leave Egypt. Verse 81 continues the same speech and turns to the nine who are going home, instructing them what to say. The instruction is quoted at length: an address to Jacob composed by one brother for the others to deliver, beginning with the words they are to use and ending with the limits of what they may claim. The chapter's arrangement gives the eldest two verses of uninterrupted speech, which is more narrative space than any of the brothers receives anywhere else in the sūrah. The content justifies the space. What he composes is not a cover story, not a mitigation and not an excuse. It is an accurate account of a disaster, in the plainest language available, and the accuracy is the reason for his having composed it at all rather than leaving the report to chance.

**"Your Son Committed Theft"**

The central statement is given in the harshest available terms and it names nobody. *Your son* — not Benjamin, not our brother, not the boy you made us swear about. The formulation forces Jacob to identify the person himself, and it places the loss where it falls: between a father and a child rather than among brothers. The charge is not softened. The brothers are not to say that a cup was found, that a search was conducted, that a penalty was applied under their own stated law, or that ten of them offered themselves in substitution. They are to say their brother stole. The eldest chooses the sentence that will cause his father the most pain and does not attempt to make it bearable, which is a decision about honesty rather than about cruelty.

**Testimony Limited to What Was Seen**

The two qualifying clauses work together. The first restricts the evidence: we testify only to what we know. The brothers watched the search, saw ten bags opened and empty, and saw the object produced from the eleventh. They did not see it placed, and the chapter has told the reader who placed it (12:70). Their testimony goes to what happened in their presence and no further. The second clause denies a capacity rather than a duty: we were not keepers of the unseen. The commentators gloss this as meaning they could not have foreseen what would happen in Egypt when they made their pledge, and they connect it to the exception Jacob wrote into the oath itself — unless you are totally overpowered (12:66). The plea is placed inside the covenant's own terms rather than outside them.

**An Accurate Report for the Second Time**

The chapter has now shown these men deliver two reports to their father about two lost sons, and the difference in kind is complete. The first was composed before the event, supported by a bloodied shirt, delivered in the evening with weeping, and accompanied by the remark that their father would not believe them however truthful they were (12:16–18). The second is composed after the event, supported by named witnesses, delivered without tears, and confined to what was observed. Both are addressed to the same man, both concern a son he will not see for years, and both receive the same answer (12:18, 12:83). The sūrah does not comment on the difference in truthfulness. It presents the two reports and lets the reader hold them together, which is the method it has used for every moral comparison in the chapter.

**The Brothers Are Not Defending Themselves**

Nothing in the speech argues that they acted well. They do not claim to have protected Benjamin, do not explain why they failed to secure his release, and do not mention the offer of substitution that all ten of them made in verse 78 — an offer that would have counted for something with a father looking for evidence of good faith. The report is a bare statement of fact followed by a denial of foreknowledge, and it stops there. The absence of self-defence is consistent with what the eldest has just said to his brothers in private: that their father took an oath from them in God's name, and that they failed him once before regarding Joseph (12:80). A man who has made that judgement cannot compose a report that argues against it.

**Why the Report Names No Culprit Beyond the Brother**

The speech assigns responsibility for the theft to Benjamin alone and assigns responsibility for the loss to nobody. The brothers do not explain why they did not secure his release, do not describe the official who refused their offer of substitution, and do not characterise the Egyptian procedure that detained him. The report is therefore complete about the fact and silent about the agents. That silence has an effect on the father who receives it. Jacob is given a theft, a penalty and a detention, with no person to hold accountable except a son he cannot reach and ten sons standing in front of him. Verse 83 shows what he does with the gap: he fills it with the only agents he knows. The composition of the report, honest as it is, leaves room for a conclusion its authors did not intend.

**A Message Sent to a Man Who Will Not Believe It**

Jacob's answer in verse 83 rejects the report, not because he has found it inaccurate but because he has heard a similar report before from the same mouths. The eldest presumably knew this. He composed the speech anyway, added the limits on testimony, supplied witnesses in the following verse, and stayed in Egypt rather than deliver it himself. The chapter therefore shows a man taking responsibility for a message he expects to be disbelieved, and choosing absence over the position of being the one who says it. Verse 81 is the brothers' most honest act in the sūrah, and it is performed by nine of them at the instruction of a tenth who will not be present to defend it. That arrangement is what makes the report both credible and, in the event, useless.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:82

> Ask ˹the people of˺ the land where we were and the caravan we travelled with. We are certainly telling the truth.’”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Witnesses Offered Rather Than Proof Produced**

The speech composed in verse 81 ends with a direction to seek verification. The brothers do not ask Jacob to accept their word, and they do not produce an object to support it. They name two groups he can question. The move is unusual for this family and the contrast with their earlier report is exact. When they came home about Joseph they carried evidence — a shirt stained with false blood — and offered no witnesses whatever (12:18). Here they carry nothing and offer witnesses instead. The chapter's comparison of the two reports is completed by this verse: fabricated proof replaces inquiry in the first case, and available inquiry replaces proof in the second. The difference is not merely rhetorical. One report was designed to end questioning, and the other is designed to invite it.

**"Ask the Town Where We Were"**

The commentators understand the town as the people of Egypt — the inhabitants of the place the brothers have just left. That is a wide and largely inaccessible category for an ageing father in Canaan during a famine. He cannot travel to a rationed capital to question granary officials about a search conducted at a loading yard, and the brothers know it. The offer may therefore be less a practical proposal than a statement of confidence: our account would survive scrutiny by anyone who was present, including the people who conducted the search. The chapter does not test the offer and Jacob does not take it up in verse 83. What the offer establishes is that the report was made in a jurisdiction full of witnesses, which is the strongest thing a party of foreign travellers can say about its own conduct.

**"And the Caravan We Travelled With"**

The second group is more usable, and the commentators identify it as the people of Canaan — travellers who made the journey with the brothers and would return to the same region and the same families. One account adds that when these travellers came back to Jacob they testified to the brothers' truthfulness, which means the offer was taken up and produced the confirmation the brothers expected. The Qur'an does not record that testimony, and the chapter's silence leaves the matter where the brothers left it: verification was available, it was named, and the report was made in the knowledge that it could be checked by people who would soon be standing in Jacob's own household. That knowledge shapes the wording of verse 81, which claims only what was seen.

**Two Categories of Witness Covering Two Different Claims**

The pairing is deliberate and the two categories are not interchangeable. The town represents the officials and residents of the jurisdiction where the events occurred — the people who announced the loss, offered a reward, conducted the search and applied a penalty. They can confirm what happened. The caravan represents fellow travellers from the brothers' own region, who observed the party on the road, at the gates of the city and during the loading of provisions. They can confirm who the brothers are and how they behaved. Together the two groups cover both halves of the account: the fact of the detention and the character of the men reporting it. A single category would leave one half unverified, and the verse names both.

**The Claim Is Truthfulness, Not Innocence**

*We are certainly telling the truth.* The assertion is confined to the accuracy of the report and extends no further. The brothers do not claim that Benjamin is innocent, that the detention is unjust, that the penalty was misapplied, or that their father's grief is misplaced. They do not ask for anything. The narrowness is consistent with the limit on testimony stated in the previous verse: a person who says only what he saw can defend the saying, whereas a person who vouches for someone else's innocence cannot. The chapter shows these men learning to make the smaller claim, and the smaller claim is the one that survives — not Jacob's acceptance in verse 83, which is withheld, but the reader's, who has watched the whole search and knows exactly what the brothers did and did not see.

**The Verse Ends a Speech and Begins a Journey**

The closing quotation marks the end of the eldest's address, and the narrative immediately changes location. Verses 80 to 82 have been a single scene in Egypt, conducted privately among nine brothers and one who has vowed to stay. Verse 83 moves to Canaan and to Jacob's answer. The chapter does not describe the journey home, the arrival, or the moment the report is delivered; it passes from the composition of the message to its reception. That economy is typical of the sūrah's transitions. What matters is the message and the answer, and the weeks of travel between them are omitted because nothing in them affects either. The reader is carried from a granary in a famine to a household in mourning in the space of a single verse break.

**A Family That Now Prefers Verification**

The chapter's interest in evidence runs from beginning to end. A torn shirt established Joseph's innocence inside the ʿAzīz's household (12:26–28). A public enquiry before the king established it again, at Joseph's own insistence and at the cost of further years in prison (12:50–51). An oath with an exception clause governed Benjamin's journey (12:66). A stated penalty governed his detention (12:75). And here, at the moment of a second loss, the brothers offer inquiry instead of an object. The progression is never announced, but it is visible across the verses and it runs in one direction. A family that once lied to its father with a bloodied shirt is now composing a report that invites checking, and the chapter records the change without praising it or pretending that it will be believed.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:83

> He cried, “No! Your souls must have tempted you to do something ˹evil˺. So ˹I am left with nothing but˺ beautiful patience! I trust Allah will return them all to me. Surely He ˹alone˺ is the All-Knowing, All-Wise.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Same Answer Given Twice, Decades Apart**

Jacob's reply repeats the answer he gave when the bloodied shirt was brought to him. On that occasion he said: *“No! Your souls must have tempted you to do something ˹evil˺. So ˹I can only endure with˺ beautiful patience!”* (12:18). The opening word and the first clause are identical here, and the second clause returns in a slightly altered form. The repetition is not a habit of speech but a judgement formed by experience: this is the second time his sons have returned from an errand with a story about a missing brother, and the first story was false. A father who has lived through the first account does not need new arguments to doubt the second, and the chapter supplies him with none. He answers the pattern rather than the report.

**Doubt Grounded in Precedent, Not Evidence**

Nothing in verses 81 and 82 supports Jacob's suspicion. The brothers have limited their testimony to what they saw, offered two categories of witness, invoked the exception clause of their own oath, and asked for nothing. Jacob's response engages with none of that. It states a conclusion about their souls. The verse is honest about the epistemic position: a man deceived once by the same people about the same subject cannot evaluate their second report purely on its merits, because the merits are not the only thing he is weighing. The chapter does not rebuke him for it. Nor does it vindicate him on the facts, which turn out to be as his sons described. What it shows is that a person can be right about a family's character and wrong about the event in front of him, and can hold both at once.

**"Beautiful Patience"**

The phrase *ṣabrun jamīl* appears only twice in the Qur'an, and both occurrences are Jacob's, in this chapter, in response to the loss of a son. The commentators understand it as patience without complaint to other people — endurance that does not seek relief by protesting to creatures. The adjective does not describe the patience as pleasant or easy. It describes it as unmarred, free of the element that would spoil it. The verse's second use of the term shows the same discipline being applied to a second loss, decades later, by a man whose eyes have already failed from weeping (12:84). The chapter presents that application as prophetic conduct rather than as stoicism, and verse 86 will define the practice by stating where the complaint is actually directed.

**"Perhaps God Will Return Them All to Me"**

The hope is plural and specific. The commentators identify the three whom Jacob expects back: Joseph, Benjamin, and the eldest son who has vowed to remain in Egypt (12:80). Jacob has been told nothing about Joseph except that a wolf devoured him, and nothing about the eldest except that he will not come home until permitted. His expectation that all three will return is therefore not a conclusion drawn from information he possesses. The commentators describe it as a kind of prophecy, since Joseph was not known to be alive, and they connect it to the claim he makes in verse 86 — that he knows from God what his sons do not know. The verse places in a grieving father's mouth an expectation that the remaining twenty-eight verses fulfil exactly and completely.

**Rejection, Endurance and Hope in One Sentence**

The structure of verse 83 is unusual and deserves to be read as a whole. It opens with a rejection of his sons' account, moves to a declaration of patience, and ends with hope and two divine names. Nothing connects the three movements except the person speaking them. A man who believes he has been deceived again, who has just lost a second child, and who has a third stranded in a foreign country states that God may return all of them to him. The chapter does not explain how the hope survives the grief and the distrust, or whether it is caused by either. It records all three in a single verse and leaves them side by side, which is the same treatment the following verse gives the grief on its own.

**The Answer Is Given to the Report, Not to the Sons**

Jacob's reply does not question the brothers, demand details, or ask what happened to the eldest. He does not interrogate the witnesses they offered or test the limit they placed on their testimony. He answers with a verdict and a hope, and the verdict concerns their souls rather than their evidence. The omission is informative. A father conducting an enquiry would have asked about the official, the search, the penalty and the substitution; a father conducting a grief asks none of it. Verse 83 therefore tells the reader less about what happened in Egypt than about what the report did to the man receiving it. The chapter keeps the facts in Egypt and keeps Canaan for the response to them, which is the division of material that governs verses 83 to 87.

**The Two Names Answer the Two Difficulties**

*Innahu huwa al-ʿalīm al-ḥakīm* — He is the All-Knowing, the All-Wise. The pairing is placed at the end of a verse that has raised two problems, and it answers each. Jacob does not know what happened in Egypt and cannot rely on the accounts of the people who were there; the first name states that someone does know. The detention of a second son, after the loss of a first, looks like meaningless repetition; the second name states that events are ordered toward an end. The claim is not that things will turn out well but that they are known and arranged, which is harder to hold and is what distinguishes this verse from ordinary consolation. Verse 100 will report the ordering in Joseph's own words: my Lord is subtle in what He wills.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:84

> He turned away from them, lamenting, “Alas, poor Joseph!” And his eyes turned white out of the grief he suppressed.

**Expanded Commentary**

**He Turns Away Before He Speaks**

The verse begins with a gesture and places it first. Jacob turns away from his sons — *tawallā ʿanhum* — and only then speaks. The order separates the two acts that occupy verses 83 and 84. The first was addressed to the brothers and contained a judgement about their souls, a declaration of patience and a hope. The second is addressed to nobody. What Jacob says after turning away is not part of the conversation and is not intended to persuade, explain or answer anyone. The chapter's arrangement makes the lament private without making it secret: the sons hear it, report it and rebuke him for it in the next verse, but the words were not spoken to them.

**The Name He Has Not Used**

*Yā asafā ʿalā Yūsuf* — alas, my grief for Joseph. This is the first occasion in the chapter on which Jacob names his lost son in the presence of other people. In verse 64 he referred to him only as *his brother*, inside an argument about a different child. In verse 83 he spoke of *them all* being returned without specifying who. Here the name arrives, in an exclamation made after he has turned away from the people whose report provoked it. The placement is exact. Jacob can say the name only when he is no longer answering his sons, which suggests that the name belongs to a grief he has been carrying separately from every conversation the chapter has recorded, including the conversations about the son who was lost.

**A New Loss Reopens the Old One**

The commentators note what the verse is doing. Jacob has just been told that Benjamin is detained in Egypt under a penalty his brothers supplied. What he mourns is Joseph. The second loss does not produce a second grief; it reopens the first, and the first is what he names. The structure of the exclamation makes the point: the grief is his, it is old, and its object is a son he has believed dead for decades. A reader who knows Joseph is alive and administering Egypt's grain watches a father mourn a living child by name at the moment when a second child is genuinely beyond his reach. The chapter supplies no commentary on the coincidence and does not treat it as a sign. It simply reports the words and the whiteness of the eyes.

**"His Eyes Turned White"**

The physical consequence is stated without explanation or elaboration. The commentators record two readings: that he wept so much he went blind, or that his eyesight became very weak. Either way the whiteness denotes a visible change in the eye produced by sustained weeping, and the chapter treats it as a fact rather than as a figure of speech. The injury is the only bodily harm in the sūrah that receives a stated cure. Verse 93 provides the remedy — a shirt carried from Egypt and cast over his face — and verse 96 reports the recovery in the same plain terms: he regained his sight. A grief described physically is healed physically, and the healing arrives by means of an object belonging to the son he is mourning.

**"He Was Choked With Grief"**

The closing word describes a person who holds sorrow in. *Kaẓīm* denotes one filled with grief who does not let it out, who restrains the expression of what he feels. The term qualifies everything else in the verse. Jacob's lament is a single exclamation; his weeping is prolonged but private; his complaint goes to God alone (12:86). The chapter's portrait of this man's sorrow is a portrait of sorrow contained, and the containment is what damages his sight. He does not rail, does not curse his sons, does not withdraw from worship and does not accuse God of injustice. What he does is turn away, say one name, and weep until he cannot see — and the verse reports all three acts in a single sentence without comment.

**Restraint as the Habit of This Family**

Verse 77 showed Joseph concealing a response inside himself and refusing to disclose it to the men insulting him. Verse 84 shows his father doing the same with grief. Verse 86 will have Jacob state the practice explicitly: I complain of my anguish and sorrow only to Allah. The chapter is describing a shared family discipline, and it presents the discipline in both of its prophets without praising or criticising it. What it does show is the cost. Joseph's suppression costs him years in a prison he could have left; Jacob's costs him his sight. Neither man is depicted as wrong to have endured silently, and neither is depicted as unharmed by the endurance. The sūrah's interest is in the price of restraint rather than in its virtue, and it records the price in the body rather than in argument.

**No Rebuke From the Narrator**

The verse reports the grief and stops. It does not say Jacob was excessive, does not remind him of God's decree or of the patience he declared in the previous verse, and does not contrast his conduct with an ideal of composure. The only criticism of his mourning anywhere in the chapter comes from his sons in verse 85, and the sūrah places that criticism in the mouths of the men who caused the mourning and who do not know that its object is alive. The narrator's silence is itself a judgement in favour of Jacob. A father who has lost two sons to the same errand, who does not know that the first is ruling the country holding the second, and who weeps until his sight fails is described in a handful of words and then left alone.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:85

> They said, “By Allah! You will not cease to remember Joseph until you lose your health or ˹even˺ your life.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**An Oath About Their Father's Grief**

The sons begin with *tallāh*, by God — the same formula they used when reporting the loss of Joseph decades earlier (12:16) and when denying the theft in Egypt (12:73). Here it introduces not a claim about facts but a prediction about their father: you will not stop remembering Joseph until you are wasted or dead. The oath form gives the rebuke a weight it would not otherwise carry. They are not speculating or expressing worry; they are asserting, in God's name, that his grief will continue to a fixed endpoint. The chapter's use of an identical formula for a false report, an honest denial and a rebuke of a grieving father is not explained, and the reader is left to notice that the same words serve all three purposes in this family.

**The Objection Is to Duration, Not Intensity**

What the sons object to is not the depth of Jacob's grief but its persistence. *You will not cease remembering Joseph* — the complaint is that the remembrance does not stop.
