Decades have passed, a second son has been detained, the family is in a famine, and the father's mourning has not diminished or moved on to the new loss. The verse records an observation any household would recognise: a bereavement that has become the settled shape of a person's life rather than a stage within it. The commentators note that the rebuke is motivated by love and compassion, and that it is a figurative way of telling him they fear for his life. The sons are describing something true, drawn from what they can see — eyes that have turned white — rather than from cruelty.

**Two Endpoints, Both Physical**

The verse offers an alternative: until you become *ḥaraḍ* — wasted, enfeebled, ill to the point of collapse — or until you are among the perished. The commentators take the first as a state of exhaustion short of death and the second as death itself. The formulation is a prognosis in the shape of a warning, and it is precise about the mechanism. No illness is named, no enemy is expected, no accident is feared. The grief itself is identified as what will destroy him. Verse 84 has already supplied the evidence for the prediction, so the sons are not threatening their father with anything; they are telling him what they believe is happening to him and what they believe it will end in.

**The Rebuke Comes From the Cause of the Grief**

Nothing in the verse acknowledges this, and the chapter does not acknowledge it either. The men speaking are the ones who proposed disposing of Joseph, agreed on a well, sold him to a passing caravan for counted coins and brought their father a bloodied shirt with a story about a wolf (12:15–18). They are also, on the report they have just delivered, the ones whose journey produced the detention of the son whose loss reopened the wound. Jacob's grief for Joseph has a single identifiable cause, and the cause is standing in front of him asking him to stop. The sūrah reports the rebuke in the sons' own words, without narrative comment, and leaves the reader to supply the fact that makes those words unbearable to overhear.

**No Comfort Is Offered**

The verse contains no consolation and no proposal. The sons do not say Joseph is with God, do not suggest that their father's patience will be rewarded, do not offer him back the hope he expressed in verse 83, do not ask his forgiveness, and do not promise to search. They state a prognosis and stop. The absence of comfort is the verse's real content. A family that has caused a grief it cannot relieve tends to ask the sufferer to reduce it, and the chapter records that request without softening it. The answer arrives in the next verse and is not addressed to them: Jacob says where his complaint goes, and he does not say what he intends to do about his sons' warning.

**The Sons Are the Only Audience for the Grief**

The verse records a rebuke, and the rebuke presumes access. Nine men are close enough to their father's mourning to describe its trajectory, to predict its endpoint and to address him about it in God's name. Jacob has turned away from them, named Joseph aloud and wept until his eyes failed, and his sons are present for all of it. The chapter does not describe them leaving, objecting earlier, or being excluded. Whatever Jacob's grief has cost him, it has not isolated him from the people who caused it, and they have not withdrawn from him because of it. That proximity is what makes the exchange in verses 84 to 86 possible, and it is also what makes verse 86 necessary: a man whose complaint is heard by the wrong audience redirects it to the right one.

**Joseph Survives Only in His Father's Memory**

For the whole of the Egyptian section the brothers have believed Joseph dead. The only person in Canaan who still speaks his name is the man mourning him, and he speaks it once, after turning away (12:84). The verse therefore identifies the actual function of the grief inside this household: it is the only place Joseph still exists. To ask Jacob to stop remembering is, from within the family's assumptions, to ask that the last trace of a dead son be removed along with the suffering it causes. The chapter does not put that argument in Jacob's mouth and he never makes it, which is consistent with his restraint throughout. The point is visible in the structure of the exchange rather than stated in it, and it is what makes the rebuke more damaging than the sons appear to understand.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:86

> He replied, “I complain of my anguish and sorrow only to Allah, and I know from Allah what you do not know.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Complaint Is Redirected, Not Withdrawn**

Jacob does not defend his grief, explain it, or promise to reduce it. He states where it goes. The rebuke in verse 85 predicted that his remembrance of Joseph would end in collapse or death; the answer accepts the description and changes the addressee. *Innamā ashkū baththī wa-ḥuznī ilā Llāh* — I complain of my anguish and my sorrow only to God. The particle carries the sense of restriction: to God alone, not to you, not to anyone. Nothing is suppressed by the restriction. The grief continues at the intensity his sons have described; what changes is the audience for it, and the change is the whole of the verse's moral content. Changing the audience is the only change available to him. Jacob cannot stop grieving, cannot undo twenty years and cannot compel his sons to believe him; what he can decide is where the grief is spoken. The verse treats that decision as an act rather than a mood, and it is the act that keeps his patience from turning into silence.

**Two Words for a Single Condition**

The verse names two things: *bathth* and *ḥuzn*. The first denotes the innermost distress that a person cannot keep inside and pours out — the grief that escapes, the burden too heavy to carry silently. The second is grief in its ordinary sense, sorrow as a settled state. The pairing is precise and it describes the situation exactly. Jacob has been described in verse 84 as *kaẓīm*, one who holds sorrow in; here he names what is held and what escapes. A person whose eyes have turned white from weeping has both. The chapter gives him two nouns rather than one because one would not cover the case, and because the complaint he makes to God includes the part he has not been able to contain.

**Beautiful Patience, Now Defined**

The commentators connect this verse directly to the phrase Jacob used twice before. Beautiful patience (12:18, 12:83) is understood as patience in which a person complains only to God about his suffering and not to other human beings. Verse 86 is therefore the definition of the term rather than a separate statement. The three verses form a single account of a practice: the phrase is declared at the first loss, declared again at the second, and its content is stated when the practice is challenged. The chapter does not present the practice as an absence of feeling. It presents it as a direction — sorrow of a particular intensity, addressed to a particular recipient. That definition rescues patience from stoicism. A sorrow that is intense, acknowledged and addressed is not a weaker form of endurance than a sorrow that is hidden; on this account it is the harder one, because it requires the grieving person to keep speaking to God while receiving nothing in reply. Jacob has been doing exactly that for twenty years.

**Complaining to God Is Not a Failure of Patience**

The verse is careful about this. Jacob complains, and complaining is not treated as a lapse. The restriction is on the audience, not on the act. A reader who expected the chapter's ideal of patience to be silence will not find it here; the ideal is speech confined to the one listener who can do something about it. The distinction matters for how the sūrah understands endurance generally. Joseph's restraint in verse 77 was silence in front of men who would misuse what he said. Jacob's restraint here is speech directed away from men who can only rebuke him for it. Both are forms of the same discipline, and neither is numbness. The distinction protects the reader from a cheap moral. Patience is not the absence of complaint but the direction of it, and a man who complains only to God is doing something harder than a man who complains to nobody, because he is still speaking. The chapter honours both the speech and its addressee and refuses to require the suppression of either.

**"I Know From God What You Do Not Know"**

The second clause is a claim to knowledge, and it is made to the people who have just predicted his death. The commentators read it as Jacob's intuitive knowledge that Joseph was still alive — a conviction held without evidence and against a report he had accepted for decades. The claim is unusual in form. He does not say he hopes, or suspects, or wishes; he says he knows, and he attributes the knowing to God rather than to reasoning. Verse 83's hope — that God will return them all — is restated here as knowledge, and the restatement is what separates Jacob from his sons in the chapter's economy of understanding. The separation is epistemic before it is emotional. His sons have evidence — a bloodied shirt, a caravan, a decade of absence — and their conclusion follows from it; Jacob has knowledge whose source he names and whose content he does not explain. The chapter does not adjudicate between the two positions here, and verse 94 settles the matter without argument.

**The Grounds of the Claim Are Reported but Not Stated**

The commentators offer two explanations for the conviction. One holds that Jacob saw in a dream that Joseph was alive. The other reports that he was informed by the Angel of Death that his soul had not been taken. Neither account is in the Qur'an and neither can be verified from it; the text states the knowledge without stating its source, which is consistent with the way the chapter handles prophetic knowledge elsewhere. Joseph's interpretations are described as taught by God (12:21) without the teaching being narrated. Jacob's conviction is described as knowledge from God without its transmission being narrated. The sūrah's method is to report the fact of the knowledge and leave its mechanism aside.

**Verse 96 Confirms the Claim in Jacob's Own Words**

When the shirt is cast over his face and his sight returns, Jacob says to his children: *“Did I not tell you that I truly know from Allah what you do not know?”* (12:96). The repetition is the chapter's verification. The claim made in verse 86 — against a rebuke, during a famine, with two sons missing and a third stranded — is restated after the first of the three has been restored. Nothing in verse 86 argued for the claim or offered evidence for it. Its vindication arrives ten verses later in the form of a recovered son and a father's reminder that he had said so. The chapter's structure is the argument, and it is the only argument the verse offers.

**Sorrow Sent Where It Can Be Received**

The verse distinguishes between two audiences for suffering, and the distinction is the practical content of Jacob's faith. His sons can observe his grief, predict its endpoint and ask him to reduce it. That is the whole of what they can do. They cannot find Joseph, cannot release Benjamin, cannot restore his sight, and cannot tell him whether his conviction is founded. God, on Jacob's account, can do all of these, and knows whether the conviction is founded. The complaint is therefore not an escape from his family but a routing decision: the sorrow goes to the only listener who can act on it. The chapter supports the decision by what happens next — a commission in verse 87, a journey, a disclosure in verse 90, a shirt in verse 93 and a reunion in verse 99. Nothing in that sequence would have begun with the sons' rebuke, and everything in it follows from the redirection made here.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:87

> O my sons! Go and search ˹diligently˺ for Joseph and his brother. And do not lose hope in the mercy of Allah, for no one loses hope in Allah’s mercy except those with no faith.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**An Order to Search, Not to Endure**

Verse 86 ended with a claim to knowledge. Verse 87 turns that knowledge into a commission. The verb Jacob uses denotes diligent inquiry — searching with effort, tracking something down, not waiting for news to arrive. The instruction is active and it is addressed to the same sons who have just returned from Egypt and who have just rebuked their father for his grief. He does not ask them to grieve less. He sends them back out. The chapter's sequencing is deliberate: complaint directed to God, knowledge claimed from God, and then a task assigned to human beings. Nothing in the verse suggests that reliance replaces effort, and the effort required is a second journey into a famine.

**Joseph Is Named Alongside Benjamin**

The commission covers two people, and the inclusion of the first is the verse's central fact. Benjamin is in custody, which is why the brothers were sent to inquire about him; but Jacob adds Joseph, a son he has been told for decades was devoured by a wolf and whose name he has spoken once in the entire chapter, after turning away (12:84). The conjunction is not casual. To send men to search for a living detainee and a dead child in the same breath is to state a belief, and verse 86 has already stated it. The sūrah places the belief in an instruction rather than in a declaration, which makes it actionable and testable. Naming both sons also refuses to rank the losses. Joseph has been gone twenty years and Benjamin a matter of weeks; the search is commissioned for both in a single sentence, which treats the older loss as no less recoverable than the newer. That equality is the substance of Jacob's hope and it is carried by the structure of the instruction rather than by any claim about it.

**Why Jacob Believes Joseph Is Alive**

The commentators offer grounds. One account holds that he saw it in a dream. Another reports that he was told by the Angel of Death that Joseph's soul had not been taken. Others connect the conviction to the hope expressed in verse 83 and the knowledge claimed in verse 86, without specifying a source. The Qur'an itself gives no ground at all: it reports the instruction and the prohibition against despair, and leaves the conviction standing on the claim that its author knows from God what his sons do not. The chapter's method throughout is to report prophetic knowledge as a fact and withhold its mechanism, and this verse follows the method exactly. The withholding preserves the claim's force. Had the verse explained how Jacob knew, the knowledge would belong to the explanation; left unexplained it belongs to him, and the reader is asked to accept that a man can know what his evidence does not support. The chapter tests that acceptance over eight verses and vindicates it in verse 96.

**"Do Not Lose Hope in God's Mercy"**

The noun behind the translation denotes relief, ease, the breathing-space that comes after constriction. Jacob is not telling his sons to expect a specific outcome. He is telling them not to abandon the expectation that relief is possible, and he attaches the instruction to a journey whose likely outcome — a second refusal in Egypt, a detained brother, an unrecovered son — argues against it. The commentators read the phrase as God's mercy, and connect it to the forgiveness the brothers will eventually receive (12:92, 12:97). The mercy in view is therefore not only the return of two sons but the restoration of a family that has committed a crime against one of its members and never confessed it.

**"No One Despairs of God's Mercy Except the Disbelievers"**

The prohibition is given a theological ground, and the ground is severe. Despair of divine mercy is placed in the category of unbelief rather than of weakness. The Qur'an makes the same connection elsewhere in explicit terms. A prophet tells his people: *“O My servants who have exceeded the limits against their souls! Do not lose hope in Allah's mercy, for Allah certainly forgives all sins”* (39:53). Abraham's own household asks: *“Who would despair of the mercy of their Lord except the misguided?”* (15:56). Verse 87 stands inside that tradition, and it is spoken by a father to sons who have every worldly reason to despair and no revealed reason to. The audience makes the sentence an instruction rather than a comfort. These are men who have lost a brother, a surety and now their father's sight, and who are being sent back to the country where all three losses happened. Jacob tells them that despair would be a statement about God, and that the evidence does not entitle them to make it.

**Despair as a Claim About God**

The reasoning behind the prohibition is worth stating. To despair of mercy is to assert that a situation has exceeded the range of what God will do, which is a claim about God rather than about the situation. Jacob's own circumstances are the test case: two sons gone, a third self-exiled in Egypt, his sight failing, a famine over the region, and a family report he does not believe. The verse asks men inside those circumstances to continue expecting relief, not because the circumstances suggest it but because the alternative is a judgement about divine capacity. The chapter then spends thirteen verses showing the expectation met in a way none of the parties could have predicted. The unpredictability is part of the lesson. What Jacob expects is news; what arrives is a reunion, a restoration of sight, the relocation of an entire household and the fulfilment of a dream reported eighty-three verses earlier. Hope directed at God's mercy is not a prediction about method, and expecting one particular outcome is a different act from refusing to despair.

**The Verse Sends Them Back to Egypt**

Structurally verse 87 is the mechanism that produces the climax. Without it there is no third journey, no plea in verse 88, no question in verse 89, no disclosure in verse 90, no reunion in verse 100. A grieving father's conviction becomes a commission, the commission becomes a journey, and the journey becomes a recognition scene. The sūrah is careful to attribute the movement to Jacob rather than to the brothers: they do not decide to return, they are sent. That attribution matters for the shape of the ending, since the reconciliation the chapter arrives at is initiated by the person who was injured and by the father who lost both sons. Neither of them has to act, and both do. Joseph could have kept his family in Canaan and simply continued the grain shipments; Jacob could have refused to risk a tenth son after losing two. The search is commissioned by the two people with least to gain from it and most to lose, and the chapter records the initiative without praising it.

**Searching for a Dead Son as an Act of Faith**

The commission to look for Joseph is, on the family's own account, an order to search for a corpse that was never recovered. Jacob accepted the report about the wolf in verse 18; he did not dispute the fact of Joseph's disappearance, only the explanation of it. Sending men to inquire after a son lost decades earlier is therefore not a practical measure in any ordinary sense, and the verse does not present it as one. It presents it as the consequence of two claims made in the preceding verse — knowledge from God, and a refusal to despair of mercy. The brothers carry out the instruction, which is itself notable: nine men who do not share their father's conviction travel into a famine to look for a brother they believe was eaten by an animal. The chapter records the obedience without commenting on the belief that motivated it.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:88

> When they entered Joseph’s presence, they pleaded, “O Chief Minister! We and our family have been touched with hardship, and we have brought only a few worthless coins, but ˹please˺ give us our supplies in full and be charitable to us. Indeed, Allah rewards the charitable.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Third Journey Has a Different Purpose**

The commentators note that the brothers travelled to Egypt this time to inquire about Joseph and Benjamin, in accordance with their father's instruction (12:87), rather than only to buy grain. The verse does not report that purpose in their speech. What they say to the official concerns hardship, inadequate goods and a request for full measure. The search commissioned by Jacob is therefore conducted silently, in the same audience as the commercial plea, and the chapter keeps it out of the dialogue. The reader knows both purposes are in the room. The man being addressed knows one of them, since he arranged the other.

That silence has a consequence for the scene. Because the brothers do not mention Joseph, nothing in their speech prompts the question they receive in verse 89. They ask about grain and charity; they are answered about a brother they believe to be dead. The chapter thus makes the disclosure wholly unforced by the visitors. It arises from the person addressed rather than from anything said to him, which is what allows verse 89 to land as a revelation instead of as a reply.

**"Hardship Has Touched Us and Our Family"**

The word denotes affliction that has made contact — hardship that has touched them, not merely threatened them. The commentators gloss it as intense hunger, and the verse extends it beyond the travellers to their household. This is the third or fourth year of a seven-year famine (12:48), the stores carried home on earlier journeys have been consumed, and the family in Canaan is described in the same terms as the family in Egypt. The plea is therefore not a bargaining position. It is a statement of condition, and the condition is what makes the request in the second half of the verse both necessary and difficult to make.

The extension to the family is also a rhetorical move. The brothers are not describing their own discomfort on a long journey; they are describing a household that includes an ageing father whose sight has failed from weeping and whose youngest son is detained in this same country. Everything the officials might need in order to be moved is contained in the phrase, and the brothers do not elaborate on any of it. Verse 89 will show what the official actually does with a plea of this kind: he ignores its content entirely and asks about a well.

**"A Few Worthless Coins"**

The brothers describe what they have brought: meagre merchandise, goods of little value. The commentators understand this as the small amount of trade goods or money they possessed to exchange for food. The admission is remarkable in a negotiation. A buyer normally conceals the weakness of his position; these men state it in their opening address, before making their request. The verse is describing people who have nothing left to bargain with and who know it. What they offer instead is a description of their need and an appeal to the official's character, which is the only currency remaining to them in a famine.

The admission also has a history in this chapter. On the first journey they arrived with eleven camels and requested eleven loads (12:59). On the second they had their money returned to them unasked and argued from it at home (12:62, 12:65). Now they bring little and say so. The three journeys trace the exhaustion of a household, and the verse records the endpoint in the brothers' own words rather than in the narrator's. Nothing about their appearance, their numbers or their camels is described; what the reader is given is their own assessment of what they are worth as customers.

**"Give Us Our Supplies in Full"**

The request uses the same standard Joseph set for himself in verse 59, when he asked the brothers whether they could not see that he gave full measure. They have watched him meet that standard twice, and they now ask for it a third time while admitting they cannot pay for it. The request is therefore grounded in his own published conduct rather than in their entitlement. The chapter is showing the brothers arguing from what they have observed — the same method they used in Egypt when denying the theft (12:73) — and the observation happens to be accurate. The man they are addressing is the one who established the standard.

**"And Be Charitable to Us"**

The commentators understand this clause as a request for the return of Benjamin, which means the plea has two objects: grain for the family and the release of a brother. The verse does not distinguish them, and the wording allows both readings at once. *Taṣaddaq ʿalaynā* — be charitable toward us, give us something beyond what is owed. In a rationing economy the phrase covers a supplementary measure of food; in the situation these brothers are actually in, it covers a detained son. The ambiguity is the verse's device. The brothers ask for charity without specifying which of the two things they need, and the answer they receive is neither.

**"Allah Rewards the Charitable"**

The closing clause invokes a religious maxim to an Egyptian official. The brothers have used the same method before, appealing to what their interlocutors already know (12:73), and here they appeal to a principle of recompense. The irony is complete and the chapter does not mark it. They are asking for charity from the most charitable person in the country — a man who fed them at full measure twice, returned their money into their bags unasked (12:62), and has just disclosed himself to the brother they are pleading for (12:69). The maxim they quote is true, and its fulfilment is standing in front of them, and none of them knows it.

**From Eleven Camel-Loads to a Few Coins**

The verse measures the family's decline in the chapter's own units. In verse 59 ten brothers arrived with eleven camels and requested eleven loads. In verse 65 they argued for an additional load as something easily secured. Here they bring a small quantity of low-value goods and ask for full measure as a favour. The distance between those positions is the famine, and the famine is the thing Joseph predicted and administered. The chapter does not draw the connection, but the arithmetic is visible: the men who once requested an extra camel-load are now asking a stranger for charity, and the stranger is the brother whose sale funded nothing and whose foresight is feeding the region that sold him.

**What the Brothers Do Not Ask For**

The plea is confined to food and to charity, and its omissions are as informative as its contents. The brothers do not ask for Benjamin's release by name, although one reading of the request for charity covers it. They do not mention their eldest brother, who has stayed in Egypt under a vow and whom they have now joined. They do not refer to the oath their father took from them, or to the penalty they themselves stated in verse 75, or to the substitution they offered in verse 78 and had refused. They do not mention Joseph at all. What they bring to the minister of Egypt is a household's hunger and a small purse, presented without leverage and without history, to a man who has the whole of that history and has just been told by his own father's sons nothing at all.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:89

> He asked, “Do you remember what you did to Joseph and his brother in your ignorance?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question Instead of an Answer**

The brothers have asked for grain and for charity. Joseph answers neither. The verse records a question about an event thirty or forty years old, in Canaan, involving a boy and a family that no Egyptian official has any reason to know about. The non-answer is the disclosure. Nothing else in the chapter could have made these men understand who was speaking to them, and the chapter chooses the least expected route: not a name, not a sign, not a removal of the barrier between them, but a sentence that only one person in the world could have constructed. The plea of verse 88 is left hanging, and the reader is given the reason in the following verse.

**"What You Did to Joseph and His Brother"**

The commentators specify the content of the question. It concerns the separation of Joseph from Benjamin when they were young, and the harm done to Joseph together with the injustices done to Benjamin — debasing him and reviling him after his brother was taken. The chapter's own narrative supports the first half: the brothers planned the disposal of one son (12:8–10), executed it (12:15), and reported it with a bloodied shirt (12:16–18). The treatment of the remaining child is not narrated anywhere in the sūrah, and the question supplies it. Joseph and Benjamin are named together as joint objects of the same conduct, which is a claim the brothers have never had to answer before. The pairing also revises the family's account of itself. For twenty years the brothers have lived with one crime and one victim; this sentence places a second victim beside the first and describes what followed as continuous rather than concluded. Benjamin's separation, their father's blindness and their own guilt are gathered into a single act with a single set of authors.

**"When You Were Ignorant"**

The qualifying phrase is the verse's most discussed feature. The commentators read it as Joseph making an excuse for his brothers: they did not know the vileness of what they were doing, or they were in the general state of ignorance that belongs to youth. The phrase is not an accusation softened by circumstance; it is an accusation that includes its own mitigation, offered by the person injured. Nothing in the chapter obliges Joseph to supply it. He could have asked the question bare, and the men in front of him would have had no answer to a bare version. Instead the verse contains a judgement and a concession in a single sentence, and the concession comes from the injured party rather than from the offenders.

**The Question Names What an Egyptian Could Not Know**

Ten brothers stand before the minister of Egypt's granaries. They have told him about a father, a brother and a household in Canaan. They have not told him about a well, a caravan, a sale or a shirt. The question refers to all of it, and refers to it as something the hearers did. The disclosure is therefore epistemic rather than physical: no curtain is drawn back, no identity is announced, and the recognition has to be produced by the content of a sentence. The chapter has prepared for this moment since verse 58, where one side knew and the other did not, and it resolves the asymmetry with words rather than with a reveal. The resolution makes the scene a recognition rather than a reveal. Joseph produces no evidence, summons no witness and recounts no history; he asks a question that only a brother could ask, and the answer identifies the men before him as surely as any face would. What has been concealed for thirteen verses is disclosed by a sentence that could come from nowhere else.

**How They Recognized Him**

The commentators record three accounts. One holds that the brothers realised who was speaking to them when he said these words. Another reports that recognition came when he smiled. A third states that he removed his crown and they saw him. The Qur'an gives none of these details; verse 90 reports only their question back. The variety of the traditions is worth noting because it shows the exegetes aware that the text does not describe the moment of recognition, and that a moment described only through a question needs some account of how the hearers understood it. The chapter's own answer is in the following verse, where their reply is a question rather than a statement. The question is the recognition. By asking whether he is indeed Joseph the brothers concede that the possibility has occurred to them, that the evidence before them points that way, and that they need confirmation before acting on it. The chapter treats that suspension between suspicion and certainty as the true moment of recognition, and it lasts exactly one verse.

**The Injury Is Named Before the Confession**

The order matters for everything that follows. Joseph states what was done before the brothers admit anything. Verse 91 has them acknowledge preference and sin; verse 97 has them ask their father to pray for their forgiveness. Neither admission comes first. The chapter is describing a reconciliation initiated entirely by the injured party: he names the injury, he supplies the mitigation, he discloses himself, and he forgives (12:92), all before any confession is offered. The structure excludes the possibility that the forgiveness is a response to repentance. It is prior to it, and it is what makes the repentance possible. The order makes the confession specific rather than general. A man who says we were wrong has said almost nothing; a man who has just heard the injury enumerated — you did this, in ignorance, to these two — can only respond by accepting the enumeration. Joseph's description is the form his brothers' admission will take, and the chapter supplies the form first.

**"His Brother" Speaks of Himself in the Third Person**

Joseph does not say *me*. He says Joseph and his brother, and the phrasing keeps him inside the sentence as an object rather than as the speaker. The effect is twofold. It preserves the concealment for the length of the question, since a first-person account would disclose him immediately. And it describes the crime accurately: what was done was done to two children, one of whom was taken and one of whom was left behind with the people who took him. The third person is not a device of coyness. It is the form in which the injury can be stated completely, and the chapter uses it once, at the exact moment when the statement is needed. Its use here is precise. The phrase completes the pairing established in the question, so injury and forgiveness are both addressed to two brothers rather than to one, and it does so without breaking the disclosure. Joseph names himself and names Benjamin in the same construction, and the construction tells his brothers that the man before them has been counting them both.

**The Verse Contains No Anger**

Nothing in the wording marks the speaker's feeling. There is no reproach, no reminder of the well, no reference to the shirt, the sale, the twenty dirhams or the years in prison. Joseph asks whether they know what they did, adds that they were ignorant when they did it, and stops. The chapter has shown him suppressing a far sharper response in verse 77, and it shows the suppression ending here — but what emerges is a question, not an accusation. A reader expecting the confrontation to carry the weight of thirty years will find the verse almost administrative in tone, and that is the point. The sūrah has been building toward this sentence for eighty-eight verses and delivers it without emphasis, because the emphasis has already been supplied by everything that came before it.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:90

> They replied ˹in shock˺, “Are you really Joseph?” He said, “I am Joseph, and here is my brother ˹Benjamin˺! Allah has truly been gracious to us. Surely whoever is mindful ˹of Allah˺ and patient, then certainly Allah never discounts the reward of the good-doers.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question Back, Not a Recognition**

The brothers do not say his name. They ask it: are you indeed Joseph? The interrogative form is the verse's record of shock, and it tells the reader something about the thirty years between the well and this room. A man they believed dead, whom they have just heard described in the third person by an Egyptian official, is being identified by them tentatively and in the form of a question. The chapter does not describe their faces, their voices or their movements. It gives a question and an answer, and the question's form carries the entire weight of the recognition. Nothing else in the sūrah has been awaited this long or resolved this briefly. The question is also self-protection. To answer directly would require the brothers to state what they did, and Joseph has not asked them to; he has asked whether he is right about who they are. The chapter lets them keep the interrogative form for one more verse, and the admission arrives in the verse after that, in their own words and undemanded.

**"I Am Joseph, and Here Is My Brother"**

The answer has two halves and the second is as important as the first. Joseph identifies himself and immediately presents Benjamin, who is standing beside him — the brother whose detention produced this journey, whose bag carried the cup, and who has known the truth since verse 69. The disclosure therefore resolves two losses at once, and it does so in a single sentence. A father in Canaan believes both sons are gone; the verse puts both of them in the same room, named and present. The chapter has withheld this moment for thirty-two verses and delivers it in nine words, which is characteristic of how the sūrah handles its climaxes. The compression is the point. No account is given, no reproach is made, no explanation of the years is offered, and the speaker does not describe what was done to him. The disclosure consists of two names and a demonstrative, and everything the reader has waited for since verse 4 arrives inside it without a single additional word.

**"Allah Has Truly Been Gracious to Us"**

The verb denotes kindness, favour, a subtle and generous bestowal. The pronoun is plural — to us — and it includes Benjamin in the account. Joseph does not summarise his own story here: no reference to the well, the sale, the prison or the elevation. What he says is that God has been kind to the two of them. The chapter will expand the summary in verse 100, in the presence of his parents, where he names the prison, the desert, the rivalry and the subtlety of divine action. Here the summary is one clause, and its object is the pair of brothers rather than the individual who suffered most. The plural is the sentence's most remarkable feature. Joseph has been sold, enslaved, falsely accused, imprisoned and forgotten, and his summary of all of it is a statement about what God did for both of them. He claims no compensation and describes no personal survival; he describes a favour conferred on a pair of brothers, and he includes the brother who did him no harm.

**The Maxim Is Stated to the Men Who Broke It**

The second half of the answer is not addressed to the situation in front of him. It is a general proposition: whoever is mindful and patient, God does not discount the reward of the good-doers. The brothers have neither been mindful nor patient. They plotted against a child, sold him, lied to their father about him, and have just been asked what they did. The maxim is therefore not a description of them, and it is not a compliment. It is the standard by which the last thirty years are to be measured, stated by the person who met it, to the people who did not. Verse 91 shows what they do with it. What they do is agree. The men who threw their brother into a well and sold him are told that fear of God and patience produce a reward that is never wasted, and their response is to confirm the statement from their own experience rather than to dispute its application to themselves. The maxim is delivered to the worst possible audience and it lands.

**Two Conditions Named Together**

Mindfulness — *taqwā*, the guarded consciousness that produces restraint — and patience. The chapter has documented both in Joseph's case and their absence in his brothers'. Restraint kept him from the door of the ʿAzīz's house (12:24) and from answering the insult in verse 77. Patience held him through years in a prison he could have left (12:50). The brothers' conduct is the inverse: a plot that seemed fair to them (12:18), a report composed in advance (12:16–18), a penalty proposed for a brother (12:75), a slander about a dead one (12:77). The maxim names the two qualities whose absence produced the injury Joseph has just described. The absence is what eighty-nine verses have been documenting. Fear of God would have stopped the well; patience would have carried the twenty years that followed. Joseph offers the maxim neither as rebuke nor as boast, and he does not need to: the two qualities he names are the two his brothers lacked, and they are hearing them described by the man who supplied both.

**"God Does Not Discount the Reward of the Good-Doers"**

The closing phrase returns the chapter to its own earlier statement. Verse 56 attached it to Joseph's establishment in Egypt: *“We never discount the reward of the good-doers”* (12:56). The same assurance appears as an instruction to the Prophet ﷺ in a Meccan sūrah: *“And be patient! Certainly Allah does not discount the reward of the good-doers”* (11:115), and in the context of hardship borne in a righteous cause (9:120). Joseph's use of it here is the chapter's own verdict on his story, spoken by him rather than by the narrator. The verb means to cause to be lost, and the claim is that nothing is lost — including the years that produced no visible result at the time.

**The Chapter's Theology in Joseph's Mouth**

Verse 90 is the point at which the sūrah's argument and its narrative coincide. Everything the chapter has said about knowledge taught by God (12:21), mercy given according to a will (12:56), the soul that commands evil (12:53), and stratagem that does not reach its destination (12:52) is now summarised by the person those statements were about. He does not explain himself, describe his suffering, or ask for an account of it. He states a rule and a fact: God has been gracious to us, and the reward of the mindful and patient is not discounted. Verse 100 will apply the rule to the whole story in his father's presence; verse 92 will apply it to the men standing in the room.

**Thirty Years Answered in Two Sentences**

The disclosure resolves more than an identity. It answers the report about the wolf (12:17), the bloodied shirt (12:18), the slander in verse 77, the grief that cost Jacob his sight (12:84), the rebuke in verse 85 and the commission in verse 87. Every one of those events was caused by a false belief about a single fact, and verse 90 states the fact. The chapter's economy is striking: thirty or forty years of consequence are undone in a sentence of nine words, and the sentence is followed not by an accounting but by a maxim about patience. Joseph does not collect what is owed to him. He states the rule that explains how he survived, and he states it to the men whose conduct made the survival necessary, which is the closest thing in the sūrah to an explanation of the last three decades.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:91

> They admitted, “By Allah! Allah has truly preferred you over us, and we have surely been sinful.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**An Oath Introduces an Admission**

The brothers begin with *tallāh*, by God — the formula they have used throughout this chapter. They swore by it when reporting the loss of Joseph with a bloodied shirt in hand (12:16), when denying the theft in Egypt (12:73), and when predicting that their father's grief would kill him (12:85). Here the same formula introduces the first true statement about themselves that the chapter records. The repetition is not accidental. A family that has sworn to a lie, sworn to a denial and sworn to a prediction now swears to a confession, and the chapter uses the identical opening word for all four so that the difference is located entirely in the content. The uniformity makes the four statements comparable, and the comparison is unflattering. Each of Joseph's disclosures has been sworn and each of the brothers' responses has been sworn too, while the content of the two sets could hardly be further apart. The chapter lets the formal parallel run so that the moral distance between the speakers is measured by the reader without narration.

**"God Has Preferred You Over Us"**

The verb denotes preference, choosing one thing over another. The brothers are not merely congratulating Joseph on his station; they are attributing the difference between them to a divine act. The commentators do not restrict the preference to any single respect, and the chapter supports several at once: Joseph received knowledge and wisdom at maturity (12:22), the interpretation of events (12:21), an office over the country's stores (12:55), and an establishment in the land (12:56). The brothers name the preference in the general form because that is the form in which they have just experienced it — a man they sold is feeding them, forgiving them and instructing them. The preference is stated as something observed rather than as doctrine accepted. They have watched Joseph govern a famine, spare a brother, refuse revenge and articulate a maxim about patience, and their comparison between him and themselves is drawn from that record. The confession is not a theological claim but a summary of the last thirteen verses, offered by the people who lived them.

**The Dream of Verse 4 Answered by the Dreamer's Brothers**

Joseph's first vision showed eleven stars, a sun and a moon prostrating to him (12:4), and his father warned him not to relate it to his brothers lest they plot against him (12:5). The plot happened anyway. Verse 91 is the beginning of the fulfilment: ten of the eleven stars are standing in front of Joseph, addressing him as the one God has preferred over them, and admitting a fault. The prostration itself is reserved for verse 100, when the parents arrive. The chapter divides the fulfilment across two scenes and places the brothers' submission in words here and in gesture later, which is why the recognition scene occupies ten verses rather than one. The length measures what the fulfilment costs. One verse could have reported the brothers bowing; ten are needed to carry a search, a detention, a private conference, a journey home, a father's blindness, a second journey, a disclosure, an admission and a forgiveness. The dream is fulfilled at the end of a moral process rather than at the end of a plot.

**"We Have Surely Been Sinful"**

The admission is emphatic and its scope is not limited. The brothers do not specify which act they are confessing — the well, the sale, the shirt, the report to their father, the slander about a theft in verse 77. They state a condition: we have been people who err. The chapter's own vocabulary supports the breadth of the claim. The word family used here concerns missing the mark and being at fault, and it is the same register in which verse 89 described their conduct as done in ignorance. What Joseph supplies as a mitigation, they accept as a description of themselves, and they accept it without qualification or exception. The absence of qualification is what makes it a confession. No circumstance is offered, no blame is distributed, no reference is made to youth, to their father's favour or to the pressure of the moment. The sentence has a subject, a verb and an adjective, and the brothers add no fourth element, which is the difference between an admission and an explanation.

**The Admission Follows the Maxim, Not the Disclosure**

Order matters here. Joseph identified himself and stated a rule — whoever is mindful and patient does not have his reward discounted (12:90). The brothers' confession comes after the rule and before the forgiveness. Nothing in the chapter suggests that the confession caused the forgiveness; verse 92 will grant it unconditionally, and Joseph has already named the injury and supplied the excuse in verse 89. The sequence therefore describes a confession that is not a plea. The brothers are not bargaining. They have been told who is speaking to them and what he thinks about patience, and they respond by agreeing with the second half of that assessment rather than by asking for anything. That ordering tells us what the chapter considers decisive. Learning who Joseph is would have produced shock; hearing what he believes about patience and the fear of God produces agreement. The brothers are moved not by the identity of the man in front of them but by the accuracy of his description of them, and that accuracy is something they can verify from their own history.

**The Same Words Are Said Again to Their Father**

Verse 97 records a second admission, addressed to Jacob after the family is reunited: the brothers ask him to pray for their forgiveness and add that they have certainly been sinful. The wording echoes this verse almost exactly, and the repetition is deliberate. The chapter has the confession made twice — once to the brother they injured and once to the father they deceived — because the crime had two victims and each requires a separate acknowledgment. The sīrah of the story is therefore complete only at verse 98, when Jacob agrees to intercede. Verse 91 is the first of the two admissions, and it is made to the person who has the least reason to accept it.

**What the Brothers Still Do Not Know**

The confession is comprehensive in tone but limited in knowledge. The brothers do not yet know that Joseph placed the cup in Benjamin's bag (12:70), that he searched their bags in a calculated order (12:76), that he disclosed himself privately to Benjamin before any of this (12:69), or that their detention of a second brother was arranged rather than accidental. Verse 100 will supply part of the explanation in Joseph's own terms, attributing the separation to Satan's stirring of rivalry between him and his brothers. Until then the brothers are confessing a crime they remember while standing inside a sequence they do not understand, and the chapter leaves them in that position for nine more verses.

**The Preference They Name Is the One They Tried to Prevent**

Verse 5 recorded the reason for the plot: Joseph and his brother were dearer to their father than the rest of them, and they said their father was clearly mistaken. The preference they resented was paternal and they acted to end it. The preference they now name is divine, and it has been confirmed by everything that followed — an office, a country's grain, a famine survived, a family fed. The chapter sets the two statements fifty verses apart and lets the reader compare them. What the brothers tried to reverse by disposing of a child was not reversed; it was enlarged, transferred from a father's affection to a ruler's authority, and returned to them in the form of the brother they removed. Their admission in verse 91 is therefore an acknowledgment that the plot achieved the opposite of its aim.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:92

> Joseph said, “There is no blame on you today. May Allah forgive you! He is the Most Merciful of the merciful!

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Word Used Twice in the Qur'an**

The noun Joseph chooses for what he is withholding denotes reproach, upbraiding, the rubbing of a fault into a person's face. The commentators describe the phrase as a beautiful form of forgiveness, and note that the word can also carry the sense of physical punishment. Both senses are relevant. Joseph is refusing to blame these men and refusing to punish them, and he is doing both in a single clause that names the thing he will not do rather than the thing he has done. The chapter's use of the term is economical: one word covers the reproach they expect, the penalty they might fear, and the accounting they have just offered in verse 91.

**"Today" Fixes the Amnesty in Time**

The temporal particle is significant. The brothers stand before Joseph on the day of their lowest position: a famine has stripped them of goods, their youngest brother is detained under a penalty they proposed, their eldest is stranded by a vow, their father's sight has failed from weeping, and they have just confessed. The amnesty is dated to that day. Joseph does not wait for evidence of reform, for a period of probation, or for his father's judgement. The forgiveness is immediate and it is announced at the moment when the offenders are least able to offer anything in exchange for it, which is the condition under which forgiveness is distinguishable from negotiation. The date also forecloses the future. A forgiveness without a time reference could be withdrawn the next time the offender disappointed; a forgiveness dated today closes the matter at the moment it is spoken. Joseph is not promising patience with his brothers going forward, he is declaring that the case is finished, and the chapter records the declaration as the end of the accusation.

**The Prophet ﷺ Used These Words at the Conquest of Makkah**

The tradition records that Joseph's sentence became the model for the Prophet's own amnesty. During the conquest of Makkah, when the city had been overtaken and the Quraysh had no hope of escape, he asked them what they expected him to do with them. They answered that they expected good from a noble brother, the son of a noble brother. He then said that he spoke to them as Joseph had spoken to his brothers: there is no reproach against you — go, for you are free. The report is transmitted in the sīrah and tafsīr literature, and its significance is that the Qur'anic verse was treated by the Prophet ﷺ as a precedent for a political amnesty granted to people who had persecuted him for two decades.

**He Does Not Forgive in His Own Name**

The second clause is a prayer: may God forgive you. Joseph has removed the reproach that belonged to him, and the sin that does not belong to him he refers to God. The distinction is exact and it matters. A person can waive an injury done to himself; he cannot absolve an offence against God. The brothers' crime involved both — a brother betrayed and a father deceived, a trust broken before God and an oath sworn in His name (12:66). Joseph's sentence handles the first by renouncing it and the second by interceding for it, and the chapter will show the intercession being taken up by the father in verses 97 and 98. The referral is both humble and practical. Forgiveness given in God's name cannot be measured against the forgiver's feelings, which is what makes it reliable; it also opens a route for the brothers' remaining obligation, since an offence against God requires an approach to God. Joseph supplies the first half here and the chapter supplies the second in verses 97 and 98.

**"The Most Merciful of the Merciful"**

The superlative closes the verse and it belongs to a family of usage the chapter has already established. Jacob used the same formula when refusing to trust his sons with Benjamin, pairing it with the statement that God is the best of keepers (12:64). Moses used it after the golden calf, asking forgiveness for himself and his brother (7:151). In each instance the formula is spoken by someone who has been wronged or bereaved and has no remedy left. Joseph's use of it here places him inside that pattern and answers his father's earlier words from the same vocabulary — the son forgives in the terms the father trusted. The shared vocabulary binds the two halves of the family. Jacob used the same superlative when he consented to let Benjamin go, transferring the risk to God rather than minimising it; Joseph uses it when he releases his brothers from blame, transferring the debt rather than collecting it. Father and son are described in identical terms at the two decisive moments of the sūrah.

**The Qur'an's General Ground for This Act**

Nothing in the verse argues for forgiveness, but the Qur'an supplies the argument elsewhere in explicit terms. Believers are told: *“Let them pardon and forgive. Do you not love to be forgiven by Allah?”* (24:22). The reward for pardoning is stated as exceeding the equivalence that justice would require: *“whoever pardons and seeks reconciliation, then their reward is with Allah”* (42:40), and *“whoever endures patiently and forgives — surely this is a resolve to aspire to”* (42:43). Verse 92 is the narrative instance of those rulings, and verse 90 has already supplied the other half of the pairing — patience — in Joseph's own mouth moments before. The pairing is completed across two verses by the same speaker. Joseph describes himself as one who fears God and is patient, and then forgives in the name of the Most Merciful; the qualities he claims and the quality he invokes belong to one account of why the amnesty is possible. The chapter refuses to separate the ethics from the theology behind them.

**The Forgiveness Precedes the Full Account**

Verse 100 will contain Joseph's own reading of the last thirty years: God was kind to him when He freed him from prison and brought his family from the desert after Satan had stirred up rivalry between him and his brothers. The amnesty in verse 92 is given before that explanation, and before the brothers have any idea of the arrangement they were inside. They are forgiven without being shown the mechanism, and the mechanism is disclosed to a family already reconciled rather than as a condition of reconciliation. The chapter's order is deliberate: forgiveness first, understanding afterwards, and the understanding attributed to a third party rather than to the offenders. Both choices protect the offenders. Forgiveness offered before the account means the brothers are not required to justify themselves in order to be released; understanding attributed to God means their eventual comprehension is treated as a gift rather than as an achievement. Joseph removes from the transaction every element that would have let him hold something over the men standing in front of him.

**Nothing Is Required of the Brothers**

The verse attaches no condition to the amnesty. Joseph does not ask for an explanation of the well, does not request the return of the price he was sold for, does not require a public acknowledgment in Canaan, and does not make the forgiveness contingent on the brothers' future conduct. Verse 91 has already supplied a confession, and verse 92 accepts it without building on it. What follows is practical rather than penitential: a shirt to carry, a father to heal, a family to bring. The chapter's account of reconciliation contains no rite, no restitution and no probation, and the absence is the point — the injured party simply stops holding the matter, in the same sentence in which he asks God to forgive it.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:93

> Go with this shirt of mine and cast it over my father’s face, and he will regain his sight. Then come back to me with your whole family.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Commands in One Verse**

The verse contains an instruction about an object and an instruction about a journey. Take this shirt, cast it over my father's face, and he will see; then bring me your family, all together. The first command concerns a single person and a single act, and the second concerns a whole household and a migration. The chapter places them together because they belong to a single purpose: the restoration of Jacob. Sight is restored by the shirt, and the reason for wanting the sight restored is that there will be a son to see. Verse 99 will report both commands fulfilled — the parents received, the family entering Egypt — and verse 100 will show the result.

**The Third Shirt in the Chapter**

Shirts have carried the chapter's evidence from beginning to end. The first was stained with false blood and produced to a father as proof that a wolf had devoured his son (12:18). The second was torn from behind and examined by a household witness, establishing that Joseph was fleeing rather than attacking (12:26–28). The third is this one, and its function reverses the first: a garment belonging to Joseph is carried to Jacob and restores what a garment falsely attributed to him destroyed. The chapter never comments on the pattern. It simply uses the same object three times, for a lie, for a truth and for a healing, and leaves the reader to notice the sequence. The sequence is the chapter's most sustained symbolism and it is never explained. A garment dipped in false blood establishes a death, a garment torn from behind establishes an innocence, and a garment carried to Canaan establishes a healing. Each is offered as evidence, each is trusted by whoever receives it, and the third undoes what the first asserted twenty years earlier.

**"Cast It Over My Father's Face"**

The instruction specifies the face, and the specification is not incidental. Verse 84 reported the injury in the same location: his eyes turned white from the grief he suppressed. The remedy is applied where the damage was done. The commentators do not elaborate on the mechanism, and the verse offers none — no explanation of how a garment affects sight, no statement that the shirt has a property, no claim about the material. What is given is a command, a location and a predicted result. The chapter's economy here is at its most severe: three clauses, and the middle one is a physical instruction. The physical instruction is what makes the healing concrete. Joseph does not say that his father will be restored or that God will cure him; he specifies an object, an action and a body part, and attaches the outcome to the performance. The chapter treats the recovery of sight as an event with a method, ordinary enough to be carried out by a travelling son.

**A Prediction Stated as Certain**

*He will regain his sight.* Joseph does not say perhaps, or if God wills, or that he hopes. The prediction is unconditional, and the commentators explain it as based on divine inspiration rather than on medical judgement. Nothing in Joseph's knowledge of his father would support the claim: he has not seen Jacob in decades, does not know that his eyes have failed, and has received no report of the injury. Verse 84 describes the whiteness of the eyes in Canaan, and verse 93 predicts its reversal in Egypt. The chapter therefore attributes the prediction to the same source as Joseph's other knowledge, which verse 21 described as taught by God. That source has been established repeatedly and is not restated here. The interpreter of two prison dreams, the forecaster of fifteen years of Egyptian weather and the man who told his brother not to grieve now predicts a blind father's recovery, and the chapter places the prediction in the same category as the others: knowledge taught to him, delivered without mechanism, verified by the outcome.

**The Tradition About the Shirt's Origin**

One account records that this shirt was made of the silk of Paradise and was brought to Joseph by Gabriel while he was in the well (12:15). The report is not in the Qur'an and cannot be verified from it, but it explains why the exegetes looked for something unusual about the garment. A shirt that heals blindness by being placed on a face invites an account of its nature, and the tradition supplies one. The Qur'an's own text is content to describe the shirt as Joseph's — *my shirt* — and to attach the healing to a command rather than to a property of the cloth. Attaching the healing to the command keeps the theology clean. A cloth with intrinsic power would become an object of veneration; a cloth that heals because a prophet said to cast it is an instrument, and the power stays where the chapter puts it. The wording is careful to make the shirt a means rather than a cause, which is why it is called his shirt.

**"Bring Me Your Family, All Together"**

The second command names the whole household. The brothers are to return to Canaan and bring the family in its entirety, and the phrase used denotes the people belonging to a man — his household, his kin, those who follow him. The instruction completes the movement that began with the dream in verse 4: eleven stars, a sun and a moon are to be gathered into one place. Joseph does not ask for a visit or for his father alone. He asks for the migration of a family, which is the precondition of verse 99 and verse 100 and of the settlement of the Israelites in Egypt that the rest of the Qur'an's narrative assumes. The comprehensiveness completes the reversal. Joseph was taken from his family alone, at night, by his own brothers, and sold out of the country; he now summons the whole household into it, on his own authority, described by relationship rather than by number. The well removed one boy from Canaan and this verse puts an entire family into Egypt.

**Verse 96 Reports the Fulfilment in the Same Terms**

The bearer of good news arrives, casts the shirt over Jacob's face, and he regains his sight (12:96). The chapter reports the healing in a clause as short as the prediction, and Jacob's response is to remind his children that he had told them he knew from God what they did not know. The verification is therefore complete and public: a man blind from weeping receives his sight by means of a garment sent from Egypt by a son he was told had been eaten by a wolf. Verse 93 is the instruction, verse 94 is the anticipation of its effect, and verse 96 is the report. The chapter gives the healing three verses and no explanation.

**The Shirt Travels the Route the Bloodied Shirt Travelled**

The object's journey reverses an earlier one. Decades before, a garment belonging to Joseph was carried from a well in the wilderness to Jacob in Canaan, stained with blood that was not his, and produced as evidence of his death (12:18). Now a garment belonging to Joseph is carried from Egypt to Jacob in Canaan and produced as the means of his recovery. The same route, the same object type, the same recipient, and the same sender in both cases — the brothers, who carried the first lie and now carry the second truth. The chapter does not draw the comparison. It arranges the two journeys so that the reader draws it, which is the method it has used for every structural echo in the sūrah.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:94

> When the caravan departed ˹from Egypt˺, their father said ˹to those around him˺, “You may think I am senile, but I certainly sense the smell of Joseph.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Scent Arrives Before the News**

The caravan has left Egypt and is on the road. Jacob is in Canaan. Between the two there is a journey of weeks, and the chapter collapses it in a single verse: as the caravan sets out, the father perceives his son's scent. Nothing has been reported to him, no messenger has been sent ahead, and the brothers themselves are still travelling. The verse therefore describes knowledge that arrives ahead of the event that produces it, and it does so through the most physical of senses. The chapter has been careful throughout to distinguish what Jacob knows from what he is told; this verse places him in possession of a fact nobody has communicated. That possession is what the rest of the sequence depends on. The caravan is still on the road, no messenger has been sent ahead, and Jacob is already describing something he cannot have been told. The chapter establishes the priority of his knowledge over the arrival of the evidence, so that when the shirt comes through the door it reads as confirmation rather than information.

**"When the Caravan Set Out"**

The temporal clause fixes the moment precisely, and the precision matters. It is not when the caravan arrived, not when the shirt was given to the brothers, and not when Joseph spoke the words of verse 93. It is when they departed — the beginning of the return journey. The commentators note the difficulty this creates and resolve it by reference to the shirt: the scent Jacob perceives is the heavenly fragrance that comes from it, carried from Egypt as the party sets out. On that account the perception is caused by an object already in motion. The chapter states the timing without stating the cause, and leaves the two readings — a natural carrying of scent and a miracle — side by side.

**He Is Speaking to People Around Him**

The verse specifies an audience. The commentators identify those present as members of his own tribe or household who were with him at the time, rather than the returning sons, who are still on the road. The specification resolves an apparent problem in the sequence: the brothers now know Joseph is alive, having met him in verse 90, so a remark dismissing their father's perception of Joseph cannot come from them while they are travelling. The people answering in verse 95 are therefore understood by some as those in Canaan. Other commentators attribute the reply to the sons, and the difference affects how verse 95 is read without changing what verse 94 says. The distinction changes the character of the doubt that follows. If the reply comes from his sons it is the scepticism of men who have spent twenty years disbelieving their father; if it comes from bystanders it is the ordinary reaction of a household to an old man's claim. Both readings make verse 95 a rebuke, and the chapter does not choose between them.

**"Unless You Think Me Senile"**

The clause is a pre-emptive concession, and it reveals what Jacob expects his statement to sound like. He announces the perception of a scent and immediately anticipates that his hearers will attribute it to a failing mind. The formulation is not defensive so much as exact: he knows that an old man in mourning, blind from weeping, who claims to smell a son lost decades ago, will be dismissed. The chapter gives him the self-awareness rather than leaving the dismissal to be discovered. Verse 95 shows the dismissal arriving in the terms he predicted, which is the sūrah's way of confirming that Jacob understood his own position inside his family perfectly well. That understanding is what makes his hope credible rather than pitiable. He knows how he sounds, he knows what his household has concluded about him, and he names the conclusion before anyone else can offer it. A man able to state the case against his own belief is not being deluded by it, and the chapter uses the clause to establish the difference.

**The Explanations Offered by the Commentators**

Two accounts are recorded. One holds that Jacob perceived the scent because of the heavenly fragrance belonging to the shirt, which was already travelling toward him. The other describes the perception as a miracle, on the grounds that both father and son were prophets and that God granted the father knowledge of the son's approach. The Qur'an states neither. What it states is the perception, the audience and the anticipated objection. The chapter's method here is identical to its treatment of Joseph's knowledge: the fact of prophetic awareness is reported and its mechanism is left aside, so that the reader is given the event rather than an explanation of it. Setting the mechanism aside keeps the event extraordinary. A scent carried across days of desert could be explained by wind, by miracle or by prophetic knowledge, and any single explanation would reduce the verse to a claim about that explanation. The chapter reports a father saying he smells his son, reports the household doubting him, reports him being right, and stops there.

**The Verse Fulfils the Claim of Verse 86**

Jacob told his sons that he complained of his sorrow only to God and that he knew from God what they did not know (12:86). Verse 87 sent them to search for Joseph and his brother on the strength of that knowledge. Verse 94 is the knowledge itself, arriving as a scent before the search party returns. The chapter does not connect the verses explicitly, but the sequence is unambiguous: a claim to know, a commission to search, and then the arrival of the thing searched for ahead of those who were sent to find it. Jacob is shown to have been right about the fact and wrong about nothing else, and the sons who doubted him are still on the road.

**A Prophet Disbelieved by His Own Household**

The verse sets up a pattern the chapter will complete in verse 95 and reverse in verse 96. A man perceives something true, anticipates that he will not be believed, and is not believed. The dismissal comes from the people closest to him, who have watched his grief for decades and have concluded that it has damaged his judgement. The Qur'an's account of prophecy repeatedly involves this structure — a messenger whose household or town treats his knowledge as madness or delusion. Here the structure is domestic and the accusation is gentle: not heresy but senility. Verse 96 will report the arrival of the bearer of good news and the restoration of Jacob's sight, and the household's assessment will be withdrawn in his own words.

**Contrast With the Biblical Account of the Same News**

The parallel passage describes a different reaction. When Jacob's sons tell him that Joseph is alive and ruler over Egypt, the biblical account reports that his heart grew faint because he did not believe them, and that his spirit revived only when he saw the wagons Joseph had sent to carry him (Genesis 45:26–28). Disbelief precedes the evidence and is overcome by it. The Qur'an reverses the order entirely: Jacob perceives Joseph's approach before any messenger arrives, predicts the dismissal he will receive, receives it, and is then vindicated by the arrival of the thing he already sensed. The two accounts share the wagons and the revival; they differ about whether the father needed convincing. The difference concerns the father's epistemic position. In one account Jacob's spirit revives when he sees the wagons; in this one he is already convinced before the wagons are visible, and their arrival answers a household's doubt rather than his own. The Qur'an gives him knowledge that the evidence comes to ratify, and it makes that knowledge the subject of two verses.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:95

> They replied, “By Allah! You are definitely still in your old delusion.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Shortest Verse in the Chapter's Final Movement**

Four words of dialogue occupy a verse of their own, and the allocation is a marker of weight. The chapter has used the same technique throughout its closing section: a promise given its own verse (12:61), a question given its own verse (12:71), a report given its own verse (12:81). Here a dismissal receives the same treatment. The brevity is not a lack of content but a concentration of it. Everything the family has believed about Joseph for decades, everything they have said to Jacob about his grief, and everything they are about to have corrected is contained in a single sentence, and the chapter lets the sentence stand alone. Its brevity gives the correction weight. A longer verse would have reasoned with the household, explained the scent or appealed to Jacob's record; this one asserts that they will find him neither senile nor deluded, and leaves the assertion to be tested by what arrives. The chapter is confident enough in the coming event to introduce it in a single unadorned sentence.

**"Your Old Delusion"**

The adjective is the verse's sharpest word. The speakers do not say their father is mistaken now; they say he is in his old, long-standing error — a condition they date to the past and present as continuous. The commentators gloss the reference as Jacob's excessive love for Joseph and his hope of meeting him again, which those around him considered unreasonable given what they took to be the facts. The adjective therefore does double work. It identifies the duration of the grief, and it identifies the speakers' long acquaintance with it: they have watched this hope for decades and have concluded that it is a fixed feature of an old man's mind rather than a belief about the world. Treating it as a feature rather than a belief is what makes the charge unanswerable at the time. A mistaken belief can be corrected by evidence; a disposition cannot, because every piece of evidence offered will be read as a symptom of it. Jacob's household has reached the point of dismissing his claims by reference to his age rather than examining them, and the verse records the dismissal in their words.

**An Oath Used to Dismiss a Prophet**

The reply opens with *tallāh*, by God — the same formula used in verse 91 by the brothers confessing their sin, in verse 73 denying the theft, in verse 85 predicting their father's death from grief, and in verse 16 supporting a false report. The chapter has used the formula five times and it now uses it for a dismissal. The accumulation is worth noticing without being moralised: an oath is a form of emphasis, and this family reaches for emphasis whenever it is most confident, including at the moments when it is most wrong. Verse 96 will show the confidence collapsing inside a single verse. The collapse is immediate and total. The household that swears by God that Jacob is mistaken receives a traveller, a shirt and a restoration of sight inside a single verse, and no further argument is addressed to them. The chapter sets the oath and its refutation beside each other with nothing in between, which is the most economical rebuttal available to it.

**Who Is Speaking, and When**

The identity of the speakers is disputed, and the dispute matters. Some commentators attribute the reply to those of Jacob's tribe who were with him in Canaan, which fits the timing: the caravan has only just departed Egypt (12:94) and the sons cannot yet be present. Others attribute it to the brothers, reading the exchange as part of a conversation that occurs on their return. The chapter does not settle the question, and both readings leave the sense intact: the people closest to Jacob dismiss his perception of Joseph. On the first reading the dismissal comes from a household that has never had cause to reconsider; on the second it comes from men who have just met the person being discussed.

**Two Charges in Two Verses**

Verse 85 accused Jacob of a grief that would kill him. Verse 95 accuses him of a delusion that has lasted decades. The two charges are different in kind: the first concerns the effect of his sorrow on his body, the second concerns the truth of his belief. Together they describe a family that has concluded both that their father is being destroyed by his mourning and that the object of the mourning is not real. The chapter does not have anyone answer these charges in argument. Verse 96 answers them with an event — a bearer of good news arrives, a shirt is cast over a face, and sight returns. Answering with an event is the chapter's characteristic method. Senility and delusion are claims about a mind, and no argument about a mind settles them; a blind man recovering his sight when a shirt is cast over his face settles them. This verse reports the charges, the next reports the arrival, and the juxtaposition performs the work of a rebuttal.

**The Verse Is the Low Point of Jacob's Position**

Read in sequence, verses 83 to 95 trace a steady deterioration. Jacob rejects a report, declares beautiful patience, hopes for a return of all three sons, turns away and weeps until his eyes fail, is rebuked for the grief, redirects his complaint to God, claims knowledge his family does not have, commissions a search, perceives a scent, anticipates dismissal, and is dismissed. Every step is recorded without relief and without support from anyone in his household. Verse 95 is the end of that descent, and it is the last thing said to Jacob before the reversal begins. The chapter gives it a verse of its own so that the reader marks the point. Marking it is an act of honesty about belief. For the length of one verse the sūrah shows a prophet who is not believed by his own household, whose claim cannot be checked and whose only support is a knowledge he cannot explain. That is where Jacob stands before the caravan arrives, and the chapter does not soften it by anticipating the arrival.

**Verse 96 Answers in Jacob's Own Words**

The reversal is immediate and it is total.
