The bearer of good news arrives, the shirt is cast over Jacob's face, his sight returns, and he says to his children: did I not tell you that I know from God what you do not know (12:96)? The reply is not an argument against the charge of delusion. It is a reminder that the charge was made against a claim already stated in verse 86 and now verified. The chapter's method is to answer a dismissal with an event and then let the dismissed person note that he had said so. Nothing in verse 95 is refuted directly; it is simply overtaken, in the space of one verse, by the arrival of what it denied.

**The Delusion Was the Only Accurate Belief in the Room**

The verse's irony is exact and the chapter states none of it. The people dismissing Jacob as deluded are either his household, who have believed for decades that a wolf ate his son, or his sons, who have just met that son in an Egyptian granary and watched him forgive them. On either reading the accusation is false in a way the accusers either cannot know or have just been shown. What the dismissed man believed — that Joseph was alive and that God would return all three sons to him (12:83) — is the only belief in the exchange that the sequel confirms. The chapter therefore closes the family's argument not by persuading anyone but by arriving at the father's position from a direction nobody in Canaan expected.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:96

> But when the bearer of the good news arrived, he cast the shirt over Jacob’s face, so he regained his sight. Jacob then said ˹to his children˺, “Did I not tell you that I truly know from Allah what you do not know?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Bearer of Good News From Among the Brothers**

The verse names its messenger by function rather than by name: the bearer of good news. The commentators identify him as the same brother who interceded when the others plotted to kill Joseph and proposed the well instead (12:10). The identification is not in the Qur'anic text, but it fits the chapter's method of assigning pivotal acts to particular individuals, and it gives the scene a shape worth noting. The man who once persuaded his brothers to spare a child now carries the news that the child is alive, and he carries it in the garment the grown man has sent. The chapter does not comment on the arrangement and does not name him.

**The Predicted Act Is Performed Exactly**

Verse 93 gave an instruction with three elements: take the shirt, cast it over my father's face, he will regain his sight. Verse 96 reports all three in order, with no variation and no elaboration. The messenger casts it; the sight returns. Nothing intervenes — no prayer, no explanation, no description of the moment. The chapter's economy at the point of fulfilment is characteristic: predictions are stated at length and fulfilments are reported in a clause. The same pattern governs the dream of verse 4 and its fulfilment in verse 100, and the famine forecast of verses 47 to 49, whose arrival is never narrated at all. The silence about the famine is as deliberate as the report of the healing. Fifteen years of Egyptian weather were forecast in detail and the sūrah never narrates a single one of them arriving; a shirt was promised to restore a blind man's sight and the restoration is recorded in the verse after it is dispatched. The chapter narrates what confirms a prophet's word and passes over what merely fills time.

**Healing Reported Without a Mechanism**

The verse does not say how the shirt restored the sight. It does not describe a property of the garment, a fragrance, a medical effect or a stated miracle. The commentators supply explanations — a heavenly scent belonging to the shirt, a miracle granted because both father and son were prophets — and the Qur'an supplies none. The text states a command, a performance and a result. That refusal to explain is consistent with the chapter's treatment of every extraordinary element in it: Joseph's interpretations are attributed to divine teaching (12:21) without the teaching being narrated, and Jacob's knowledge is attributed to God (12:86) without its transmission being described. The consistency gives the episode its character. Sight is restored and no account is offered of how; a scent is perceived across a distance and no account is offered of that either. The chapter reports both events in the same register as it reports a famine forecast, treating them as statements about God's action rather than as phenomena requiring an explanation.

**Jacob's Reminder Is a Question**

The father does not say *I told you so* in declarative form. He asks: did I not tell you that I know from God what you do not know? The interrogative is gentler than a statement and more complete. It recalls verse 86, where the same claim was made under rebuke, and verse 83, where the hope of all three sons returning was expressed against every available fact. The question places the claim, the doubt and the verification in a single sentence, and it does so without naming the people who doubted or the terms in which they doubted. The chapter's restraint here matches Jacob's own: the vindication is stated as a reminder rather than as a reproach.

**Charge Withdrawn by an Event, Not by an Argument**

Nothing in verse 96 argues against the dismissal of the previous verse. The word *delusion* is not answered with reasoning, evidence or authority. It is answered by a man who was blind and now sees, holding a shirt from a son who was dead and now rules Egypt. The chapter's method of resolving its domestic arguments is consistently this one: an event arrives and the argument becomes unnecessary. Verse 85's prediction that grief would kill Jacob is answered the same way, and so is verse 83's suspicion about the sons' report. The sūrah does not require its characters to be persuaded; it requires them to be overtaken. Being overtaken is what makes the withdrawal final. No argument is offered to the household, no appeal is made to Jacob's standing, and no rebuke is recorded; a man arrives with a shirt and the case against the old man's mind closes. The sūrah treats evidence as something that happens to people rather than as something they are persuaded by, and it happens here inside a single verse.

**Biblical Parallel Reverses the Order of Belief**

The corresponding passage describes a father who does not believe. When Jacob's sons tell him Joseph is alive and ruler over all Egypt, the biblical account reports that his heart grew faint, for he did not believe them; his spirit revived only when he saw the wagons Joseph had sent to carry him (Genesis 45:26–28). The Qur'anic sequence runs the other way. Jacob perceives his son's approach before any messenger arrives (12:94), anticipates the dismissal (12:94), receives it (12:95), and is then confirmed by the arrival of the shirt. Both accounts contain a father restored and a household convinced. They differ about whether the father ever needed convincing. The reversal changes who the verse is about. If belief arrives with the wagons the scene belongs to Jacob's relief; if belief precedes them the scene belongs to the household's error, and Jacob has been right throughout. The Qur'an chooses the second arrangement, and in doing so makes the father's knowledge the subject of three consecutive verses instead of an outcome reported in passing.

**One Son Returned, Two Still to Come**

The verse reports the healing and the reminder, and stops. Joseph is alive and has sent a garment; Benjamin's status is not mentioned here, and the eldest brother's vow (12:80) is not yet resolved. Verse 93's second command — bring me your family, all together — is still outstanding, and verses 99 and 100 will report its fulfilment. The chapter therefore places the restoration of Jacob's sight before the reunion of the family, so that the father sees his sons arrive rather than being told about them. Verse 83's hope concerned all three; verse 96 confirms one of them by an object, and leaves the rest to the journey that follows. The partial confirmation keeps the sequence moving. Jacob's claim has been vindicated as to Joseph's existence but not yet as to the reunion, and the chapter lets him stand in that position for several more verses. A man whose knowledge has just been proved is still waiting for the thing he knew about, and the waiting is reported without comment or consolation.

**The Verse Completes the Chapter's Account of Knowledge**

Three people in this story have known more than those around them, and the chapter tracks each. Joseph knew his brothers from the moment they entered (12:58) and kept the knowledge for thirty-two verses. Benjamin was told privately and kept it for eleven (12:69). Jacob claimed knowledge from God while blind, grieving and dismissed (12:86), and verse 96 is his confirmation. The three cases differ in kind: Joseph's knowledge came from sight, Benjamin's from a disclosure, Jacob's from a source the text never names. What they share is that each was held against the judgement of the people nearby, and each was verified by an event rather than by an argument. Verse 96 is the last of the three verifications and the only one that restores something physical.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:97

> They begged, “O our father! Pray for the forgiveness of our sins. We have certainly been sinful.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Second Confession, Addressed to the Other Victim**

The brothers have already admitted their fault. In Egypt, immediately after the disclosure, they told Joseph that God had preferred him over them and that they had certainly been sinful (12:91), and they were forgiven in the next verse (12:92). Verse 97 repeats the admission to a different person, in almost the same words, and asks for something different: not forgiveness from the injured party but intercession with God. The chapter requires two confessions because the crime had two victims. What was done to Joseph was done to a brother; what was done to Jacob was done to a father who was deceived for decades and whose sight failed from the grief. The second confession completes the moral accounting. An offence against Joseph has already been named, admitted and forgiven by Joseph himself; the offence against the father has not, and it is the offence that produced the blindness healed in the previous verse. The brothers address the injury whose consequences they can still see, in the person whose eyes have just been restored.

**They Ask Their Father to Pray**

The request is specific in form. *Istaghfir lanā* — seek forgiveness for us. They do not ask Jacob to forgive them, although the deception was directed at him. They ask him to pray, which transfers the matter to God and places their father in the position of intercessor rather than judge. The distinction is precise and it reflects the chapter's understanding of offences. An injury between people can be waived by the person injured, and Joseph waived his (12:92). A sin against God cannot be waived by anyone; it can only be asked about, and the asking is properly done through someone whose standing with God is acknowledged. The brothers acknowledge theirs in the form of the request. The intercessory form identifies where they believe the remaining problem lies. Having been forgiven by Joseph in Egypt, they do not ask their father to forgive them; they ask him to approach God on their behalf. The distinction tells the reader that they understand the offence as having a dimension Joseph's amnesty did not close, and the following verse confirms that they are right.

**The Conversation Happens After the Return Home**

The commentators record that this exchange took place after the brothers came back from Egypt, which means the request is made in Canaan and not in the granary. The timing matters for the scene. Jacob has recovered his sight (12:96), has reminded his children of the knowledge he claimed (12:86), and is now facing the sons who deceived him decades earlier about the son whose shirt has just healed him. The confession arrives in that room. The chapter gives it a verse of its own and gives the answer another, which is the allocation it reserves for statements of weight. The allocation separates the request from its occasion. The brothers do not ask for intercession at the moment of their confession in Egypt, in front of Joseph, while the shame is fresh; they ask later, in Canaan, after everything has been reported to their father. The chapter gives the request its own space so that it reads as a settled intention rather than as a reaction.

**Why Not Joseph, Who Has Already Forgiven**

Joseph's amnesty in verse 92 removed the reproach that belonged to him and included a prayer: may God forgive you. The brothers nevertheless ask their father to pray as well. Nothing in the chapter suggests that Joseph's prayer was considered insufficient or that his forgiveness was incomplete. What the second request acknowledges is that the deception was a separate act with a separate victim, and that a person who was told his son had been eaten by a wolf, who wept until his eyes failed, and who was called deluded for hoping otherwise, is owed an admission of his own. The chapter treats the two offences as distinct even though they arose from a single afternoon at a well.

**The Wording Echoes Verse 91 Almost Exactly**

The confession is the same one, spoken to a different person. In verse 91 the brothers said they had certainly been sinful; in verse 97 they say we have certainly been sinful. The repetition is not careless. It records that the admission was made twice, in two countries, to two victims, and that the second was not weakened by the first having been accepted. A confession that has been forgiven is easy to repeat; the chapter does not make it easy. The brothers ask their father for prayer at a moment when he has just been vindicated against them and has every reason to withhold it, and the wording they use is the wording they used to the brother who had already pardoned them.

**No Explanation, No Mitigation, No Excuse**

The verse is short and it contains nothing but the request and the admission. The brothers do not explain why they acted, do not describe their youth or their jealousy, do not claim that their father's preference for Joseph provoked them (12:8), and do not repeat the mitigation Joseph himself supplied in verse 89 — that they acted in ignorance. They state the fact and ask for prayer. The chapter's economy here is a moral judgement as much as a narrative one. A confession that explains itself is partly a defence; a confession that does not is what verse 97 contains, and verse 98 shows it accepted. That distinction is what verse 98 answers. A request for intercession supported by circumstances would invite an assessment of the circumstances; a request supported only by an admission invites mercy, and mercy is what Jacob promises. The chapter builds the exchange out of two halves — a confession containing nothing but itself, and a reply addressed to the One who forgives.

**Jacob's Answer Comes in the Following Verse**

The chapter separates the request from the response, giving each its own verse. Nothing in verse 97 predicts whether the prayer will be granted, promised or refused. Jacob has reason to refuse: he rejected their first report outright (12:18), doubted their second (12:83), was rebuked by them for his grief (12:85) and called deluded for his hope (12:95). The reader who has followed the chapter knows what verse 98 will say, and knows it because Jacob has already been described in verse 64 as a man who places his sons in God's keeping rather than holding them to account. The verse ends on the request, and the pause before the answer is the chapter's. The pause is the only hesitation the chapter allows him. Jacob has been described as patient, as knowing and as refusing to despair, and here he is asked by his sons to intercede for them; the request is recorded and nothing else is, with the answer arriving in full in the verse that follows. The reader is shown a father taking the request seriously enough to defer it.

**The Confession Arrives After the Good News, Not Before**

Nothing obliged the brothers to make this request. Joseph has forgiven them, their father's sight has been restored, the family is about to migrate to Egypt, and the famine is still running. The admission in verse 97 is not required by any of those facts and is not prompted by anyone in the narrative. The chapter places it here, after the healing and before the reunion, which means it arrives at the moment when the brothers have least need of it and most capacity to make it. A confession extracted under pressure is a negotiation; a confession offered after the pressure has been removed is what verse 97 records. Verse 98 answers it, and the two verses together close the moral account the chapter opened in verse 8.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:98

> He said, “I will pray to my Lord for your forgiveness. He ˹alone˺ is indeed the All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Promise of Intercession**

Jacob agrees, and the agreement is stated in the future tense: I will seek forgiveness for you from my Lord. The verse records a commitment to pray rather than a prayer performed on the spot, and the distinction has been discussed by the commentators at length. What is promised is an act of intercession directed to God, made by a father to sons who deceived him about the death of a child and whose deception cost him his sight. The chapter gives the promise in a single clause and attaches no condition to it — no requirement of restitution, no probation, no demand that the confession be repeated or made public. The absence of conditions distinguishes this from a negotiated peace. Jacob asks his sons to return nothing, to make no statement and to demonstrate no change over time; he says only that he will ask his Lord to forgive them. The chapter reports the promise in a clause and attaches nothing to it, which is how it treats forgiveness everywhere else in the sūrah.

**The Delay Recorded in the Tradition**

The commentators report that Jacob deferred his prayer for a period of time, and they offer a reason: he wished to establish whether the brothers were sincere and truthful in what they had asked. The report is not in the Qur'anic text, and the verse as it stands contains only a future tense. The tradition's interest in the delay reflects a genuine question about the scene — why a father would promise rather than perform — and the answer given is a pastoral one rather than a punitive one: verification, not revenge. The Qur'an's wording is compatible with the report and does not require it, and the chapter's own emphasis falls on the promise and on the two divine names that close it.

**"My Lord"**

The phrase is Jacob's habitual address and it appears at the moments of his greatest strain. He complained of his anguish and sorrow only to God (12:86); he described God as the best of keepers when refusing to trust his sons with Benjamin (12:64); he declared beautiful patience twice after losing a child (12:18, 12:83). The intercession promised here is made to the same Lord, in the same terms, by the same man. The chapter's portrait of Jacob is built from these short addresses, and the consistency is the point: the person who complained only to God is the person who prays for the people who caused the complaint. That continuity is the verse's quiet argument. The same address appears where Jacob redirects his grief and here, where he undertakes to intercede for the people who caused it. The chapter places both uses of the phrase in his mouth and lets the reader see that a complaint carried to God and a prayer offered for an offender belong to a single practice rather than to two temperaments.

**"The All-Forgiving, the Most Merciful"**

The two names close the verse, and their pairing is one the chapter has already used. Joseph ended his statement about the soul that commands evil with the same two names: *Surely my Lord is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful* (12:53). Father and son both conclude a difficult speech with an assertion about God's capacity to forgive, and in both cases the assertion concerns offences they did not commit. The names are not a formula appended to a promise. They are the ground of the promise: a father can undertake to pray for the forgiveness of a deception because the One being prayed to is defined by forgiving. The two names are chosen for the situation rather than for their splendour. A deception sustained across decades requires a forgiveness that covers a long offence, and a household whose father was blinded by grief requires a mercy reaching the consequences as well as the act. Jacob names precisely the attributes the request depends on, and names them as the ground of his promise rather than as a hope.

**Both Injured Parties Have Now Prayed**

Joseph said: may God forgive you, and He is the Most Merciful of the merciful (12:92). Jacob says: I will pray to my Lord for your forgiveness, and He is the All-Forgiving, Most Merciful. The two prayers cover the two halves of the offence and are recorded in adjacent scenes. The chapter's account of reconciliation is therefore complete before the family is physically reunited. Verse 99 reports the arrival in Egypt and verse 100 the prostration, but by the end of verse 98 nothing remains outstanding between the brothers and the people they wronged. The reunion that follows is the confirmation of a settlement already reached, not its occasion. The settlement is therefore complete before the family meets. Joseph has forgiven, the brothers have confessed twice, and Jacob has undertaken to ask God's forgiveness for them; nothing is outstanding when the household arrives in Egypt. The reunion that follows is not the moment the family is reconciled but the moment a reconciliation already achieved becomes visible to everyone in it.

**The Verse Ends the Family's Moral Arc**

From verse 8 to verse 98 the chapter has traced a single movement: envy, plot, disposal, false report, decades of grief, a second loss, a search, a disclosure, an admission, a forgiveness, a request and a promise of intercession. Verse 98 is the last of these, and after it the narrative has no further moral business to conduct. What remains is reunion, recognition of a dream, a migration and a prayer. The sūrah closes its ethical sequence here, in Canaan, in a sentence of twelve words, before the family has entered Egypt. The placement means that when the brothers appear in verse 100 they appear as forgiven men rather than as petitioners. That status is what the closing scenes require. Men still under accusation could not stand at a throne, prostrate, and hear a brother say there is no blame on you today; forgiven men can, and the chapter prepares the ending by settling the moral question three verses before it arrives. What remains after this verse is geography rather than ethics.

**Intercession Is Asked of a Father, Not of a King**

The brothers could have asked Joseph to pray for them. He had already done so in verse 92, and his standing with God is described throughout the chapter in terms of knowledge taught, mercy conferred and reward preserved. They ask their father instead, and the chapter records the choice without comment. What it reflects is the shape of the offence: the deception was directed at Jacob, the grief was his, and the intercession properly belongs to him. Joseph's role in the reconciliation was to waive his own injury and to explain the larger arrangement (12:100). Jacob's role is to pray. The two roles are not interchangeable, and the chapter assigns each to the person it belongs to.

**A Promise Made by the Man Who Was Deceived**

The verse is worth reading against verse 18, where Jacob answered the same sons with beautiful patience and the remark that God's help must be sought against what they described. He did not promise prayer then. The intervening eighty verses have produced a disclosure, a healing, a confession and a migration, and the promise arrives at the end of them rather than the beginning. What changed is not the offence, which was fully known to Jacob in verse 18, but the offenders. Men who lied with a bloodied shirt and men who ask their father to intercede for them are not the same men, and the chapter's decision to place the promise in verse 98 rather than verse 18 is its account of that difference. The prayer is given to people who have asked for it.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:99

> When they entered Joseph’s presence, he received his parents ˹graciously˺ and said, “Enter Egypt, Allah willing, in security.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Whole Family Arrives**

Verse 93's second command was to bring the family, all together. Verse 99 reports the arrival, and the chapter gives it a single verse. No journey is described, no route is named, and no account is taken of the migration itself beyond the fact of entry. The sūrah's economy at moments of fulfilment is consistent: predictions and instructions receive full treatment, and their realisation is stated in a clause. What matters here is not that a household travelled from Canaan to Egypt during a famine but that the household arrived intact, with the parents alive, the sight restored and the eleven sons present — the terms of the dream in verse 4 at last capable of being met. The capability is the verse's contribution. For ninety-five verses the dream has been a statement about an unseen future and a source of trouble; here the eleven sons and both parents are in one country at one time, and the arithmetic of the vision becomes possible for the first time. The chapter notes the arrival and leaves the fulfilment to the verse that follows.

**He Received His Parents**

The verb denotes taking someone in, giving them a place, drawing them close to oneself. It is the same verb the chapter used when Joseph drew Benjamin aside to disclose himself (12:69), and its use here for the parents marks the reunion as an act of shelter rather than of ceremony. The verse specifies the parents and does not mention the brothers, who have already been received, forgiven and provided for. The order is one of honour: Joseph's first act on the family's arrival is directed at his father and mother, and the greeting given to them precedes anything said to the party as a whole. Verse 100 will continue the sequence by raising them to the throne. The order carries the meaning. Joseph's first act on meeting the family he has not seen for decades is to bring his parents forward, and the honour conferred on them in the next verse follows that gesture rather than preceding it. What the chapter records is a son receiving his parents, with a state ceremony arranged around the reception rather than the other way about.

**"Enter Egypt in Security"**

The word denotes safety, freedom from fear — the condition the chapter has been describing the absence of since verse 8. Joseph's own entry into Egypt was as cargo, hidden in a well and sold for counted coins (12:19–20). His brothers' entries were as anxious purchasers during a famine, once accused of theft publicly and once threatened with exclusion (12:60, 12:70). Jacob's household has lived for decades under a false report and a famine. The invitation names the opposite of all of it in a single word, and it is addressed to the whole family at the moment of arrival rather than after a period of adjustment. The timing makes it a welcome rather than a reassurance. Security announced after a period of adjustment would be a report; announced at the gate it is a gift conferred on arrival, and it answers the particular insecurity this family has lived with — a son sold into this country, a son detained in it, and a household that twice travelled to it afraid of starving.

**The Qur'an Uses the Same Formula Elsewhere**

The combination of entry and security appears in two other contexts worth noting. The angels' greeting to the inhabitants of Paradise is an invitation to enter in peace and security (15:46). And the Qur'an records the fulfilment of a vision given to the Prophet ﷺ in the same terms: *Allah willing, you will surely enter the Sacred Mosque, in security* (48:27). Verse 99 stands inside that pattern — an entry promised, an entry granted, and the granting marked by the removal of fear. The parallel with 48:27 is particularly close, since both concern a vision disclosed in advance and fulfilled at a stated destination, and both attach the divine exception to the promise. The shared formula also imports a shared condition. Wherever security is promised in these terms it is promised by God and held subject to His will, so it cannot be treated as a possession. The family entering Egypt receives something belonging to the category of divine grant rather than political protection, which is how the chapter has treated every other good thing in the story.

**"If God Wills" Attaches to a Promise of Safety**

The exception is not a hedge about whether the family will enter Egypt; they are standing at the entrance as the words are spoken. It attaches to the security. Joseph is a ruler with authority over the country's stores and a personal position with the king (12:54), and he nevertheless qualifies the promise of safety with a reference to divine will. The chapter has shown him doing the same at every point where his own power is greatest: he declines to absolve himself from the condition of the soul (12:53), attributes his preservation to God's turning of a scheme away (12:34), and ascribes his establishment to mercy given according to a will (12:56). The exception here belongs to that pattern rather than to uncertainty.

**Security Is the Chapter's Final Gift**

The sūrah's movement can be described as a passage from fear to security. A boy is afraid of a dream his father warns him not to relate (12:5). He is thrown into a well by people who intend his death (12:9–10). He is sold, imprisoned on a false charge, and forgotten by the one man who owed him a message (12:42). His father fears for a second son, weeps until his eyes fail, and is called deluded for hoping (12:84–95). Verse 99 names the end of that sequence in a single word, and it names it as something given rather than achieved. Nobody in the family secured themselves; they were fed, forgiven, healed and received. The passive runs through the entire sūrah. Joseph was saved from the well, taught, released and elevated; Jacob was given patience, knowledge of his son and the return of his sight; the brothers were fed, spared and forgiven. What arrives at the gate of Egypt is the last item in a long list of things done for this family rather than by it.

**A Migration With Consequences Beyond the Chapter**

The entry of Jacob's household into Egypt is the point at which the sūrah's story becomes the background of a much longer one. The chapter does not mention what follows — the settlement, the generations, the later Pharaoh who did not know Joseph, the exodus. Its interest ends with the reunion and the prayer that follows it in verses 100 and 101. The reader is nevertheless aware that the invitation in verse 99 has a scope larger than the scene: a family enters a country in security, under the protection of one of its own, during a famine that the same person's foresight made survivable. What the chapter records as an arrival is elsewhere in the Qur'an the beginning of a long residence.

**Receiving Before Reuniting**

The verse's sequence is deliberate. Joseph receives his parents, and only then speaks the words of welcome. Nothing in the text reports an embrace, a greeting from the brothers, or a public announcement of the family's identity to the Egyptian court. The first act is shelter given to two elderly arrivals, and the first words are a promise of safety to the whole party. The chapter has been careful all along to describe Joseph's care in terms of acts rather than feelings — food loaded, money returned, a brother taken aside, a shirt sent — and verse 99 follows the pattern. The reunion the reader has waited ninety-nine verses for is reported as something done: he took them in, he gave them a place, he told them they were safe.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:100

> Then he raised his parents to the throne, and they all fell down in prostration to Joseph, who then said, “O my dear father! This is the interpretation of my old dream. My Lord has made it come true. He was truly kind to me when He freed me from prison, and brought you all from the desert after Satan had ignited rivalry between me and my siblings. Indeed my Lord is subtle in fulfilling what He wills. Surely He ˹alone˺ is the All-Knowing, All-Wise.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Raised to the Throne**

Joseph seats his parents on the throne. The commentators gloss the action as elevating them to a place of honour — the royal seat itself, according to one account — and the gesture is the chapter's clearest statement of what the elevation of verse 56 amounts to in practice. A man who has settled wherever he pleased in Egypt gives the highest seat in the room to his father and mother. The verse does not describe the throne's material or the setting, and it does not record any objection from the Egyptian court. What it records is an ordering of honour, performed in public, in which the ruler of the country's stores places two arrivals from the desert above himself. The publicity is the point. A minister who raised two arrivals from the desert to his own seat in front of his court was making a statement about where his authority came from, and the chapter records the statement without glossing it. Joseph honours his parents in the only register Egypt recognises, and he does so at the cost of his own precedence.

**The Prostration and Its Meaning**

They all fell down in prostration to Joseph. The commentators identify the party as Joseph's parents and his eleven brothers, corresponding to the sun, the moon and the eleven stars of the dream in verse 4. They also explain the gesture: prostration toward a person in that period was a form of greeting and honour, comparable to standing for someone out of respect or kissing the hand, and was not an act of worship. The distinction matters for the verse's theology and for its law. What is described is a ceremonial acknowledgment of a person God has elevated, performed by a family that has just been fed, healed and forgiven by him, and it is the physical fulfilment of a vision reported in the chapter's second narrative verse. The fulfilment also answers the warning that opened the story. Jacob told his son not to report the dream to his brothers lest they plot against him, and they plotted; ninety-six verses later the same brothers are prostrating, and the plotting is over. The verse closes the sūrah's longest thread with a gesture and says nothing whatever about the irony.

**"This Is the Interpretation of My Old Dream"**

Joseph supplies the interpretation himself, and the word he uses is *taʾwīl* — the same term his father used when warning him not to relate the vision (12:6), the term the narrator used for what God would teach him (12:21), the term both prisoners asked for (12:36), and the term the court declared itself unable to supply (12:44). The chapter's central technical word is used throughout for the meaning of a dream as an event that will occur. Here it reaches its final instance: the interpretation of verse 4 is not a statement but a scene, in which eleven brothers and two parents bow to the boy who dreamt. Joseph names the connection himself, which is the only time in the sūrah that an interpretation is identified by the person who received it.

**"He Was Kind to Me When He Brought Me Out of Prison"**

The summary of thirty years mentions the prison and omits the well. The commentators explain the omission: Joseph had already told his brothers that there was no blame on them that day (12:92), and to name the well now would be to reopen a matter he had closed. The choice of the prison as the point of deliverance is also accurate in another sense. It was the prison that produced the two dreams, the cupbearer's memory, the king's summons and the office. What God brought him out of, in the sequence that led to this throne, was the cell — and the verse attributes the kindness to that event rather than to the injury that preceded it. The choice of reference is striking and not accidental. Joseph's summary of God's kindness passes over the well, the sale and the accusation, and even the elevation to office, to settle on the moment of release. What he remembers is being taken out of a place where he had been forgotten, and the verse lets that memory stand as the representative instance of favour in his life.

**"After Satan Had Ignited Rivalry Between Me and My Siblings"**

The explanation of the brothers' conduct is given to a third party. Joseph does not say they plotted, envied, sold him or lied. He says Satan stirred up hostility between him and his brothers, and the phrasing includes himself in the relation — *between me and my brothers* — rather than placing the fault entirely on one side. The commentators describe the move as tantamount to making an excuse for them, consistent with the mitigation he offered in verse 89 when he said they acted in ignorance. The chapter's account of the crime is thus stated three times in three registers: as an act of the brothers' souls made to seem fair to them (12:18), as an act done in ignorance (12:89), and as an enmity stirred up by Satan (12:100).

**"My Lord Is Subtle in What He Wills"**

The word denotes subtlety, refinement, the reaching of an end by means that are not visible in the means. The commentators explain that the manner in which God carries out His will is subtle, and the verse is the chapter's own summary of its plot. A dream is disclosed, a plot is made, a well is chosen, a caravan passes, a market prices a child at twenty dirhams, a household buys him, a woman desires him, a prison receives him, two servants dream, a king dreams, a cupbearer forgets and remembers, a famine is forecast, a cup is planted, a law is supplied by the accused. None of these looks like the fulfilment of verse 4 while it is happening. The word *subtle* is Joseph's name for the arrangement.

**"The All-Knowing, the All-Wise"**

The verse closes with the two names, and they close the story in the same terms in which Jacob held it during its worst point. Verse 83 ended with the same pairing, spoken by a blind father who had just rejected his sons' report and hoped for the return of three children. Verse 100 ends with the pairing spoken by a son seated in front of that father, with the family assembled and the dream fulfilled. The chapter's last theological statement is therefore identical to its darkest one, and the identity is the argument: the names that made sense as hope in Canaan make sense as fact in Egypt, and nothing about them has changed except what the speaker can now see. That is the verse's final claim about the story. Every element of it — the dream, the well, the shirt, the prison, the famine, the cup — was already arranged when it happened, and the names invoked here describe the arrangement rather than a response to it. Joseph's recognition adds nothing to the events; it changes his description of them.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:101

> “My Lord! You have surely granted me authority and taught me the interpretation of dreams. ˹O˺ Originator of the heavens and the earth! You are my Guardian in this world and the Hereafter. Allow me to die as one who submits and join me with the righteous.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Chapter's Only Extended Prayer From Joseph**

Joseph prays several times in this sūrah, and the chapter quotes him briefly each time: a plea for the women's scheme to be turned away (12:33), a preference for prison over what he is being called to (12:33), a statement about the soul that commands evil (12:53). Verse 101 is different in kind. It is a sustained address with a structure — gifts acknowledged, God named, a request for the manner of his death, a request for his company afterwards. The chapter places it at the end of the narrative, after the prostration, after the interpretation of the dream, after the explanation of the brothers' conduct. Everything Joseph has been given is now in his hands, and the first thing he does with it is pray.

**"You Have Granted Me Authority"**

The word denotes sovereignty, dominion, rule — and the prayer qualifies it. Joseph has been given something of it, not all of it. The commentators explain the qualification by reference to his actual position: the highest standing in Egypt next to the king (12:54, 12:56). He controls the granaries, settles wherever he pleases, and holds a personal attachment to the ruler, and he describes all of it as a portion. The verse is the chapter's own assessment of worldly power, spoken by the person holding most of it. Nothing in the prayer claims the authority as earned, deserved or permanent; it is stated as a gift received, in the same clause as the second gift. The grammar of the clause carries that meaning. Authority is described as something granted, in the same sentence and the same construction as the second gift, so both are received on identical terms. Joseph does not present his office as the outcome of his competence, though the chapter has documented his competence at length; he presents it as one of two things given to him.

**"And Taught Me the Interpretation of Dreams"**

The second gift is named with the same verb the chapter has used from the beginning. Joseph's father told him that God would teach him the interpretation of dreams (12:6); the narrator reported that God established him in Egypt in order to teach him that interpretation (12:21); two prisoners asked him for it (12:36); a court declared itself unable to supply it (12:44). Verse 101 is the only place where Joseph himself states that he was taught. The prayer therefore confirms the chapter's central claim about his knowledge from inside his own mouth, at the end of the story, after every prediction he made has been verified by events. The verification is what makes the claim modest rather than proud. Every interpretation Joseph offered has been fulfilled: two prison dreams, a king's vision of fifteen years, a blind father's sight, and a reunion he predicted to his brother in confidence. He names the skill at the end of the record, after the reader has watched it work, and attributes it to the One who taught it.

**"Originator of the Heavens and the Earth"**

The address shifts from gifts to the Giver, and the name chosen is one of origination — the bringing into being of something that did not exist. The Qur'an opens another sūrah with the same title: *“All praise is for Allah, the Originator of the heavens and the earth”* (35:1). The choice is significant in context. Joseph has just watched a family bow to him in fulfilment of a vision given to him as a child, and he has just attributed the hostility that produced the delay to Satan. The name he addresses is the one that describes the beginning of everything rather than the governance of a part of it, and it puts the thirty years of the story into its proper scale. The scale qualifies everything else in the prayer. A man holding the granaries of Egypt addresses the One who originated the heavens and the earth, and the address reorders his achievement before he mentions it. The chapter sets the cosmic title first and the two gifts second, so that authority and knowledge are received inside a frame far larger than either.

**"My Guardian in This World and the Hereafter"**

The word denotes a protector, an ally, one who is close to a person and responsible for him. The Qur'an states the general form of the claim elsewhere: *Allah is the Guardian of the believers—He brings them out of darkness and into light* (2:257), and it describes those under that protection as people for whom there will be no fear and no grief (10:62). Joseph applies the term to himself across both abodes, which is a larger claim than protection in this world alone. He has been guarded in a well, in a market, in a household, in a prison and in a palace, and the prayer states that the same guardianship extends past death. The extension makes the prayer more than thanksgiving. Joseph has been guarded through a well, a slave market, a locked room and a prison, and the verse asks that the same relationship continue where no event can confirm it. He describes a guardianship tested across thirty years and requests it beyond the period in which it is capable of being tested.

**"Allow Me to Die as One Who Submits"**

The request concerns the manner of his death rather than its timing. The Qur'an makes the same request a general instruction to believers: *“Be mindful of Allah in the way He deserves, and do not die except in ˹a state of full˺ submission ˹to Him˺”* (3:102). Joseph's formulation is personal and it is placed at the moment of his greatest success, which gives it a particular force. A man who has just been bowed to by his family, who holds authority over a country and who has seen a childhood vision fulfilled asks not for more of any of it but that he should die in a state of submission. The chapter's ranking of the two worlds, stated in verse 57, is here enacted.

**"And Join Me With the Righteous"**

The final clause asks for company. Joseph does not ask for a rank, a reward, a station in Paradise or a reckoning made easy. He asks to be joined with the righteous, and the chapter has already told the reader who some of them are: his father, his forefathers Abraham and Isaac (12:6), and the categories described in verse 57 as those who believe and are mindful. The request is the prayer of a man who has been separated from his family for most of his life — sold by brothers, imprisoned among strangers, elevated among officials — and whose last stated wish is not to be alone. The verse ends where the story began, with the family, and moves past it. Moving past the family is the prayer's last movement. Everything Joseph has sought in this chapter has concerned his household — a brother, a father, a reunion, a settlement in Egypt — and the final request concerns a company that includes them but is not defined by them. The verse ends with the family present and the speaker looking beyond it in a single clause.

**The Prayer Placed at the Summit of the Story**

The chapter could have ended with verse 100: a dream interpreted, a family reunited, a famine survived, an explanation given. Verse 101 continues past the reunion into a prayer, and the placement is the sūrah's final judgement on everything that preceded it. Authority, knowledge, reconciliation, honour and a fulfilled vision are all in hand, and the response to all of them is a request to die submitting and to be joined with the righteous. Verse 57 stated that the reward of the Hereafter is better for those who believe and are mindful. Verse 101 shows a man who has received the reward of this world in full asking for the other one, which is the chapter's way of confirming its own ranking without arguing for it.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:102

> That is from the stories of the unseen which We reveal to you ˹O Prophet˺. You were not present when they ˹all˺ made up their minds, and when they plotted ˹against Joseph˺.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Story Ends, Address Begins**

Verse 101 closed with a prayer spoken by Joseph. Verse 102 turns away from the narrative entirely and addresses the person recounting it. The shift is abrupt and complete: no transition, no summary, no closing formula for the story. The chapter has told a continuous account for a hundred verses and now steps outside it to say where the account came from. The sūrah's structure is therefore framed — it opens with a statement about revelation (12:1–3) and closes its narrative with a statement about revelation — and the framing is what tells the reader that the story was never the chapter's only subject. The shift is abrupt and unsignposted. A hundred verses of continuous narrative close with a prayer, and the next verse turns to a reader who has been invisible until now. The chapter's structure claims that the narrative existed for the sake of the address, and the absence of any transition is what makes the claim without having to argue for it.

**"Accounts of the Unseen"**

The phrase denotes reports of matters absent from the recipient's experience: events he did not witness, in a country he has not visited, involving a family that lived centuries before him. The category is one the Qur'an uses repeatedly for its narratives, and its function is evidential. An account of the unseen is not a legend retold or a tradition collected; on the Qur'an's own claim it is information transmitted. The verse places the story of Joseph inside that category explicitly, which means the chapter is asking to be read as a report rather than as an edifying tale, and is asking the reader to consider how the report reached the person delivering it.

**"You Were Not Present When They Decided"**

The argument is stated as a fact about the recipient's absence. He was not with Joseph's brothers when they settled on their course and plotted. The commentators draw the conclusion directly: the knowledge contained in this chapter was given to the Prophet ﷺ without his having been present at the events, without his having learned it from human teachers, and without his having read it in a book. The verse does not elaborate the argument. It states the absence and leaves the inference — that a detailed account of a private council held centuries earlier cannot have been obtained by the person now reporting it. The argument targets a specific kind of knowledge. Not the general outline of a famous story, which many people possessed, but the content of a private conspiracy among eleven men in a desert, including what they said to one another. The verse asks where that came from and leaves no candidate except revelation, which is the chapter's claim about itself in a single sentence.

**A Council That Was Private**

The moment specified is not a public event. The brothers' deliberation is described in verses 8 to 10 as a conversation among them — a proposal to kill Joseph or cast him into a land where he would be forgotten, a counter-proposal to throw him into a well, an agreement. Nobody outside the group was present, and the chapter has already noted that the plan was concealed from the father, executed with a false report and supported by a bloodied shirt (12:16–18). Verse 102 selects that scene as the test case. A private conspiracy, hidden from the only adult in the family and never disclosed until decades later, is the kind of thing whose detailed narration requires a source other than observation.

**The Unlettered Prophet**

The Qur'an describes its recipient in terms that bear directly on this verse: the Messenger, the unlettered Prophet, whose description people find in the Torah and the Gospel (7:157). The description is relevant to the argument in verse 102 because it removes the ordinary explanations for detailed knowledge of the past. A person who does not read cannot have acquired the account from scripture; a person who was not present cannot have acquired it from the participants; a person who lived centuries later cannot have acquired it from witnesses. The verse states the absence and the Qur'an states elsewhere the literacy; together they are the chapter's claim about its own origin. The two statements work as one argument. A narrative containing details unavailable to any living source, delivered by a person without access to written accounts, leaves the audience with a question the chapter does not need to answer because it has already framed it. What is asserted is not the truth of the story but the impossibility of its having been assembled.

**Addressed to the Prophet and Through Him**

The address is in the second person singular and it names the recipient. The chapter has spoken in the narrator's voice throughout, and it now speaks directly to the man reciting it. The effect is to place the recipient inside the verse rather than outside it: he is not a transmitter mentioned in passing but the person to whom the accounts of the unseen are being revealed, at the moment of the revelation. The reader is thereby positioned as a third party overhearing a communication, which is the stance the sūrah's opening verses asked for when they described the Qur'an as a clear recitation sent down so that people might understand. The double address is what makes the story usable. A revelation given only to its recipient would have ended with him; a recitation sent down for people to understand is addressed to everyone who reads it, including whoever is holding this verse. The chapter tells its audience that the hundred verses behind them were meant for them, in the voice of a description rather than an appeal.

**Why This Verse Belongs to This Chapter**

Verse 102 could have closed any of the Qur'an's narrative sūrahs, and it closes this one. The reason is that no other chapter contains a comparable concentration of private, unwitnessed material: a family council, a concealed plot, a false report, a woman's locked room, a prison conversation, a search conducted in a calculated order, a cup planted in a bag, a disclosure made to one brother and withheld from nine. Almost nothing in the story was public at the time it happened. The chapter therefore has the strongest internal reason to make the argument in verse 102, and it makes it once, at the end, after the reader has encountered the private material a hundred times.

**Story and Claim Held Together**

The verse does not detach the narrative from its purpose. It says *that* — referring back to the account just completed — is from the accounts of the unseen. The demonstration and the claim occupy the same verse, which means the chapter expects the story it has told to function as the evidence for the claim it now makes. A reader unconvinced by the reasoning is being pointed back at the material: the detail, the coherence, the fulfilment of the dream, the fifteen-year agricultural calendar, the private council. The sūrah's method is not to argue for its origin in the abstract but to supply a narrative and then ask where it came from. The method distinguishes the chapter's argument from a doctrinal one. No premise is stated in advance, no authority is invoked at the opening, and no conclusion is drawn until the narrative has run its full length. The reader is given the evidence first and the question afterwards, which is the only order in which that question can be asked seriously.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:103

> And most people will not believe—no matter how keen you are—

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Limit Stated, Not a Failure Recorded**

The verse makes a prediction about an audience, and it is not a report about the past. Most people will not believe, however much the recipient desires it. Nothing in the wording blames the messenger, questions the message or suggests that the failure of belief indicates a defect in either. The limit is stated as a fact about people, and it is stated to a person whose effort the Qur'an elsewhere describes in terms of near self-destruction. The verse therefore does two things at once: it declares that the outcome is not in the messenger's control, and it implies that he has been trying as though it were. The implication is the comfort. A messenger told that most people will not believe is being told that the outcome was never the measure of his work, and the verse can only say that to someone who has been working as though it were. The chapter addresses the effort rather than the result, immediately after the most successful outcome in the whole narrative.

**"However Much You Desire"**

The conditional clause acknowledges an eagerness that the Qur'an addresses repeatedly. The Prophet ﷺ is described elsewhere as grieved and almost consumed by his people's refusal, and is told not to destroy himself over it. The phrase in this verse concedes the intensity of that concern without criticising it. Desire for people's guidance is not presented as a mistake; it is presented as insufficient. The distinction is important for how the verse functions. A reader who takes the chapter's story seriously has just watched a prophet succeed in saving a country, reuniting a family and forgiving his brothers — and the verse immediately states that most people will not believe anyway. The desire is not presented as misplaced. Joseph wanted his family restored for twenty years and worked towards it through a famine, a search and a detention; the verse honours that wanting by addressing it directly and then separating it from the outcome. What a messenger desires for people and what happens to them are two different things, and the chapter insists on the difference after demonstrating both.

**Guidance Is Not the Messenger's to Give**

The Qur'an states the principle explicitly elsewhere: *You surely cannot guide whoever you like ˹O Prophet˺, but it is Allah Who guides whoever He wills, and He knows best who are ˹fit to be˺ guided* (28:56). Verse 103 is the narrative application of that ruling. The chapter has shown a prophet with every advantage — divine knowledge, a verified forecast, a country's gratitude, a family's confession, a public exoneration — and it now states that none of these produces belief in most of those who encounter them. The verse separates the messenger's task from the messenger's outcome, and it does so at the point in the chapter where the task has been most visibly successful. That placement keeps the statement safe from a cynical reading. Following a story of failure it would have sounded like an excuse; following a story of total success it can only be a description of how the success occurred. Joseph did not guide his brothers — he fed them, detained them, disclosed himself and forgave them — and the guidance arrived as something other than his doing.

**The Same Claim Runs Through the Qur'an**

The statement that most people will not believe appears in several places, and the repetition is deliberate. It is a claim about a proportion rather than about a person, and it functions as a standing correction to the expectation that clear evidence produces conviction. The chapter's own story supplies an instance in miniature: a torn shirt examined by a witness inside a household established Joseph's innocence (12:26–28), and the household imprisoned him anyway (12:35). A public confession before a king established the same thing (12:51), and the years already spent were not returned. Evidence and belief are not treated in this sūrah as cause and effect. The pattern continues past the story: the brothers saw a cup produced from a bag they had watched loaded, and their first response was to slander a brother they believed dead (12:77).

**Why This Follows a Story of Success**

The placement of verse 103 is the point. Verses 1 to 101 have described a prophetic life that ends in complete vindication: the dream fulfilled, the family reconciled, the country fed, the injury forgiven, the prayer answered in advance. Verse 103 states that most people will not believe. The two statements are held together without reconciliation, and the effect is to detach the value of the story from its outcome. Joseph's success did not depend on general belief, and the messenger receiving this account is being told that his own will not either. The chapter is describing what prophetic work consists of when the numbers are against it. The placement refuses the reader an easy inference. Joseph's story ends with a family saved, a country fed and a dream fulfilled, and the verse that follows says most people will not believe however much the messenger desires it. The chapter will not let a successful narrative become a promise about success, and it corrects the inference in the same breath as it completes the story.

**The Verse Comforts and Restrains in the Same Sentence**

Two effects run together. The comfort is that failure to persuade is not failure, since most people will not believe regardless of the effort expended. The restraint is that the effort must nevertheless continue, since the desire itself is acknowledged rather than rebuked. A reader looking for permission to stop will not find it; a reader looking for a guarantee of results will not find that either. The verse removes the guarantee and keeps the obligation, which is the same structure that governs Jacob's precaution in verse 67 and Joseph's plan in verse 55 — do what is required, and leave the outcome with God. That is the chapter's own practice stated as a rule. Jacob entered his sons by separate gates and declared that it would avail nothing against God's decree; Joseph built a storage programme and named God as the source of his knowledge. Both men acted fully and claimed nothing from the outcome, and this verse describes the pattern they were each inside without knowing it.

**What the Verse Is Not Saying**

Three misreadings are excluded by the wording. The verse does not say belief is impossible, only that most will not arrive at it; the quantifier leaves the remainder open. It does not say the messenger's effort is wasted, since the effort is described as keen and is not criticised. And it does not say the message is deficient, since verse 104 immediately states that no reward is asked for it and that it functions as a reminder addressed to the whole world. What remains is a claim about an audience and nothing more. The chapter has already illustrated the claim in narrative form: a household that examined a torn shirt and learned the truth imprisoned the man it cleared, and a court that heard a public confession did not return the years the confession showed had been unjustly taken.

**"Most People" Leaves Room for the Rest**

The quantifier is not universal. Most will not believe; the verse does not say all, and the chapter itself is full of the exceptions. A prisoner believed enough to carry a message, eventually (12:45). A king recognised competence and trusted it (12:54). A woman confessed before her sovereign (12:51). Ten brothers admitted their fault twice (12:91, 12:97). A father knew from God what his household did not (12:86). The verse's claim about proportions is compatible with a story in which individuals are repeatedly moved by evidence they cannot dismiss, and the chapter keeps both facts in view without reconciling them. The exceptions are not marginal either: they include the ruler of the country, the accuser who prosecuted Joseph, and the entire family that injured him.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:104

> even though you are not asking them for a reward for this ˹Quran˺. It is only a reminder to the whole world.

**Expanded Commentary**

**No Payment Is Requested**

The verse completes the sentence begun in verse 103, and the completion supplies an argument. Most people will not believe, however keen the messenger is — even though he asks them for nothing in return. The Qur'an states the same claim in the Prophet's own voice elsewhere, listing the prophets who were rightly guided and instructing him to follow their guidance: *“I ask no reward of you for this ˹Quran˺—it is a reminder to the whole world”* (6:90). The formulation is therefore not local to this chapter. It belongs to the Qur'an's standing account of itself: a message delivered without charge, by a person with no financial interest in its acceptance. The absence of charge is also a claim about the messenger's position. A man who earns nothing from belief has no incentive to manufacture it, and the verse puts that fact in his own mouth as a disclaimer. The chapter offers it immediately after a story in which its hero was sold for money and later distributed grain for nothing.

**The Argument From Disinterest**

The logic is worth stating plainly. A person who profits from persuasion has a motive to persuade, and his arguments must be discounted accordingly. A person who asks for nothing has no such motive, and what he says must be evaluated on its own terms. Verse 104 places that argument immediately after a hundred verses of narrative and immediately after the claim that most people will not believe. The chapter is answering an objection before it is fully formed: the messenger is not enriching himself, not seeking office through the message, and not charging for access to it. What remains, once those explanations are removed, is the content. The argument also runs the other way. A person who gains nothing from being believed has no reason to invent an account that cannot be checked, which is the claim verse 111 will make explicitly.

**"A Reminder to the Whole World"**

The phrase describes the message's scope and its function in a single clause, and the Qur'an uses it of itself elsewhere: *Surely this ˹Quran˺ is only a reminder to the whole world* (81:27). The noun denotes a reminder, a mention, something that calls to mind what is already known rather than introducing a wholly new object. The scope is universal — not a tribe, not a city, not a generation. The combination is significant: a message addressed to everyone, requiring nothing from anyone, functioning by recall rather than by compulsion. Verse 104 states the Qur'an's own self-description at the end of its longest continuous narrative. The self-description is placed where it can be checked. A reader arriving here has just completed a story containing famine economics, prison procedure, family conflict and a fulfilled dream, and is told that all of it functions as a reminder rather than as an account. The claim is made after the material exists to test it against, not before.

**Reminder as the Chapter's Own Method**

The word describes what the preceding hundred verses have been doing. The sūrah does not argue for God's existence, establish a legal code, or refute a named opponent. It tells a story in which a series of propositions are enacted rather than asserted: that concealed planning does not reach its destination (12:52), that mercy is given according to a will (12:56), that the soul commands evil unless mercy intervenes (12:53), that the reward of the mindful and patient is not lost (12:90). A reader who accepts none of these as doctrines can still recognise them in the events. That is what a reminder requires — not assent to a premise but recognition of a pattern, and the chapter supplies the pattern in narrative form precisely because recognition works differently from argument.

**What a Reminder Does and Does Not Do**

The category explains verse 103. A reminder can be refused without contradiction, since it does not compel assent; it presents what is already available to be recognised. That is why eagerness does not produce belief and why the absence of a fee does not secure acceptance. The chapter's own story illustrates the mechanism repeatedly: a torn shirt reminds a household of a fact it can see (12:26–28), a confession reminds a court of a fact it has heard (12:51), a question reminds ten brothers of a crime they committed (12:89). In each case the reminder supplies nothing new. It re-presents what the hearers already possess, and the hearing is the variable. That is why the chapter's argument is narrative rather than demonstrative. A proof addresses someone who lacks information; a reminder addresses someone who has it and is not attending. The hundred verses behind this one supply no new premise and are not intended to: they are a story arranged so that what the hearer already knows becomes unavoidable while he reads it.

**Contrast With the Chapter's Only Sale**

The sūrah contains one transaction in which a payment is made for a person. Joseph is sold for a few counted dirhams by men who set no store by him (12:20), and the chapter uses the smallness of the price to measure the smallness of their regard. Verse 104 states that no price is asked for the message. The contrast is not drawn by the text and does not need to be: a chapter whose central injury was a sale ends by declaring that what is being offered to the world is offered free. The same money is later returned to the brothers unasked (12:62), and the pattern of gratuitous giving runs from that act to this verse. The pattern gives the disclaimer its weight. Joseph was priced at twenty dirhams and ended up distributing grain without charge to the family that priced him; the message he is now associated with asks for nothing either. The chapter has spent a hundred verses describing gifts that nobody paid for, and this verse describes the message in the same terms.

**Two Verses Forming One Sentence**

Verses 103 and 104 should be read together, since the second completes the first grammatically. The combined statement is that most people will not believe, however keen the messenger is, even though he asks them for no reward, and the message is nothing but a reminder to the whole world. The sentence contains four elements: an audience, an effort, a price and a function. Three of them are favourable to belief — the effort is intense, the price is nil, the function is a reminder addressed to everyone. The fourth is not. The chapter states the imbalance without resolving it and moves on to the signs people pass by. The unresolved imbalance is the sūrah's account of the messenger's position: everything that could make belief easy has been supplied, and belief remains rare.

**The Claim Applies to the Story Just Told**

Verse 104 is not a general statement inserted after a narrative; it describes the narrative. The account of Joseph is presented as a reminder — something that recalls what hearers already know about envy, betrayal, patience, providence, forgiveness and the difference between this world and the next. Verse 111 will say the same thing in explicit terms: in their stories there is a lesson for people of reason. The chapter therefore claims for its own hundred verses exactly what it claims for the message as a whole, and it makes the claim without charge, to everyone, in a form that can be refused. The reader holding verse 104 has been told what the preceding narrative was for. Being told differs from being instructed. The verse lists no lessons from the story, ranks none of them and directs no application; it states the category the story belongs to and leaves the content where it was placed. What the reader has just finished is defined as a reminder for the worlds, and the definition is the last word on its purpose.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:105

> How many signs in the heavens and the earth do they pass by with indifference!

**Expanded Commentary**

**An Exclamation About What Is Overlooked**

The verse opens with a quantitative exclamation: how many. The construction does not ask a question and does not expect an answer. It registers an abundance — signs in great number, encountered constantly — and then states what happens to them. The chapter has just declared that most people will not believe however keen the messenger is, and that the message asks for nothing and functions as a reminder. Verse 105 supplies the diagnosis. The problem is not a shortage of evidence. It is that evidence is met with indifference, and the indifference is described physically: they pass by. The verb is ordinary and repeated, which is what makes the diagnosis severe — nothing dramatic happens at the moment of passing by, and that is the point.

**Two Locations Named**

The signs are in the heavens and in the earth, and the pairing covers the whole of a person's ordinary experience. Above: the sun, the moon, the stars, the alternation of night and day, the rain that ends a famine. Below: the crops, the soil, the animals, the ruins of previous peoples, the bodies people inhabit. The chapter's own narrative has depended on both categories. Joseph's forecast turned on rainfall and harvest (12:47–49); his vision concerned eleven stars, a sun and a moon (12:4); his deliverance from famine turned on grain stored in the earth. The verse names the same two domains and states that they are full of signs that are passed by. The pairing covers the whole of ordinary experience. Nothing in the two domains requires travel, education or revelation to encounter; the heavens are above every person and the earth beneath every person, and both are visible daily. The verse describes not rare phenomena missed through ignorance but constant ones missed through inattention, which is a different and more indictable failure.

**Turning Away From What Is Seen**

The commentators explain the verse in terms of reflection: people continuously encounter signs indicating the being of their Creator together with His wisdom, power and oneness, and they do not consider what the signs mean or take a lesson from them. The Qur'an puts the same point as a question elsewhere: *Who then does more wrong than those who deny Allah's revelations and turn away from them?* (6:157). The wrongdoing identified is not ignorance but avoidance — a person who has the evidence in front of him and declines to look at it. Verse 105 describes the avoidance as habitual: passing by is something people do repeatedly, without effort and without noticing. The habituality is what makes the diagnosis serious. A person who rejects a sign after examining it has performed an act; a person who walks past it has performed nothing, and the verse treats that nothing as the commoner and more dangerous condition. Most of the signs in this chapter's own story were missed by the people nearest them, including a shirt and a dream.

**The Historical Reading**

One line of commentary connects the verse specifically to past civilisations and the traces they leave behind. On that reading the signs in the earth include the ruins, the roads, the abandoned settlements and the recorded ends of peoples who were more powerful than those now walking among their remains. The Qur'an makes the same appeal repeatedly: travel in the land and see what became of those before. The reading gives the verse a concrete object and links it to verse 109, which asks whether the deniers have not travelled through the land to see the end of those destroyed before them. Signs in the earth, on this account, are archaeological as well as agricultural. The double sense fits the chapter's own method. The story of Joseph is itself a sign in the earth — an account of a real country, a real famine and a real family, preserved and reported — and verse 109 will ask whether people have travelled through the land and seen how earlier generations ended. The historical reading connects directly to the narrative the reader has just finished.

**Why the Verse Follows the Story**

The placement is deliberate and it changes how the story is to be read. Verse 102 claimed that the account of Joseph is among the reports of the unseen revealed to the Prophet ﷺ. Verses 103 and 104 stated that most people will not believe and that the message is a reminder requiring no payment. Verse 105 says that people pass by signs indifferently. The chapter is therefore presenting itself as a sign and asking whether it will be treated like the others. The narrative that occupied a hundred verses is not an illustration appended to a doctrine; on the chapter's own account it is one of the things being passed by. That placement turns the narrative into an instance of its own teaching. The reader who has just spent a hundred verses attending closely to a shirt, a cup and a dream has demonstrated the opposite of passing by; the verse then asks how most people behave toward the signs in the heavens and the earth. The story stands beside the diagnosis as its counter-example.

**The Chapter Itself Contains the Signs**

Nothing in verse 105 requires the reader to leave the sūrah to find examples. A boy's vision fulfilled decades later in a specific gesture by eleven brothers and two parents. A fifteen-year agricultural cycle predicted in detail, including a year that was not in the dream. A torn shirt that established innocence from its direction of tearing. A false accusation overturned by a public confession. A forgotten message that delayed a release for years and then produced a government. A cup planted in a bag under a law supplied by the accused. The chapter has narrated sign after sign, and verse 105 asks what happens to them. The question is not rhetorical for a reader who has just spent a hundred verses inside the material being described.

**Indifference as the Diagnosis**

The verse does not identify the obstacle to belief as stupidity, malice, insufficient evidence or bad argument. It identifies it as passing by. That diagnosis is more severe than the alternatives, because it locates the failure in attention rather than in capacity. A person who lacks evidence cannot be blamed for lacking it; a person who walks past evidence daily and does not look at it is described in different terms. The chapter's account of its own audience is therefore not that people are unconvinced but that they are inattentive, and the remedy implied is not more evidence but a pause. Verse 111 will name the people for whom the pause has already happened. Naming them is how the chapter closes the gap. Between this verse and the last one the difference is stated as a difference in hearing rather than in evidence: the same signs are available to everybody, and some people turn toward them. The final verse of the sūrah describes that turning as belief, and describes what it produces as guidance and mercy.

**The Verse Explains the Shape of the Whole Sūrah**

If inattention is the obstacle, then a narrative is the appropriate response, and this chapter is a narrative. A list of propositions can be declined without being heard; a story has to be followed in order to be refused. The sūrah's decision to spend a hundred verses on one family, one well, one prison and one granary is therefore a method chosen for a diagnosed condition. The reader is not asked to assent to anything until verse 102, and by then he has already spent time inside the material. Verse 105 explains why that arrangement was necessary: signs are passed by when they are encountered briefly, and the chapter's response is to make the encounter long enough that passing by is no longer available.

## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:106

> And most of them do not believe in Allah without associating others with Him ˹in worship˺.

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Belief That Is Not Exclusive**

The verse describes a condition that looks contradictory from the outside. These people believe in God — the fact is granted at the start of the sentence — and their belief is accompanied by association. They are not atheists and they are not indifferent to the divine; they hold a conviction about God that does not exclude other objects of worship alongside Him. The chapter states the combination without explanation, and the statement is more precise than a general charge of unbelief would be. It identifies a particular religious condition rather than a general absence: belief about God's existence and power, held together with practices that treat other beings as sharing in what belongs to Him alone. The verse is therefore not about people who reject God but about people whose acceptance of Him is not exclusive.

**The Occasion Reported for the Verse**

The commentators record that it was revealed concerning the idolaters of Makkah, who acknowledged God as creator and provider while worshipping other deities alongside Him.
